Title: Check Token: Real-Time Self-Verification and Precise Truncation in LLM Reasoning

  • Authors: Fei Ding, Yongkang Zhang, Yuhao Liao, Zijian Zeng, Huiming Yang
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18564
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18564
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18564]] Check Token: Real-Time Self-Verification and Precise Truncation in LLM Reasoning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18564)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: ECC memory embeds 8 parity bits for every 64 data bits and automatically detects and corrects errors on each read. The parity bits carry no data and only safeguard integrity, at ~12.5% overhead. Yet the reasoning chains of large language models lack such built-in self-verification: once an error occurs it propagates along the chain, and existing methods can only verify externally after generation completes. We propose the check token, establishing built-in self-verification for language model generation streams for the first time: a functional marker is added to the vocabulary, and the model triggers self-checking (analysis, localization, truncation, rewriting) by outputting it at any position. The check token carries no reasoning content (discarded after triggering) and only safeguards reasoning correctness, directly corresponding to the role of ECC parity bits, also at only ~13% overhead. Speculative forking further eliminates false-positive latency, and segmented reward makes trigger timing end-to-end learnable. Experiments (Qwen3-32B / Qwen3-Next) show that the check token achieves +10.8 pp improvement on HMMT25 at 1.13x overhead, with token efficiency 88x that of Best-of-8, and the precise truncation advantage monotonically increases with chain length.

Title: "I Am Not a Kiddo!": Quantifying Epistemic Infantilization in Large Language Models via Multi-Metric Bias Analysis

  • Authors: Aditya Upadhyay, Arpan Verma, Chandan Sah, Dev Utkarsh, Harshit Gupta
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.CY
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18583
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18583
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18583]] "I Am Not a Kiddo!": Quantifying Epistemic Infantilization in Large Language Models via Multi-Metric Bias Analysis(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18583)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for tutoring and professional knowledge retrieval, yet safety evaluations still focus mainly on overt harms such as toxicity while overlooking benevolent failure modes. We study epistemic infantilization, defined as an identity-conditioned reduction in technical depth that appears when disability disclosure precedes an expert-level query. To measure this effect, we construct a counterfactual dataset of matched question-answer pairs and compare model responses across three settings: no identity context, disability disclosed in prior conversational context, and disability disclosed via a short query prefix. We introduce two composite metrics, the Infantilization Bias Index (IBI) and the Epistemic Accessibility Response Score (EARS), which capture retention of mathematical and formal content, domain terminology, reasoning coverage, and tone signals such as simplification markers and unsolicited offers to adjust the level. Across five open-weight model families, we find consistent evidence that disability disclosure reduces epistemic access by replacing formal explanations with higher-level summaries, with Physics and Mathematics most affected. Using question-clustered inference that respects the repeated-measures structure of the data, the effect remains reliable after accounting for correlated observations. These findings suggest an inclusion tax, where disabled users must request rigor more explicitly than non-disabled users. We conclude by arguing that accessibility should mean format adaptation rather than content simplification.

Title: Read, Route, Patch: Robust Test-Time Evolution for LLM Agents

  • Authors: Kaijie Chen, Liang Yan, Yilan Wei, Jiachen Luo, Zhihao Dou, Jiashu Qu, Haoran Liu, Junli Wang
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18585
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18585
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18585]] Read, Route, Patch: Robust Test-Time Evolution for LLM Agents(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18585)
  • Keywords: robust, large language model
  • Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM)-based agents can improve long- horizon interactive decision-making through test-time evolu- tion, yet existing methods face three information bottlenecks: memory retrieval overemphasizes textual similarity, candi- date harnesses are often behaviorally redundant, and whole- harness rewriting may overwrite useful evidence or introduce cross-component inconsistency. We introduce Trident, which improves experience utilization, behavioral exploration, and harness evolution through boundary-aware memory reading, diversity-aware candidate routing, and order-aware insertion- based updating, respectively. We evaluate Trident on five benchmarks spanning text games, web interaction, and cod- ing tasks. Across qwen-32B, mistral-small-3.2-24B, and gpt- oss-20B, Trident achieves the best average performance and consistently outperforms non-learning, memory-based, reflection-based, and automated prompt optimization base- lines. Compared with the strongest baseline, APEX, Trident yields relative average gains of 30.7%, 30.8%, and 38.3% on the three backbones, respectively. Ablation and sensitivity stud- ies further show that the three designs provide complementary gains and remain robust to parameter variations.

Title: Human vs Machine Translation Detection: A Cross-Model, Cross-Domain, and Low-Resource Analysis

  • Authors: Yabsera Yemanberhan, Prasenjit Mitra, Roald Eiselen
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18586
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18586
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18586]] Human vs Machine Translation Detection: A Cross-Model, Cross-Domain, and Low-Resource Analysis(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18586)
  • Keywords: robust, large language model
  • Abstract: The growing reliance on web-crawled corpora for training large language models introduces a critical challenge: machine-translated (MT) content is often treated as human translation (HT), potentially degrading data quality and propagating synthetic artifacts, especially in low-resource settings. In this work, we study MT-HT detectability across high-resource (English, Spanish) and low-resource (Swahili, Afrikaans) languages using both decoder-only LLMs in zero-shot and few-shot settings, and fine-tuned multilingual encoders trained at the sentence level and on token-chunked inputs. We find that decoder-only models provide limited discrimination overall and exhibit a consistent class-asymmetric failure mode: they recognize human-written text reliably (high HT recall) but rarely flag fluent machine output (low MT recall). Encoder-based classifiers achieve high accuracy when evaluated on data generated by the same MT systems and translators used during training in a legislative domain, but their MT recall drops sharply on unseen translators and on unseen domains such as low-resource conversational text and medical content. To address this limitation, we evaluate models on longer inputs by chunking text into segments of up to 300 tokens, which substantially improves generalization, suggesting that sentence-level signals are insufficient for robust detection. Finally, we analyze the relationship between translation quality and detectability using COMET and BLEURT, showing that lower-quality MT is more easily identified, while high-quality MT remains difficult to distinguish from HT. Our results highlight the importance of input granularity, translator generalization, and translation quality in building robust MT detection systems across domains and languages.

Title: Quantize, Qualify, Rerank: A Recipe for Compressing LLMs Without Losing Quality

  • Authors: Thamme Gowda
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18587
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18587

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Compressing large language models lowers serving cost, but a single automatic metric may miss quality loss. For the WMT26 Model Compression constrained track, we compress google/gemma-3-12b-it (24.4 GB in bf16) for three translation directions on one H100. Paired bootstrap analysis exposes metric disagreement on 4-bit quantization; guided by WMT24 human meta-evaluation, we use MetricX24-XXL as primary and CometKiwi-XXL as a cross-check. Removing the unused vision stack and non-task vocabulary and using int8 weights or fp8 activations causes no resolved loss, whereas int4 leaves a small residual. QLoRA healing overfits its calibration domain. A Cometoid best-of-4 reranker at $T=0.3$ improves both the 8-bit and 4-bit systems under WMT25 test set. On post-submission WMT26, both remain competitive with bf16 under reference-free metrics; the small difference between the reranked variants is treated as noise.

Title: Automating literature screening and knowledge synthesis with large language models

  • Authors: Zhanzhao Li, Qiyao He, Weiqiang Chen, Kengran Yang, Kai Gong
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cond-mat.mtrl-sci, cs.IR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18588
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18588
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18588]] Automating literature screening and knowledge synthesis with large language models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18588)
  • Keywords: extraction, large language model
  • Abstract: Scientific literature is expanding rapidly, yet much of its knowledge remains difficult to organize and reuse due to the substantial workload needed to navigate large, heterogeneous literature corpora. Here, we develop a generalizable large language model (LLM)-powered framework that automates literature screening, structured information extraction, and knowledge synthesis within a unified workflow. We benchmarked 11 state-of-the-art proprietary and open-weight LLMs across three materials science screening tasks differing in scope, corpus size, and class imbalance. Seven LLMs achieved balanced accuracies above 0.90 across all tasks, with the best-performing LLM reaching 0.96--0.98, demonstrating the potential of LLMs to accurately screen large, highly imbalanced scientific corpora. The framework also substantially reduces manual effort, screening 10,000 records within four minutes compared with approximately 83 person-hours for manual screening. Beyond literature screening, the framework extracts structured information from full-text articles, enabling content-level quantification of research landscapes that extends traditional bibliometric analysis based on publication metadata. This capability reveals methodological trends and underexplored directions, as shown for machine learning in concrete materials science. We further demonstrate its utility for information and data discovery by identifying publications containing silicate glass density data that are difficult to retrieve using conventional search strategies, which can support downstream hypothesis development, research design, and data-driven scientific discovery. Overall, these results demonstrate the broad applicability of LLM-powered literature analysis across scientific domains and its potential to transform large, unstructured literature corpora into structured, analyzable knowledge at scale.

Title: Where Language Models Fail Like Aphasic Patients and Where They Don't

  • Authors: Yong Yang, Roger Newman-Norlund, Xiang Guan, Saeed Ahmadi, Regan Willis, Nadra Salman, Kalil Warren, Sophie Arheix-Parras, Srihari Nelakuditi, Rutvik H. Desai, Leonardo Bonilha, Christopher Rorden, Julius Fridriksson
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18591
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18591
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18591]] Where Language Models Fail Like Aphasic Patients and Where They Don't(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18591)
  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: The computational architecture of human word production -- whether semantic-lexical retrieval and phonological encoding constitute dissociable processing stages -- has been debated for decades using hand-designed models with small vocabularies. We introduce computational architectural dissociation: using known architectural properties of vision-language models (VLMs) to derive falsifiable predictions about which patient error types should and should not align with model errors under perturbation. Because VLMs perform picture naming through learned semantic competition over full vocabularies but lack phonological encoding entirely, perturbation-induced errors should match patient patterns for semantic-stage categories but not phonological ones. Testing LLaVA 1.6 (Vicuna-13B) against 88 patients with aphasia across 175 Philadelphia Naming Test items (~37 million naming attempts, 50 perturbation seeds), we find that six of eight error categories show significant item-level alignment (Spearman $\rho$ = 0.32-0.50, all p < 0.001) while the two phonological categories -- formal errors and abstruse neologisms -- show no alignment ($\rho$ = 0.110 and 0.084, both n.s.), confirming the predicted dissociation. Spectral analysis of the output probabilities places each error type at a distinct position in a six-dimensional space, giving a mechanistic interpretability tool. The dissociation is layer-specific (peaking at L22, 55% depth), severity-independent, and stable across seeds (split-half r > 0.998). Weight perturbation of four additional 7B VLMs spanning the tested architectural combinations produced correct-rate alignment of comparable magnitude ($\rho$ = 0.21-0.27). Whether the per-category dissociation itself depends on scale or on attention design requires a comparison within a single inference implementation, which is reported separately.

Title: Endogenous Preferences as Vulnerability Signals: Discovering Universal Adversarial Triggers for LLM Utility Degradation

  • Authors: Qingyu Wu, Zeyu Feng, Yuzhe Luo, Hua Cheng
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18592
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18592
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18592]] Endogenous Preferences as Vulnerability Signals: Discovering Universal Adversarial Triggers for LLM Utility Degradation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18592)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Deployed large language models must remain useful when untrusted text enters their prompt context. Adversarial prompting and red-teaming methods typically define the failures they seek through task labels, harmful targets, judge feedback, or downstream utility scores. We ask whether a frozen model's clean behavior can reveal a reusable context vulnerability when its task-level form is unknown, and downstream task feedback is withheld. To study this question, EndoUT uses a Siamese-style design that compares the same frozen victim on clean and span-modified versions of each unlabeled instruction. Without a task-level target, the clean pass supplies the victim's preferred response-onset token as an instruction-conditioned reference; Endogenous Preference Disruption (EPD) measures how insertion weakens that preference. An auxiliary generator supplies bounded-span proposals; EPD guides its update and ranks the pool without access to downstream information. The highest-scoring victim-specific span is fixed before evaluation and reused unchanged across all held-out inputs. Across four instruction-tuned models and nine benchmarks, fixed triggers reduce benign-task utility by 40.2 percentage points on average, with negative mean change on every benchmark and greater average degradation than every evaluated baseline under the reported protocols. Matched protocol ablations show stronger transfer for source-side selection followed by fixed reuse than for null-context fixed reuse or per-input generation. Transfer recurs across proposers with proposal-dependent strength; output-level analyses identify victim-specific associations between trigger families and downstream failure patterns. Thus, clean behavior can expose context vulnerabilities that transfer across inputs yet manifest through structured, victim-specific patterns of utility degradation. The code and data will be released upon acceptance.

Title: Theoretical Foundations of Computational Islamic Jurisprudence (Fiqh)

  • Authors: Elnaserledinellah Mahmoud Abdelwahab
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LO
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18593
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18593
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18593]] Theoretical Foundations of Computational Islamic Jurisprudence (Fiqh)(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18593)
  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: This paper presents the theoretical foundations and a comprehensive formal system for Computational Islamic Jurisprudence (Fiqh). We begin by establishing six foundational principles that distinguish computational Fiqh from conventional natural language processing and general theological formalization. These principles define Islamic jurisprudence as a normative deductive science operating on empirically grounded facts, necessitating a dual inductive-deductive methodology, and requiring a logical foundation specifically engineered for Fiqh rather than adopted from existing formalisms. Based on these principles, we construct a three-layer stratified computational language, $\mathcal{F} = D \circ A \circ G$, consisting of: (1) an Arabic Morphological/Grammatical Grounding Engine that transforms fully vocalized Classical Arabic into language-independent Grounded Action Structures (GAS); (2) the Arabic Reified Action Calculus (ARAC) that normalizes these structures into canonical logical representations; and (3) a function-free Horn-clause Datalog engine for executable legal deduction. Furthermore, we introduce a bridging methodology (Algorithm $\Omega$) that dynamically generates a Fiqh-Categorized Attribute Grammar from inductively extracted Horn clauses via an Ontological Meta-Map ($\mathcal{M}_{onto}$), seamlessly connecting the inductive extraction of legal rules to their deductive application. The paper rigorously proves the system's semantic correctness, representational completeness, termination, and decidability. Crucially, by leveraging morphosyntactic inflection markers ($\delta$ modes) as structural constraints, we prove the strict unambiguity of the proposed grammar, achieving deterministic $O(n)$ linear parsing complexity. This work provides a mathematically verifiable, epistemologically grounded, and computationally tractable foundation for automated Islamic jurisprudence.

Title: Whose Side Are You On? Measuring Stance at Scale for Reddit Discourse Analysis During the Israel-Gaza War

  • Authors: Shahar Oded
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.SI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18594
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18594
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18594]] Whose Side Are You On? Measuring Stance at Scale for Reddit Discourse Analysis During the Israel-Gaza War(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18594)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large-scale discourse analysis often requires stance labels, yet for many high-salience political events such labels are unavailable at the comment level. We introduce and validate a comment-level stance measurement instrument that assigns Pro-Israel, Pro-Palestinian, or Undefined stance using Reddit comment text. The instrument is calibrated from a small, manually annotated gold-standard dataset and scaled through large language model-assisted labeling, achieving weighted-$F_{1}=0.88$ against held-out human annotations. To enable corpus-scale measurement, we operationalize the instrument as a lightweight classifier based on DistilBERT embeddings and an RBF SVM, which achieves weighted-$F_{1}=0.7935$ on the original gold-standard set. Applying this instrument, we measure the stance of approximately 2.7 million Reddit comments posted between 2023 and 2025 during the Israel-Gaza war. We demonstrate what this measurement enables by analyzing stance-conditioned asymmetries in sentiment, toxicity, and emotional framing across political affiliations. This work shows how validated, low-cost stance measurement can support large-scale descriptive analysis of polarized discourse, without relying on network structure or prior labeled corpora.

Title: Profiling Hallucinations in Frontier LLMs for Entity Linking to Medical Ontologies

This study presents a comprehensive analysis of hallucinations by frontier LLMs tasked with mapping clinical text to SNOMED CT. Through rigorous experimentation, we identify a critical reliability gap: LLMs hallucinate medical codes at a rate that currently renders them unsuitable for autonomous clinical coding applications. Paradoxically, constraining models to use ground-truth mention spans exacerbates, rather than mitigates, these hallucinations. Title: Between the Lines: Multi-turn Contextual Investment Advice Detection

  • Authors: Mingshu Li, Adam Boncz, Mate Toth, Justin Williams, Justin Chan, Dhagash Mehta
  • Subjects: cs.CL, q-fin.GN
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18596

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) increasingly power financial conversational assistants, detecting investment advice in natural language has become a critical regulatory and safety challenge. Unlike explicit single-turn recommendations, investment advice often emerges implicitly and progressively over multi-turn conversations, shaped by user intent, delivery style, and contextual buildup. We introduce the first comprehensive framework for detecting both sequential and contextual investment advice in dialogue settings. Central to our approach is a modular, top-down synthetic data generation pipeline that systematically spans a taxonomy of advisory trajectories using an axis-bucket-descriptor schema. Title: Skill-Latent Rewards: LLM-Guided Reward Formulation for Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning

  • Authors: Maryam Shoaeinaeini, Brent Harrison
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18597
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18597
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18597]] Skill-Latent Rewards: LLM-Guided Reward Formulation for Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18597)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Long-horizon reinforcement learning under sparse and delayed feedback remains an open challenge. Existing return-decomposition methods redistribute episodic rewards to ease credit assignment, but they operate on raw state--action transitions and disregard the subgoal structure that governs meaningful progress in complex tasks. We propose Skill-LR, a hierarchical reinforcement learning framework in which a vision--language model identifies the agent's active subgoal at each timestep and a large language model generates a matching latent reward formulation through a two-stage prompting process. By conditioning reward signals on the currently active subgoal, Skill-LR produces stage-specific credit that evolves with the hierarchical plan, reducing temporal variance and improving sample efficiency. On multi-stage MiniGrid and BabyAI benchmarks, including DoorKey and Unlock-to-Unlock, Skill-LR converges faster and more reliably than sparse environmental rewards and strong credit-assignment baselines such as RUDDER and LaRe, demonstrating that subgoal-aligned latent rewards offer a principled path toward stable, interpretable credit assignment in long-horizon tasks.

Title: Mechanistic Interpretability of Mambas Selective Gate in Financial Markets: A Leakage-Controlled Study

  • Authors: Dip Roy, Rajiv Misra, Sanjay Kumar Singh, Anisha Roy
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18599
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18599
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18599]] Mechanistic Interpretability of Mambas Selective Gate in Financial Markets: A Leakage-Controlled Study(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18599)
  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: Selectively gated state-space models (SSMs), notably Mamba, are increasingly used for financial time series, yet how they internally represent market regimes (bull, bear, sideways) remains opaque. This paper applies Mechanistic Interpretability (MI) to a six-layer Mamba trained on approximately twenty-one years of daily data for a point-in-time set of S&P 500 constituents plus macroeconomic and Fama-French factors, decomposing per-layer hidden states with Top-K Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs). Our central contribution is methodological and cautionary. A prior, uncontrolled configuration recovered market regimes from SAE features at F1 = 1.00. Under a controlled protocol that (i) removes the three input features that overlap the HMM label definition, (ii) disables the auxiliary regime-classification head, (iii) uses a point-in-time constituent universe, and (iv) evaluates against labels disjoint from the inputs, the same regime label is recovered at only F1 = 0.398, indistinguishable from a logistic baseline on raw inputs (0.435) and from an SAE trained on a randomly initialized, untrained Mamba (0.364). A 2x2 attribution grid shows the near-perfect result is an interaction of auxiliary supervision and label-defining inputs: neither factor alone exceeds F1 ~ 0.40. Effect sizes with block-bootstrap inference show the residual selective-gate (Delta t) regime effect is statistically detectable but economically negligible and uniform across layers rather than layer-specialized; the apparent polysemanticity gradient disappears under continuous, theory-grounded measures. We contribute a reproducible leakage-control protocol for interpretability evaluation of financial SSMs, a quantified demonstration that previously reported regime structure is a label-contamination artifact, and a modest economically grounded feature subset that genuinely survives the controls.

Title: ScopeBench: Evaluating Cultural Norm Scope Awareness in LLMs

  • Authors: Haojun Liang, Ziwei Zhao, Chenyu Li, Qiyue Zhao, Rongduomijia Mu, Yulan Shen, Yu Gu, Chen Zhang, Kairui Zhang, Yanbiao Ma, Wenxuan Wang
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18600
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18600
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18600]] ScopeBench: Evaluating Cultural Norm Scope Awareness in LLMs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18600)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Safety evaluation in large language models treats harm as culturally invariant, yet a model cannot correctly apply a safety norm without knowing whose norms bind. We introduce ScopeBench, a benchmark that treats cultural scope as a first-class safety dimension. It comprises 3,323 norms spanning 49 countries across nine cultural clusters, each annotated as Universal or Specific. We define three scope error types identically across four evaluation tasks: Scope Inflation, Scope Deflation, and Scope Displacement. Evaluating 21 frontier LLMs across seven model families, we surface three structural failures. First, the dominant error type inverts with prompt format: Scope Deflation reaches 70% in single-answer classification, while Scope Inflation reaches 33% when each candidate culture is judged independently. Second, scope-classification accuracy is empirically decoupled from behavioral safety: the six classifiers achieving at least 97% Task 1 accuracy still produce harmful responses to 40-42% of scope-violating statements. Third, no model is safe in interaction, with harmful-response rates ranging from 29% to 51% across the cohort, excluding one model that systematically refuses the task. We release all data, code, and model outputs to establish cultural scope as a distinct alignment axis.

Title: Jailbreak LLMs with Linguistic Style as a Hidden Attack Surface

  • Authors: Priyanshu Mohanty, Sarthakbrata Halder, Srikant Panda, Afshin Oroojlooy, Murari Mandal
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18601
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18601
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18601]] Jailbreak LLMs with Linguistic Style as a Hidden Attack Surface(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18601)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, robust
  • Abstract: We show that linguistic style is a systematic and previously overlooked attack surface for LLM safety. Across a diverse taxonomy of styles and multiple state-of-the-art models, stylistic rewrites of identical harmful goals produce order-of-magnitude differences in Attack Success Rate (ASR), with some styles achieving up to 80\% ASR while others remain below 6\%. To exploit this vulnerability, we introduce a lightweight single-pass attack framework that combines a style-conditioned generator with a BERT-based style selector, achieving performance competitive with substantially more expensive multi-turn optimization methods. To understand why style affects safety behavior, we conduct mechanistic analysis on LLaMA-3.1-8B and find that stylistic rewrites disrupt refusal representations while preserving recoverable harmful intent, suggesting a distributed failure mode rather than a single refusal mechanism. Motivated by this finding, we develop a style-aware Direct Preference Optimization defense that reduces ASR from 86\% to 19.5\% while largely preserving utility. Together, our results establish linguistic style as a critical factor in adversarial robustness and provide both mechanistic insight and a practical defense.

Title: Internal Verdicts Track Evidence: A Template-Controlled Neutrosophic Reading of Epistemic States in Large Language Models via the Jacobian Lens

  • Authors: Maikel Yelandi Leyva-Vazquez, Alexis Matheu Perez, Florentin Smarandache
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18602
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18602
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18602]] Internal Verdicts Track Evidence: A Template-Controlled Neutrosophic Reading of Epistemic States in Large Language Models via the Jacobian Lens(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18602)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: For twenty-five years, neutrosophic logic has assigned independent degrees of truth (T), indeterminacy (I), and falsity (F) to epistemic states that classical and fuzzy semantics cannot express. Previous applications measured these components on outputs: answers, judgments, expert evaluations. This paper reports, to our knowledge, the first neutrosophic reading of components inside the internal representations of a large language model. Using the recently released Jacobian lens on Qwen3.5-4B, we project intermediate-layer readouts onto lexicons of support, refutation, and hedging, obtaining layer-wise (T, I, F) profiles under conflicting evidence, first-person false belief, and factual control (n = 20 each). A first battery yielded three striking signatures: a surge of refutation mass under conflict, sustained indeterminacy under false belief, and an apparent "verdict collapse" before the output layer. A second, template-controlled battery showed that all three, as initially stated, were largely artifacts of prompt grammar: matched templates with agreeing evidence inflate the same lexicon masses, true beliefs elicit the same hedging, and the model verbalizes its verdict when allowed to generate. What survives the controls is stronger than what died: the neutrosophic verdict balance B = log10(F) - log10(T) tracks evidence polarity under identical templates (about 3 orders of magnitude of separation, Cohen's d = 2.3-2.5, p < 10^-6 in 9 of 9 layers; paired Wilcoxon: median difference +2.4 orders, 19/20 items, p = 3.8x10^-6) and residually discriminates false from true first-person beliefs. We argue that the independence of the neutrosophic components is what keeps the common-mode channel observable, making both the measurement and its self-correction possible, and we distill the two-battery design into a reusable protocol for auditing language model internals with lens-based readouts.

Title: Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Clinical Decision Support in Gait Analysis: A Proof-of-Concept

Title: Understanding Failures in LLM Reasoning by Learning Structured Representations of Chain-of-Thought

  • Authors: Tommaso Felice Banfi, Mubashara Akhtar, Jingwei Ni, Mrinmaya Sachan
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18611
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18611
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18611]] Understanding Failures in LLM Reasoning by Learning Structured Representations of Chain-of-Thought(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18611)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) is central to improving reasoning in large language models, yet existing evaluation methods focus on step-level signals and ignore the underlying structure of reasoning traces. This raises a key question: what aspects of reasoning quality (i.e., correctness, efficiency, and failure patterns) are encoded in the structure of a CoT trace? We introduce \textbf{CoT2Graph}, a framework that represents CoT traces as directed dependency graphs, making relationships between reasoning steps explicit and capturing patterns such as backtracking, branching, and subtask merging. We model these graphs with graph neural networks and use attribution-based explanations to identify structural patterns associated with reasoning quality. We further show that graph-based signals better predict whether a CoT leads to a correct answer, outperforming previous tree-based structural methods by up to 2.5\% and simple length-based baselines by 7.8\%, while also improving Best-of-$N$ selection. Overall, our results demonstrate that reasoning structure encodes rich signals about reasoning quality, motivating a shift from sequence-level evaluation to structural analysis.

Title: Bridging Semantics and Utilities: A Controllable and Scalable Market Simulation

  • Authors: Dejun Cao, Boyang Wang, Jiayuan Wei, Tingxi Zhang, Chengyuan Zhang, Ruixuan Yan, Yishen Chen, Bufan, Honghai Yu, Jiale Han
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18613
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18613
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18613]] Bridging Semantics and Utilities: A Controllable and Scalable Market Simulation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18613)
  • Keywords: generative, large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models(LLMs) have recently been used to construct generative agents for financial market simulation. Existing approaches typically prompt each agent separately with public market information and a private investor profile, and ask the model to directly generate trading actions. In this agent-wise design, the model repeatedly interprets information shared across the population, while individual behavior is controlled through textual descriptions of investor preferences and traits. This creates two structural difficulties: continuous traits such as risk sensitivity do not reliably induce ordered behavioral changes, and repeatedly processing the same public information causes inference costs to grow with the number of agents. We propose \textbf{SUM}, a Semantic-Utility Model that separates cohort-level semantic perception from agent-level utility-based decision-making. A language model interprets public market information for each investor type and produces semantic signals shared within the corresponding cohort. Individual agents then combine these signals with their private states, preferences, and constraints to generate orders through utility-based decisions. Experiments in an A-share limit-order-book market show that \textbf{SUM} provides more stable and monotonic behavioral control than prompt-based agents while substantially reducing repeated language-model inference as the population grows. Closed-loop simulations further reproduce directional market responses, volatility persistence, differentiated investment styles, and persistent wealth heterogeneity. These results establish \textbf{SUM} as a controllable, scalable, and semantically informed framework for financial market simulation.

Title: The Judge Is Not Impartial: Self-Preference in Medical LLM Evaluation

  • Authors: Zara Ansari, Shlok Natarajan, Vincent Yip, Cally Lin, Avi Udash, Mia Garvey, Aaron Fanous, Roxana Daneshjou
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18616
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18616
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18616]] The Judge Is Not Impartial: Self-Preference in Medical LLM Evaluation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18616)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to evaluate model-generated medical content, creating a potential circularity: an LLM judge may favor outputs generated by the same model independently of clinical quality. To characterize LLM judge self-preference in medical settings, we measured same-model affinity in single-turn specialist question answering and multi-turn standardized-patient encounters. We evaluated eight models, two from OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, and Qwen, on 620 real point of care questions from clinicians (Real-POCQi). The same eight models also generated and judged 200 standardized patient-clinician scenarios (MedSP1000) up to 8 visible turns. In total, our analysis spans 13,760 LLM judgments. We define three complementary measures of self-preference. Answer Rank Bias, our primary measure, compared the rank assigned to a fixed response by its own-model judge with its average rank from outside judges. Answer Score Bias made the analogous comparison using rubric scores. Own-Answer Win Rate measured how often a judge ranked its own model's response above a competitor. Higher values across all these measures indicate greater degrees of self-preference. In Real-POCQi, pooled Answer Rank Bias was $+1.22$ positions, and all eight judges favored their own answers. Self-preference persisted both with and without a grading rubric. Revealing generator identities modestly weakened self-preference by an average of $-0.094$ positions. In MedSP1000, pooled Answer Rank Bias ranged from $+0.53$ to $+0.62$ positions, with no clear difference by conversation length ($p=0.17$). LLM judges systematically favored responses generated by their own models across multiple formats. Clinical AI deployments should exercise increased vigilance including multiple evaluation measures and judge panels spanning differing model families when deploying LLM-as-a-judge systems.

  • Abstract: For multi-hop queries in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), it is crucial to accurately obtain evidence pieces. Domain-specific encoders achieve the strongest scope preservation, while instruction-tuned LLMs often show higher overgeneralization. Synthetic supervision helps but does not close the gap to real-world PubMed and Reddit performance.

  • Abstract: Fundamental to effective emotional support is the iterative interpersonal dynamic wherein a supporter intervention continuously shapes the psychological trajectory of the seeker. Empirical evaluations across standard benchmarks demonstrate that EmoTrace yields substantial improvements in both strategic planning and response generation. Crucially, rollouts spanning multiple turns reveal that these retained structural traces confer compounding benefits over extended dialogues, establishing a robust mechanism for sustained affective alignment in human-machine interaction.

Title: GRAFT: Cross-Backbone LoRA Reuse for Continual Language and Multimodal Learning

  • Authors: Ha Pham Thien Dinh, Ming Liu, Jiesi Guo, Matteo Vergani, Kewen Liao
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18638
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18638
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18638]] GRAFT: Cross-Backbone LoRA Reuse for Continual Language and Multimodal Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18638)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: In continual learning, parameter-efficient fine-tuning enables large language models to acquire new capabilities through lightweight adapters such as LoRA, yet the reuse of pretrained adapters is typically restricted to models sharing the same backbone. This limits the transfer of learned capabilities from language models to multimodal models. We propose GRAFT (Generalist Reuse of Adapters via Feature-space Transfer), a framework for cross-backbone LoRA reuse. GRAFT retrieves the most relevant pretrained LoRA adapter from a modality-aware library and maps it into the target backbone through a learned linear bridge between corresponding source and target feature spaces. We evaluate GRAFT on ten Super-NaturalInstructions text tasks, transferring Qwen2.5 LoRAs to Qwen2.5-VL, and five multimodal tasks, where Qwen2.5-VL LoRAs are directly reused. Compared with randomly initialized LoRA adapters, GRAFT improves performance on most evaluated tasks. These results show that GRAFT provides a simple and effective mechanism for reusing learned capabilities across language and multimodal models in continual learning.

Title: HIMX: mixed-format matrix multiplication based on customized HIFLOAT4 representations

  • Authors: Zhiyuan Zhang, Yanzhao Li, Hui Dong, Sifan Cheng, Jingwei Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18641
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18641
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18641]] HIMX: mixed-format matrix multiplication based on customized HIFLOAT4 representations(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18641)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: 4-bit weight-activation quantization (W4A4) is an important approach for efficient large language model (LLM) inference. Recent hierarchical formats such as HiFloat4 (HIF4) improve the representational range of 4-bit quantization through multi-level scaling. However, the same local mantissa representation in HIF4 may not be equally suitable for weights and activations, which exhibit distinct distribution characteristics. To address this limitation, we propose HiMX, a mixed-mantissa framework built upon the hierarchical scaling structure of HIF4. Our key observation is that the two HIF4-compatible mantissa representations, S1P2 and E2M1, exhibit complementary quantization behavior: the denser S1P2 format provides higher resolution for concentrated weight distributions, while the wider-spaced E2M1 format provides complementary quantization characteristics for activation tensors. HiMX enables operand-tailored mantissa representations while converting heterogeneous mantissas into fixed-point integer operands with operand-specific widths, allowing different operand formats to share a unified integer multiply-accumulate (MAC) array. Block-level analysis on Qwen3-8B shows that HIF4-S1P2 is better suited for weights while HIF4 E2M1 is better suited for activations, and their mixed-format combination achieves lower output MSE than homogeneous configurations. Across multiple benchmarks, HiMX reduces the average end-to-end accuracy loss from 2.38 to 1.77 points on Qwen3-8B and from 2.23 to 1.85 points on Qwen3-30B-A3B, demonstrating the potential benefit of heterogeneous mantissa representations for W4A4 LLM inference.

Title: FedFit: Federated Dynamic Sparse Training via Fisher Information scoring

  • Authors: Meng Bi, Hong Huang, Jinlong Song, Charles Wang, Chengming Hu, Xi Chen, Ting Yu, Xue Liu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18643
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18643
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18643]] FedFit: Federated Dynamic Sparse Training via Fisher Information scoring(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18643)
  • Keywords: federate
  • Abstract: Cross-device Federated Learning (FL) is frequently bottlenecked by the prohibitive memory and communication costs of training deep neural networks on resource-constrained edge hardware. While federated dynamic sparse training aims to alleviate these costs by adjusting sparse structures during training, existing methods rely on magnitude-based heuristics that are fundamentally ill-suited for the non-convergent, heterogeneous environments inherent to FL. To address this challenge, we propose FedFit, a federated dynamic sparse training framework that replaces simple heuristics with optimization-centric criteria for structure adjustment. By leveraging a second-order approximation of the loss landscape via the Fisher Information Matrix, FedFit enables precise and efficient structure adjustment without the overhead of explicit Hessian computation. Empirical evaluations across computer vision and natural language processing benchmarks demonstrate that FedFit significantly narrows the sparse-to-dense accuracy gap, outperforming state-of-the-art methods while maintaining high communication efficiency. Our code is available at this https URL .

Title: MeltKV: Memory-Aligned KV Cache Compression via Variable-Bit Packed Vector Quantization for Long-Context LLMs

  • Authors: Jiyun Han, Seongjoon Cho, Seungkyu Choi
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18652
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18652
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18652]] MeltKV: Memory-Aligned KV Cache Compression via Variable-Bit Packed Vector Quantization for Long-Context LLMs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18652)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly rely on long contexts, making Key-Value (KV) caches dominate memory usage and inference latency. Existing vector quantization (VQ) methods effectively compress KV caches to sub-4-bit levels but often overlook inefficiencies in memory alignment and decoding speed. This work introduces MeltKV, a memory-latency co-optimized KV cache quantization framework for long-context LLM serving. MeltKV employs a memory-aligned variable-bit index packing scheme that adaptively assigns bit-widths to grouped key channels and compactly stores indices within standard data-types, eliminating redundant bits. Additionally, head-wise codebook sharing reduces the total codebook size while maintaining accuracy. Experiments demonstrate that MeltKV effectively compresses KV representations to sub-4-bit levels down to 1 bit and, at comparable perplexity, achieves 48.1% lower memory usage and 52.9% lower latency than state-of-the-art VQ methods.

Title: Winning the Leaderboard, Losing the Distribution: Diagnosing and Mitigating Acoustic Narrowing in Low-Resource ASR

  • Authors: Kadir Goksel Gunduz
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.SD, eess.AS
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18653
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18653
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18653]] Winning the Leaderboard, Losing the Distribution: Diagnosing and Mitigating Acoustic Narrowing in Low-Resource ASR(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18653)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: For a low-resource language, one public leaderboard is often the only shared measure of progress, so optimizing toward it means optimizing toward a test distribution. Our low-rank fine-tune of whisper-large-v3-turbo ranked first on MediaSpeech, Common Voice 17 and a five-set Turkish community board, at 9.02% word error on MediaSpeech against 11.93% for the best baseline. On its private split it lost to unmodified whisper-large-v3. We treat that split as an alarm, not evidence; the main finding does not depend on it. On a twenty-condition FLEURS-TR benchmark built independently of our model selection, the unmodified base beats every fine-tune in every condition, and all five deficits clear zero under paired bootstrap. We call this acoustic narrowing: fine-tuning contracts the acoustic range a model serves well. The central finding is methodological: our in-house proxy for detecting narrowing concealed it instead, ranking the narrowed fine-tune above the sturdier base. Model selection needs a proxy from an independently produced distribution; more of the same source will not do. A rebuilt pipeline reduces the narrowing: a larger mixture with disclosed provenance, a higher-capacity base, and base-referenced selection with trajectory averaging. It wins three same-provenance axes and draws level on FLEURS-TR, 8.16% against 8.26%, interval spanning zero. Parity holds under a second inference stack and a full epoch, decoder fixed. The recovery is one trajectory, a demonstration rather than a validated recipe. In Kazakh the inversion reappears on corpora we did not build. With matched trainable-parameter shares, fine-tuning raised out-of-pool error by 2.23 points for an attention encoder-decoder and lowered it by 3.46 for a CTC encoder-only model, across three seeds each. Narrowing thus depends on the system adapted. We release TurkSpeak Bench, a robustness-first Turkish ASR leaderboard.

Title: Rethinking the Verification of Machine Unlearning

  • Authors: Lei Zhou, Youwen zhu, Rongke Liu
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18658
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18658
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18658]] Rethinking the Verification of Machine Unlearning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18658)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Machine unlearning claims require evidence that a model has removed the influence of specified training data. Yet widely deployable post hoc verifiers. infer unlearning largely from predictive behavior, the final model response that remains under the provider's control. We make this limitation concrete through Output-Level Deceptive Response (ODR), an attack that produces responses accepted by output based verifiers while preserving the original representation state. By separating output conformity from internal unlearning, ODR motivates a rethinking of machine unlearning verification, shifting the audit target from what the model returns to what changes inside it. We operationalize this shift through Representation Unlearning Verification (RUV), a gray box verifier that compares intermediate forward representations in the original and unlearned models. RUV traces a four-stage response through activation reorganization, representation displacement, neighborhood disruption, and loss of manifold support. Matched retained controls distinguish target specific responses from global model drift, while joint permutation calibration combines the four evidence views into an adaptive decision. Experiments spanning exact and approximate unlearning, class and sample level requests, multiple datasets, and diverse architectures show mean ODR deception success rates of $97.0\%$ and $97.7\%$, respectively. RUV achieves mean verification accuracies of $98.9\%$ and $94.9\%$, while rejecting $99.5\%$ and $99.8\%$ of the corresponding ODR submissions.

Title: UMM-JEPA: Generative Pretraining via Masked Next-Scale Representation Prediction for Unified Multimodal Models

  • Authors: Yucheng Zhou, Yunjia Xi, Di Yin, Jianbing Shen
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18668
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18668
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18668]] UMM-JEPA: Generative Pretraining via Masked Next-Scale Representation Prediction for Unified Multimodal Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18668)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Unified multimodal models can support both visual understanding and generation, yet generative training does not necessarily improve understanding. We argue that this limitation may arise from a mismatch between the representation targeted by generation and the perceptual representation used for understanding. Conventional generation objectives primarily optimize synthesis-oriented spaces such as discrete visual codes or generative latents. We therefore introduce UMM-JEPA, which reformulates generation as progressive prediction of continuous visual-understanding representations. The model constructs a multi-scale representation hierarchy from coarse to fine, while pixel rendering is decoupled from representation pretraining and attached only afterward. Controlled comparisons show that the representation-prediction objective accounts for most of the understanding improvement, while a data-matched control leaves the group means nearly unchanged. UMM-JEPA reaches a 16-benchmark visual mean of 51.71, against 46.36 for the understanding-only Qwen-VL baseline, and the text mean also rises. Compared with discrete-code and latent-flow generation objectives, perceptual representation prediction yields stronger and more consistent transfer to understanding. Linear probes and visual-evidence interventions further reveal richer object and spatial structure and stronger use of image-specific information. These results suggest that generation can benefit understanding when it directly supervises the perceptual representations that understanding consumes.

Title: LogicEye: Agent Driven Multimodal Dataset for Interpretable Ophthalmic Diagnosis

  • Abstract: Clinical adoption of ophthalmic Artificial Intelligence remains limited by low interpretability and a lack of finely annotated multimodal data. Most existing datasets provide only coarse disease labels without the granular pathological findings required for differential diagnosis. We present LogicEye$^{Agent}$, a dual-pool inference and verification agent that emulates clinical reasoning by jointly verifying positive inclusion signs and negative exclusion criteria. Using LogicEye$^{Agent}$, we curate LogicEye$^{Data}$, a large-scale multimodal dataset of 804,461 fundus and OCT images paired with structured pathological reports spanning 38 diseases and 29 subtypes, with quality assured through joint verification by multimodal large language models and clinical experts. Title: Thinking from Another Side of the Box: Spacetime Images for Computer Vision

  • Authors: Jade Lambert, Sébastien Piérard, Anthony Cioppa, Marc Van Droogenbroeck
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18671
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18671
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18671]] Thinking from Another Side of the Box: Spacetime Images for Computer Vision(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18671)
  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Computer vision methods for video understanding, such as object detection and tracking, inherit the way videos are captured and displayed: as successive frames processed sequentially. However, a video is an inhomogeneous 2D+time volume of pixels, named voxels, and frames are only one family of cuts through it. In this frame-based representation, static backgrounds introduce computational redundancy, and motion can only be understood across several frames. In this position paper, we study another family of cuts: spacetime images (STIs), obtained by sampling any set of pixels other than frames, for instance a line of pixels followed over time. Such images are familiar in narrow forms, from line-scan inspection and kymographs to spatiotemporal slices for motion analysis, but have received little attention as a general input representation for computer vision and are often limited to (x,t) or (y,t) cuts. We define new cuts leading to STIs in their general form, explain how to read their content, and show that a family of cuts tiles the video so that existing dense annotations transfer at no cost. We also detail how motion appears in an STI, spatially and in the frequency domain, and discuss applications such as walking pedestrians, deposited objects, and dynamic scenes. Two pilot experiments illustrate the representation. The first trains a segmentation network entirely in the spacetime domain, on transferred annotations, and performs background subtraction on CDnet 2014 close to supervised frame-based methods, without explicit temporal modeling. The second recovers the pan speed of a camera as a moving cut, identified by a simple criterion on the frequency spectrum of the resulting image, turning a temporal estimation problem into the choice of a cut. Our position is not that STIs should replace frames, but that slicing the video volume is a design variable worth studying in its own right.

Title: Robust-GCD

  • Authors: Daniel Wang
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18673
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18673
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18673]] Robust-GCD(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18673)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Generalized Category Discovery (GCD) classifies an unlabeled pool that contains both known and novel classes, given labels for the known classes only. Existing GCD methods assume that every un- labeled image is clean, but unlabeled data collected in the wild is often degraded by blur, sensor noise and compression artifacts. We formalize the setting in which an unknown fraction of the unlabeled pool is corrupted by an unknown operator as Degraded Generalized Category Discovery (D-GCD). To tackle it, we propose Robust-GCD, a framework that estimates the corruption directly from the unlabeled pool and uses the estimate to build labeled anchors for known classes and pseudo-anchors for novel classes inside the corrupted region of feature space. A trust curriculum controls how much the unlabeled pool is trusted while the anchors take hold, and a paired alignment loss keeps a clean image and its corrupted twin close in feature space. Across five datasets and multiple corruption families, Robust-GCD recovers between 43% and 96% of the All accuracy that SimGCD loses under contamination, and more than 60% on four of the five datasets, with the largest gains on the corrupted images themselves.

Title: SynPanZ: A Synthetic Dataset to Improve Multi-Animal Pose Estimation in Chimpanzees (Pan troglodytes)

  • Authors: Flora Talyigas, Lindsay Murray, Sonya Hill, Richard Stocker
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18674
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18674
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18674]] SynPanZ: A Synthetic Dataset to Improve Multi-Animal Pose Estimation in Chimpanzees (Pan troglodytes)(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18674)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Pose estimation underpins a wide range of automated monitoring workflows in animal behaviour research. Unfortunately, endangered great apes such as chimpanzees suffer a chronic shortage of the type of hand-annotated training data that has made robust pose estimation in humans and other model organisms possible. This paper introduces SynPanZ, a synthetically generated dataset for pose estimation in great apes. Our pipeline renders multi-individual scenes from rigged, behaviourally animated chimpanzee models with randomised poses, lighting conditions, environmental contexts, and camera motion, producing animated RGB videos with per-instance 2D keypoint locations without manual labelling. To test whether synthetic augmentation improves cross-dataset generalisation, four DeepLabCut models are trained under matched settings on data sourced from DeepWild with varying real-to-synthetic augmentation ratios. Models are evaluated on ChimpACT, a visually disjoint zoo dataset. We find that augmenting the DeepWild training data with approximately three times as many synthetic frames nearly doubles instance coverage (12.34% to 22.54%) while maintaining keypoint localisation accuracy on detected individuals (PCK@0.20: 49.71% to 52.25%). Synthetic-only training fails, showing that rendered data complements rather than replaces real annotated data. This work represents a step towards reliable pose estimation for great apes.

Title: Not All Steps Matter: Temporally Adaptive Spectral Control for Concept Removal in Diffusion Models

  • Authors: Chanaka Thushitha Bandara, Lakmali Nadeesha Kumari, Sen-Ching Samson Cheung
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18678
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18678
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18678]] Not All Steps Matter: Temporally Adaptive Spectral Control for Concept Removal in Diffusion Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18678)
  • Keywords: attack, diffusion
  • Abstract: As text-to-image diffusion models see widespread deployment, removing unsafe or undesirable concepts without retraining has become a pressing need. Spectral methods are attractive because they are training-free and reversible, yet current variants apply the same output-value (OV) circuit intervention at every denoising step, assuming concept information is temporally uniform. This wastes intervention where concept energy is low while degrading image quality, an inefficiency that compounds across concept types. Through timestep-resolved spectral analysis, concept-specific energy is found to follow a consistent temporal hierarchy: content concepts decay fastest, object concepts are intermediate, and style concepts persist longest. This paper proposes Timestep-Adaptive Spectral Nullification (TASN), which adapts suppression strength and spectral components to the concept energy profile. Cross-attention at each timestep is decomposed into concept-aligned and concept-orthogonal components, and it is proved that when the orthogonal component is approximately stable, the collateral-minimizing schedule sets suppression proportional to concept energy. TASN implements this with a timestep-adaptive spectral subset tracking only active concept directions, strictly generalizing uniform Spectral Nullification (SN). On five red-teaming benchmarks, TASN lowers attack success rate below uniform SN while recovering the majority of its quality loss; ablations confirm gains arise from temporal targeting rather than reduced suppression, generalizing across concept categories and architectures.

Title: Spread Too Thin: How Higher Resolution Dilutes a Vision Transformer's Attention, and a Training-Free Rescaling

  • Authors: Neil D. B. Bruce
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18680
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18680
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18680]] Spread Too Thin: How Higher Resolution Dilutes a Vision Transformer's Attention, and a Training-Free Rescaling(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18680)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: A vision transformer read at a higher resolution than it was trained at spreads each attention row over many more patches, yet every row must still hand out exactly one unit of weight. A law of softmax attention predicts that a growing crowd of bounded scores dilutes the attention on any patch or register whose score stays fixed. The same law gives a remedy that needs no training: multiply every score, the sink's included, by the ratio of the logarithms of the test and training token counts. Given sufficient margins at the training resolution, this keeps the attention on a match when one is present, and on a sink such as a register when none is, though trained models need not meet those margins. We test these predictions in public vision transformers and in controlled models read far above their training token count. Attention spreads as predicted. Registers lose their share, though production models compensate in part, and the rescaling holds the share of DINOv2's registers nearly level. Applied at test time without training, the rescaling recovers part of the lost accuracy in nearly every model, with absolute or rotary positions and with registers, and on images with native detail. The calibrated multiplier rises with the logarithmic ratio on average and varies across models. Its rise is steeper than the square-root rule used in image generators; in controlled models the log ratio is also more accurate, while in the public models the two rules' accuracy difference remains unresolved. The rescaling does not restore the attention a model learned, and why it helps, beyond countering the dilution, remains open.

Title: PolarGait: Constructing Complementary Views from a Single Human Sketch for Gait Recognition

  • Authors: Chao Zhang, Zhuang Zheng, Zhanyong Mei, Huayan Pu, Jun Luo, Jinglei Zhao
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18682
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18682

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Existing multimodal gait recognition methods exploit complementary information by fusing heterogeneous representations. Whether useful complementarity can be constructed from a single representation before fusion remains less explored. We propose PolarGait, which constructs complementary views of Human Sketch through filtering and spatial reparameterization. Filter-Oriented Sketch Decomposition (FOSD) uses Gaussian smoothing to extract coherent structures and absolute residuals to capture local response differences. Gait-Oriented Polar Transformation (GOPT) combines silhouette-guided sketch normalization, a gait-oriented center, and lower-body-biased non-uniform angular sampling to construct a polar view complementary to the Cartesian representation. Silhouette provides auxiliary global body-shape information. Experiments on CCPG, SUSTech1K, CASIA-B, and Gait3D validate the framework. Under the single-Sketch setting, FOSD+GOPT improves the original Sketch baseline by 6.6% on CCPG and 4.6% on CASIA-B. The complete PolarGait achieves Rank-1 accuracies of 87.9%, 95.2%, 96.6%, and 86.4% on the four benchmarks, respectively. These results show that complementary gait information can be constructed within a single structured representation and integrated for robust gait recognition.

Title: ThinkWise: Learning When to Think in Large Multimodal Reasoning Models via Visual and Difficulty Perception

  • Authors: Wenkang Zhou, Chen Yang, Jiaheng Peng, Xitong Li, Hongzhi Yin, Yunke Zhang, Lu Yu, Peng-Fei Zhang, Xuhui Ren, Shijie Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18685
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18685
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18685]] ThinkWise: Learning When to Think in Large Multimodal Reasoning Models via Visual and Difficulty Perception(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18685)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large Multimodal Reasoning Models (LMRMs) extend large language models to heterogeneous modalities, demonstrating strong potential across a wide range of open-ended tasks. Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has been widely adopted to improve reasoning performance in LMRMs; however, existing approaches overlook the critical role of visual perception uncertainty in guiding reasoning processes. We term this limitation Reasoning Allocation Misalignment, where the misalignment between visual perception uncertainty and reasoning depth leads to over-allocation of deep reasoning under uncertain visual grounding, resulting in unnecessarily long reasoning trajectories and degraded performance. To address this issue, we propose ThinkWise, a perception-aware RLVR framework that jointly models task difficulty and visual perception state to regulate reasoning depth. Specifically, we introduce an instance-adaptive visual perception modeling mechanism that enables context-aware calibration of perception uncertainty, leading to more accurate perception estimation. We further design a structured reward formulation that jointly considers perception, correctness, and task difficulty. In particular, an answer reward provides supervision for correctness, a perception- and difficulty-conditioned length reward adaptively regulates reasoning depth, and a perception reward encourages trajectories with stronger visual grounding. Extensive experiments on multiple multimodal reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that ThinkWise consistently improves the trade-off between accuracy and reasoning efficiency across both 3B and 7B models. These results highlight the importance of perception-aware reasoning and establish a principled framework for advancing LMRMs.

Title: Perceptual Weight Space Programming for Diffusion Super-Resolution

  • Authors: Chenzhi Zheng, Zhuoran Zheng

  • Keywords: diffusion, large language model
  • Abstract: Diffusion models for image super-resolution can recover scene content while producing distracting artifacts or unbalanced colors. We study whether a large language model (LLM) can improve perceptual quality by editing a small subset of existing U-Net weights without a clean reference image. The proposed framework provides the editor with architecture code, selected weights, activation summaries, and sampling previews. It permits up to 1,020 candidate trials while limiting accepted changes to three layers and fewer than 0.1\% of model parameters. Title: What Does Hard-Negative Fine-Tuning Teach Multimodal Large Language Models? Controlled Evidence from a Compositional Benchmark

Title: TopSDF: Topological Signed Distance Field for High-Quality Triangle Mesh Extraction from 3D Gaussian Splatting

  • Authors: Khoi Do, Yu-kun Lai, Binh-Son Hua
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18708
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18708
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18708]] TopSDF: Topological Signed Distance Field for High-Quality Triangle Mesh Extraction from 3D Gaussian Splatting(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18708)
  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: Recent advances in 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) have demonstrated its effectiveness for photorealistic novel view synthesis. However, extracting clean, watertight, explicit surface meshes from 3DGS remains a critical challenge. Previous methods approximate geometry via discrete Marching Tetrahedra, where each grid vertex is assigned a single scalar signed distance value. At complex geometric boundaries and thin structures, this suboptimal parameterization forces severe compromises across shared vertices, causing gradient collisions and geometric frustration. Furthermore, existing optimization objectives neglect element shape conditioning, producing degenerate triangles. We introduce TopSDF, a framework that models the signed distance state as a multivariate Gaussian random field. By learning latent topological covariances across tetrahedral vertices, our formulation expands local degrees of freedom for SDF estimates, allowing the candidate mesh vertices to be placed flexibly along the bipolar edges in the tetrahedra as driven by such topological correlation. Concurrently, we directly integrate variational meshing energies into the joint optimization, coupled with an energy-driven Gaussian densification strategy to improve triangle mesh quality. Our experiments across Mip-NeRF 360, Tanks & Temples, and ScanNet++ show that TopSDF achieves state-of-the-art mesh-based novel view rendering (+0.86 dB PSNR on Mip-NeRF 360) and triangle-mesh quality (from 0.37 to 0.49 aspect ratio).

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models answer underspecified questions readily, and accuracy-only evaluation rewards guessing over abstaining.   • Authors: Nabil Madali
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Stereo Particle Image Velocimetry (PIV) recovers three-component velocity fields by triangulating tracer particles observed by synchronized cameras. We present a target-free method that calibrates a stereo PIV rig directly from particle image sequences. Because tracer particles are visually indistinguishable, we match motion rather than appearance: building on CUT3R, each camera stream is processed by a recurrent transformer whose persistent state accumulates particle motion over time. We train on a new synthetic stereo PIV dataset with ground-truth poses and evaluate on standard PIV optical flow benchmarks and on 200 real rig placements. Our model achieves lower flow error than existing PIV networks, more accurate cross-view correspondences than COTR, and higher pose accuracy than the strongest detector-matcher pipelines on both synthetic and real data, without any calibration target.

  • Abstract: Medical image classifiers require not only high accuracy but also reliable predictions under small input perturbations. We propose a fine-tuning approach based on quantum-inspired orthogonal butterfly layers that preserves the Lipschitz bounds of pretrained certified networks. Inspired by quantum circuits of reconfigurable beam splitters, these layers preserve orthogonality by construction using only $\mathcal{O}(n\log n)$ trainable parameters. Larger robustness guarantees, including certificates covering certain image-processing transformations, can be obtained at the cost of reduced accuracy. These results highlight the potential of quantum-inspired butterfly layers for parameter-efficient adaptation of medical image classifiers while preserving the mathematical guarantees underlying certified robustness.

Title: Hybrid Compression for Memory-Efficient Matrix-Vector Multiplication

  • Authors: Felipe A. Louza, Giovanni Manzini, Guilherme P. Telles
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18792
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18792
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18792]] Hybrid Compression for Memory-Efficient Matrix-Vector Multiplication(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18792)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Iterative machine-learning algorithms repeatedly access large real-valued matrices through matrix-vector products, and their performance is limited by memory bandwidth rather than arithmetic throughput. Lossless compression schemes that support computation directly over the compressed data reduce both the data moved and the memory footprint of these workloads. The mm-RePair framework [11] follows this approach by applying the RePair grammar compressor to the Compressed Sparse Row/Value (CSRV) representation of a matrix and performing matrix-vector multiplications directly on the compressed data. Its effectiveness, however, degrades on matrices that are dense or contain many distinct values. In this paper we present a hybrid strategy for the mm-RePair framework that combines grammar and entropy compression. The CSRV sequence is partitioned into a grammar-compressible component, formed by the entries that mm-RePair can compress effectively, and an entropy-compressible component holding the remaining entries, which is encoded with the ans-fold coder [24]. Compression improves further by sorting and delta encoding the entries of each row in the CSRV representation, and by remapping the alphabet to a contiguous range. Experiments on real-valued machine-learning matrices, including word embeddings and transformer hidden states, show that our hybrid strategy is more compact than the original \mmRepair on every input and reduces the peak memory of multiplication, with the largest gains on dense matrices with many distinct values, at competitive multiplication times. It also outperforms the column-reordering variant of \mmRepair on most of the matrices, and combining the two is even more compact on the sparse ones, at the cost of a much longer compression time. Finally, our strategy scales with the number of threads as well as the original \mmRepair does.

We present RescueVLM, an open-source, training-free and model-agnostic triage tool built on SketchVLM: a general-purpose open-weight VLM runs fully on-premise and is swapped for a newer model through a simple dropdown selection, so triage quality grows with the underlying models because all task knowledge lives in prompts and an output contract rather than in trained weights. In a qualitative evaluation on German fire-brigade field data from three real deployments and a live fire exercise, a single query condenses an entire survey mission into a handful of annotated frames that a commander can review at a glance.

Title: UnSPAD: Uncertainty-aware Single Photon Burst Photography

  • Authors: Sachin Shah, Kalyan Bhetwal, Levi Burner, Christopher A. Metzler
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18869
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18869
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18869]] UnSPAD: Uncertainty-aware Single Photon Burst Photography(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18869)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: We introduce UnSPAD, an uncertainty-aware burst photography framework capable of fusing and denoising arbitrarily long sequences of binary single photon avalanche diode (SPAD) measurements. Unlike prior methods that require fixed burst lengths or rely on powerful generative priors, UnSPAD combines learned motion estimation with uncertainty-informed measurement fusion and denoising to produce temporally consistent, hallucination-free reconstructions. UnSPAD first forms an initial scene estimate by averaging flow-aligned binary frames. UnSPAD then uses per-pixel uncertainty maps to perform uncertainty-aware denoising; the denoiser is informed about which regions accumulated the fewest observations (and thus contain the most relative shot noise) due to motion, occlusion, or alignment errors. This two-step reconstruction pipeline helps mitigate hallucinations---outside of the structure implicitly introduced by the flow estimates, the initial scenes estimates are entirely prior-free. Experiments on real and simulated SPAD and quantum image sensor (QIS) benchmarks demonstrate state-of-the-art reconstruction quality, improved temporal consistency, and superior color fidelity relative to the state of the art.

Title: Memory Safety Isn't All You Need: Code Reuse Attacks in Memory Safe Languages

  • Authors: Eric Cornelissen, Musard Balliu
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18910
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18910

  • Keywords: security, defense, attack
  • Abstract: Memory safety rules out classical code reuse attacks that rely on arbitrary memory corruption, but does not eliminate code reuse altogether. Attacks such as object injection, prototype pollution, class pollution, DOM clobbering, and DOM script gadgets arise in memory-safe languages without violating the underlying memory safety abstraction. We show that these seemingly different attacks share a common structure: Attacker-controlled data influences accesses to shared runtime state, such as runtime type information, prototype or class state, and the DOM, and a value fetched from that state subsequently influences a security-sensitive operation. Drawing on this insight, we develop a language-agnostic framework that decomposes an attack into a weakness, ending in a fetch from shared runtime state, and a gadget, which propagates the fetched value to a sensitive sink. We show that the framework captures five established attack classes and use it to derive stateless, mutating, and recycling weaknesses, together with simple, vertical, and horizontal gadget structures. We further systematize existing defenses into three common strategies - mediation, minimization, and constraint - and use them to derive candidate defense directions. Finally, we develop a model language that gives a formal account of code-reuse weaknesses and potential conditions for gadgets and end-to-end vulnerabilities.
