Title: OverclusterDRG: a Deterministic, LP-Free Heuristic for Robust $k$-Center

  • Authors: Rudra Bhardwaj, Rajni Dabas, Neelima Gupta
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18931
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18931
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18931]] OverclusterDRG: a Deterministic, LP-Free Heuristic for Robust $k$-Center(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18931)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: The $k$-center problem is a fundamental formulation for metric clustering. However, its min-max objective is highly sensitive to outliers, motivating the Robust $k$-Center (R$k$C) formulation which permits excluding up to $z$ points as outliers. Existing algorithms for R$k$C suffer from scalability issues: LP rounding relies on fractional relaxations that scale poorly, known combinatorial approximation algorithms are computationally expensive due to radius guessing, and scalable randomized heuristics require exponential trial multipliers to guarantee success. We present OverclusterDRG, a deterministic, LP-free heuristic for R$k$C. The algorithm leverages the theoretical insight of farthest-point oversampling -- adding exactly $z$ extra candidates to the center set to absorb outliers -- followed by a forward-greedy initialization and a $1$-swap local search. Evaluations across real-world datasets show that OverclusterDRG attains a mean approximation ratio of 1.147 against fractional LP lower bounds on the UCI Adult dataset, outperforming state-of-the-art baselines in both clustering quality and speed. By avoiding subset-guessing and exponential trial multipliers, it scales smoothly to $n=10^5$ points where LP solvers and theoretical algorithms become intractable.

Title: Bilateral Log-Linear Attention

  • Authors: Kazi Ashik Islam, Guojing Cong
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18974
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.18974
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.18974]] Bilateral Log-Linear Attention(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.18974)
  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: Sequence models represent past context at different resolutions, using token-level keys and values, fixed-size recurrent states, or hierarchies of compressed summaries. Full attention supports strong associative recall but requires quadratic work during training and prefill and a linearly growing key-value cache during inference. Linear attention and state-space models reduce these costs through fixed-size recurrent memory, but this compression can limit recall. Log-linear attention offers a middle ground by maintaining logarithmically many state matrices. However, its hierarchy favors finer resolution near the current query while compressing earlier context into coarser summaries. To complement this allocation of resolution, we introduce bilateral log-linear attention, which pairs the existing hierarchy with a second hierarchy anchored at the sequence origin. By combining their reads, the two branches provide finer access to both early and recent context while remaining causal. We derive recurrent and chunkwise parallel formulations for the added branch, preserving log-linear training and prefill work, logarithmic decoding work per token, and logarithmic persistent state storage at fixed feature dimensions and chunk size. In multi-query associative recall, this design raises mean overall test accuracy from 76.37% to 96.12% over forward log-linear attention across multiple training seeds. For pretrained language models under approximate parameter matching, it raises the mean score on six selected RULER needle-in-a-haystack tasks from 26.97% to 50.03% at a context length of 6144 tokens. In the same setting, bilateral attention also outperforms forward log-linear attention on seven of eight downstream retrieval tasks, including question answering and information extraction.

  • Keywords: security, protect
  • Abstract: Multi-agent systems increasingly delegate tasks and permissions across chains of autonomous agents that invoke protected tools. Title: Role-Separated Diffusion Transformers: Global Rule Isolation and Deterministic Sampling Manifold Compression

  • Authors: Zhiguang Liu, Yi Shang
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19009
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19009
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19009]] Role-Separated Diffusion Transformers: Global Rule Isolation and Deterministic Sampling Manifold Compression(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19009)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: We present Role-Separated Diffusion Transformers (RoSeDiT), a new diffusion transformer architecture that isolates the global rule from the image tokens. RoSeViT showed in visual reasoning that a vanilla ViT spreads a shared rule over all $L$ tokens at an entropy of order $Ld$, whereas an enforced controller route reduces it to order $d$. In class-conditional DiT and SiT, the class and the noise level are explicitly conditioned by adaLN, so a RoSeViT-style controller channel on the ViT side is insufficient to establish the full role separation. RoSeDiT-XL/2 surpasses SiT-XL/2's 7M-step FID of 8.3 at 1.4M steps with 7.98, using one-fifth as many training steps. We further observe that models with the same denoising loss can have a large FID gap, which indicates a misalignment between the denoising objective and the image manifold. We formalize this phenomenon into Deterministic Sampling Manifold Compression (DSMC), a two-stage training schedule with which RoSeDiT-XL/2 surpasses SiT + REPA's 4M-step FID of 5.9 at 2.4M steps with 5.84.

Title: Advancing the Calibration-Compute Frontier of LLM Classifiers via Asymmetric Duos

  • Authors: Berke Akturk, Ali Can Sahin, Rasit Cem Solatay, Emanuel Sommer, Yawei Li, David Rügamer
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19020
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19020
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19020]] Advancing the Calibration-Compute Frontier of LLM Classifiers via Asymmetric Duos(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19020)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: The importance of reliable uncertainty estimation in large language models (LLMs) has become increasingly pronounced, yet modern models remain poorly calibrated and frequently overconfident. In this study, we address this by proposing an efficient method for LLM classifier calibration using the Asymmetric Duos framework. Our approach combines a strong base model with a smaller sidekick model via logit-space fusion, utilizing global scaling parameters optimized on a validation set. Title: Understanding the Role of Reasoning Data in Pretraining

  • Authors: Jianhao Huang, Allan Zhang, Ali Payani, Baharan Mirzasoleiman
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19042
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19042
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19042]] Understanding the Role of Reasoning Data in Pretraining(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19042)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Reasoning data expose models to multi-step reasoning, but reasoning traces may cover only a subset of the available facts. How should pretraining balance atomic facts with reasoning traces? We study this question by constructing a setting in which every fact provides an atomic training signal, while only a subset appears in reasoning supervision, enabling us to measure generalization to transitions observed atomically but never used in training traces. For a linear transformer, we derive the weight assigned to atomic training signals that minimizes held-transition squared error and show that its leading-order optimum increases with reasoning-chain length and decreases with the fraction of facts held out from reasoning traces. These trends persist in a full transformer layer with an MLP, softmax attention, and cross-entropy loss. We further validate the role of reasoning complexity and data coverage in two natural-language settings: controlled multi-hop reasoning and mathematical pretraining. Together, our results provide a principled view of how reasoning-chain structure and data coverage should shape the allocation of training data during pretraining.

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19104
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19104

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: A sentence is built from words that carry lexical meaning and grammatical roles, and brain activity during reading reflects both. Title: ST-GAT: A Leakage-Free Spatio-Temporal Graph Attention Framework for Robust Network Intrusion Detection

  • Authors: Nikhil Parkar, Sanskar Pandey, Siddhi Prajapati, Suganeshwari G, Syed Ibrahim
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19109
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19109
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19109]] ST-GAT: A Leakage-Free Spatio-Temporal Graph Attention Framework for Robust Network Intrusion Detection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19109)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Intrusion detection systems are a central defence in modern networks. Deep learning models report strong anomaly-detection results, but evaluations on standard benchmarks such as CSE-CIC-IDS-2018 are often inflated by two methodological problems: temporal leakage from overlapping sliding windows, and contamination from preprocessing fitted before the train/test split. Per-flow classifiers also ignore the spatio-temporal dependencies across sequences of network flows. We propose a Spatio-Temporal Graph Attention Network (ST-GAT) that represents traffic as a sequence of graph snapshots. It builds k-Nearest Neighbour (k-NN) edges within each window, applies multi-head Graph Attention Network (GAT) layers for spatial aggregation, and uses a Bidirectional Long Short-Term Memory (BiLSTM) network with temporal attention for sequence modelling. This suggests that aggressive resampling of small, noisy minority classes can hurt detection in heavily imbalanced traffic.

Title: On-policy distillation as a Backdoor Propagation Channel

  • Authors: Zane Fu, Yanzhao Zheng, Ziting Qin, Qianshan Wei, Yi Yu, Gongli Xi, Jihuai Zhu, Baohua Dong, Hangcheng Zhu, Ruohui Huang, Gang Yu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19207
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19207
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19207]] On-policy distillation as a Backdoor Propagation Channel(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19207)
  • Keywords: security, attack, large language model
  • Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) is emerging as an important paradigm for the post training of large language models, improving student models by leveraging supervision generated online by teacher models. However, whether this process also transfers latent security risks from teacher models to student models remains insufficiently studied. In this work, we reveal a new security risk in which an attacker first implants a conditional backdoor into the teacher model through supervised fine tuning (SFT), after which OPD can transfer the backdoor to the student model with only an extremely small proportion of trigger prompt exposure. More concerningly, we find that such backdoor transfer is substantially stronger under OPD than under conventional distillation methods, suggesting that OPD may provide an efficient channel for propagating backdoors across models. Our mechanistic analysis shows that the backdoor signal is primarily transmitted through anomalous differences in the conditional probabilities produced by the teacher model for triggered and clean inputs, and is gradually internalized into the internal representations of the student model during training. Furthermore, we investigate how to suppress such anomalous conditional probability signals at the source of distillation supervision and, based on this analysis, propose an OPD training method tailored to this conditional backdoor propagation setting to mitigate the risk of backdoor transfer induced by untrusted teacher supervision.

  • Abstract: We freeze a guardrail probe and threshold, then measure its operating point across 18 cells covering 16 public language-model updates. A benchmark's staleness criteria classify none of them as stale. A rule based on these criteria flags 0.019 of layer cells where the rate doubles or halves. A label-free rate estimate flags 0.918, clearing 0.898 of the remaining cells. A two-moment model matches the change within a factor of 1.5 in 0.905 of 63 registered cells and in 0.773 of the 22 cells that moved. Resetting the threshold using stored benign prompts restores the false-positive rate without requiring new labels. However, poisoning 1% of these prompts leaves at most 0.017 of harmful prompts flagged. An attacker controlling the update cut detection of one of three targeted categories in three of five seeds. Both checks missed this attack, while a post hoc labelled check detected it.

  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: We present Neural Dual Bounds, a calibration-free framework that produces deterministic upper bounds for MAP inference in probabilistic graphical models using a single neural-network forward pass.   • Abstract: Visual brain decoding translates neural activity into human-interpretable images. This indicates that improving the input condition alone is insufficient; the generative process itself requires explicit supervision. Title: GameWM: Toward Next-Generation Video Games with Interaction-Centric World Models

  • Authors: Bing He, Runze Wang, Fei Zha, Zhongyuan Wu, Ke Xu, Tao Zhuang, Yuxuan Xie, Zhongyuan Liu
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19247
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19247
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19247]] GameWM: Toward Next-Generation Video Games with Interaction-Centric World Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19247)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: For more than half a century, video games have pursued better graphics, more natural interaction, and more engaging gameplay. Generative video models (VMs) now bear on all three. Neural networks already draw most of the pixels in shipped games, and recent world models let a lightweight game engine keep the world state while a VM turns the engine's coarse proxy video into final frames. These systems, however, treat the VM as a renderer that only restyles motion the engine has already authored, which leaves most of its generative capacity unused and limits the diversity a game can offer. To realize this division of labor, we build GameWM as a complete system: a data engine centered on interactions rather than exploration, an interface through which authored character sheets, scene art, and skill descriptions enter the model, and a model family from 5B to 33B parameters with a real-time causal variant for play. Together they give every player a fresh version of the same game: each player's own character, world, and skills turn the same engine mechanics into a new experience, while every event keeps the timing and outcome the GameWM-engine computed.

Title: Optional Traps: An Empirical Study of LLM Agents Against Credential Deception and Alert-Driven Revocation

  • Authors: Raditio Ghifiardi
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19295
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19295
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19295]] Optional Traps: An Empirical Study of LLM Agents Against Credential Deception and Alert-Driven Revocation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19295)
  • Keywords: protect, attack, large language model
  • Abstract: Deception (honeypots, decoys and canary credentials) rests on a simple premise: legitimate users never touch a fake asset, so any touch is a high-confidence signal. We ask whether that premise holds when the attacker is a large language model (LLM) agent, and what deception contributes once ordinary hardening is in place. We built an isolated, hash-chained lab around a mock Records API and report four studies. (1) In an exploratory run, a local 9.7B-parameter model ignored every optional decoy, read a genuinely leaked service key from a debug endpoint and exported the protected records in both static and adaptive deception modes. (2) After removing the leak, planting a canary credential in its place and revoking the offending client, a frozen 60-session study with a 4B-parameter model found export success of 20/20 at baseline, 0/20 with hardening alone and 0/20 with hardening plus deception (exact paired McNemar p = 1.9e-6 baseline vs. defended; p = 1 hardened vs. defended). Deception added detection and per-client revocation in 19 of 20 defended sessions, but no measurable prevention beyond hardening. (3) On Google Cloud, a real OpenCanary login alert was authenticated, signed and turned into revocation of the attacking client; the first denied export followed within an observed upper bound of 303 ms, a separate legitimate client completed 100/100 requests, and all 14 control checks passed. (4) In multi-agent swarm engagements, agents that used the canary were revoked 39-59 s after the bait was served without affecting siblings, but one model-driven legitimate agent also took the bait, a false-positive risk specific to LLM clients. Against LLM agents, traps must sit on the attacker's path rather than beside it, closing real weaknesses does the preventive work, and deception's measurable value is fast, attributable detection. All results are lab-scale and are not a production claim.

Title: Where Mixture Recognition Fails: An Ingredient-Level Audit of Unseen Combinations in SmellNet

  • Authors: Zirui Liu, Zheming Zhu
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19297
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19297
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19297]] Where Mixture Recognition Fails: An Ingredient-Level Audit of Unseen Combinations in SmellNet(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19297)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: An electronic nose must name every ingredient of a mixture, including combinations it has never measured. SmellNet, a public benchmark for this task, scores ingredients with a hit rate that is told how many each sample contains. We audit what this protocol hides. We hold out ingredient combinations from the SmellNet training data, evaluate four architectures (MLP, CNN, LSTM and a Transformer encoder) without revealing the ingredient count, and repeat the evaluation once on the official unseen split. Under the count-informed protocol, 90-96% of mixtures receive an ingredient that is absent, on our folds and on the official split alike. On our folds, every model identifies one ingredient reliably but the second rarely, and it completes the pair with a familiar training partner about twice as often as chance. On the official split, even the first-ranked ingredient is correct in only 29-46% of binary mixtures, often because a frequent training ingredient that is absent from every test pair is ranked first, and on its combinations that are truly absent from training no model names both ingredients in more than 1.2% of them. The failures fall on the same ingredients for every architecture, and a frequency correction only moves errors between ingredients. Mixture recognition is therefore limited by ingredients the models cannot separate and by a preference for frequent ingredients, not by the architecture, and it should be evaluated with set-level errors and without oracle cardinality.

Title: A Think Block Is Conditioning, Not a Plan

  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19341
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19341

  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Generative models increasingly write a chain of thought before drawing, and report large gains from doing so. Across a unified model with a think mode, a reinforcement-learned reasoning generator, and a planner driving a frozen diffusion model, the same picture emerges. Field-level interventions find only colour executed reliably, and a control writing the same sentences into the user prompt shows that limit belongs to the generator rather than to the planning channel. Two judges and two annotators replicate the central contrasts. A think block earns its keep as a specification of what to draw, not as a plan that is carried out.

In this paper, we present the first attempt to introduce event cameras into 3D lane detection. We further propose RETR, a structure preserving transformer that converts complementary RGB and event observations into coherent 3D lane geometry. RETR first aligns modality consistent evidence through Reciprocal Context Flow Fusion, then preserves thin and uncertain lane structures with Uncertainty Aware Structural Consolidation, and finally decodes ordered lane hypotheses using a Geometry State Decoder with proposal conditioned initialization, reference conditioned query evolution, and diversified geometric inquiry. Extensive experiments show that RETR achieves state-of-the-art performance on both benchmarks, especially showing strong improvements in challenging lighting and complex road geometry scenarios. Code and dataset will be available at this https URL.

Title: Test-Time Constraint-Aware Diffusion Predictive Control

  • Authors: Braghadeesh Lakshminarayanan, Ojas Phadake, Qi Chen, Dominik Baumann
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19380
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19380
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19380]] Test-Time Constraint-Aware Diffusion Predictive Control(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19380)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion models can learn multimodal distributions over finite-horizon state-input sequences, providing a way to generate candidates for predictive control. However, sequences generated by a pretrained model may violate safety constraints introduced at deployment, for example when obstacles are added or repositioned. Retraining whenever these constraints change limits the usefulness of learned predictive controllers. In this paper, we propose Test-Time Constraint-Aware Diffusion Predictive Control (TCA-DPC), an algorithm for adapting the sampling distribution of a pretrained diffusion controller to newly introduced safety constraints without retraining. We treat the pretrained model as a prior over finite-horizon sequences and define an adapted distribution through exponential tilting based on a constraint-violation penalty. The tilt strength is adjusted online using the fraction of generated sequences that fail constraint verification. We approximately sample the adapted distribution using the Metropolis-adjusted Langevin algorithm in the Gaussian noise space of the pretrained diffusion sampler.

Title: Memorization Lives in a Few Attention Heads: Mitigating Memorization in Diffusion Models via Projection-Based Suppression

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19427

  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19427]] Memorization Lives in a Few Attention Heads: Mitigating Memorization in Diffusion Models via Projection-Based Suppression(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19427)
  • Keywords: privacy, diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion models can memorize and reproduce training samples during inference, raising serious privacy and copyright concerns. Existing inference-time mitigation methods often suppress conditional information globally, which may reduce memorization but can also harm semantic alignment and image quality. In this paper, we revisit memorization from the perspective of cross-attention heads. Through systematic analysis and targeted ablation, we find that memorization is mainly driven by a small subset of shallow-layer attention heads with abnormally strong semantic control, rather than by the entire cross-attention mechanism. Motivated by this finding, we propose Projection-based Top-k Attention Head Suppression, a targeted inference-time mitigation method. Our method first identifies the top-k attention heads most related to memorization using a semantic-control score, and then removes their condition-specific components through directional projection between conditional and unconditional representations. This design selectively weakens memorization-related features while preserving useful semantic information. Extensive experiments on different diffusion models and datasets show that our method effectively reduces memorization and achieves a better trade-off between memorization mitigation and image quality than existing approaches.

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Depth recurrence increases effective model depth by repeatedly applying a fixed computation block. However, sharing attention parameters does not share the key-value (KV) states generated at different recurrence iterations, which causes memory growth with recurrence depth during autoregressive decoding. We study how to reduce this memory cost through architectural and cache-sharing approaches within a common depth-recurrent framework. We first study a looped hybrid that repeats a stack of Transformer layers, with recurrent mLSTM or Gated DeltaNet blocks replacing most attention layers and periodic Transformer attention anchors retained. Finally, we train a looped Transformer with recurrent KV replay, where only selected recurrence iterations write KV states that later iterations reuse. This trains the model to operate with the same shared KV topology used during inference. We find that looped hybrids require sufficiently frequent attention anchors, with performance depending strongly on the recurrent mixer. For cross-iteration KV sharing, recurrent KV replay allows multiple recurrence iterations to reuse the same KV states while retaining performance close to the full looped Transformer with separate KV caches for each iteration.

Title: Grokking Can Be Steered: Selecting Desirable Solutions By Choice Of Learning Rule

  • Authors: Pascal Sager, Benjamin Grewe, Pau Vilimelis Aceituno, Thilo Stadelmann
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19477
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19477
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19477]] Grokking Can Be Steered: Selecting Desirable Solutions By Choice Of Learning Rule(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19477)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: During grokking, a network transitions from memorization to generalization, yet what shapes the neural implementation (circuit) of the generalizing solution and specifically the number of participating neurons (its size) remains unclear. We find that the learning rule, the combination of credit assignment algorithm and optimizer, controls this: On modular addition, every learning rule we study that groks recovers the same Fourier-based algorithm, yet implemented by circuits that differ in size. Why? A neuron stays in use when its credit is large relative to the non-gradient term of its own update, and this single condition unifies three routes to unused neurons: the coupled weight decay, Adam's $\epsilon$ and the decay of a trainable per-neuron gain. Equalizing the magnitude of credit assigned to each neuron, or scaling each neuron's weight decay by its credit, expands the effective circuit size, measured from the outgoing weights, from 60% to 99% of the layer's neurons at similar training and test accuracy. We vary which neurons share one equalization factor, from the whole layer to individual neurons, and find that the circuit stays concentrated for groups of eight or more and fully distributes only when every neuron has its own factor. Credit magnitudes are usually set by the normalization of standard optimizers: Nero, which normalizes per neuron, distributes the computation, whereas Adam and seven others, including RMSProp, Muon, and Shampoo, use other normalization schemes and retain small, concentrated circuits. Distributed circuits tolerate neuron deletion better, while concentrated circuits can be pruned further and transfer about twice as fast to a task that recombines the same features, largely by recruiting their unused neurons. Learning rule choice thus steers circuit size, trading robustness against compressibility and transfer speed without changing the learned algorithm.

  • Abstract: Automated Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) image classification provides a crucial non-invasive approach for neurological diagnosis, yet it is fundamentally challenged by inherent acquisition noise and high inter-subject structural similarity, which obscure subtle pathological variations, thereby limiting the performance of deep neural networks. This mechanism explicitly orders positive and negative pairs based on their structural and semantic difficulty, thereby amplifying the fine-grained semantic discriminability of the learned representations. Extensive experiments across three diverse neurological datasets demonstrate that DBHiCo achieves state-of-the-art performance, consistently outperforming existing MRI multi-view representation learning and advanced contrastive fine-tuning methods.

Title: Agent Authenticator: Passkey-Grade, Hardware-Bound Authentication for AI Agents

  • Authors: Harish Santhanalakshmi Ganesan
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19536
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19536
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19536]] Agent Authenticator: Passkey-Grade, Hardware-Bound Authentication for AI Agents(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19536)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: AI coding agents now push code, publish packages, call model APIs and operate cloud resources on behalf of developers. They authenticate to these services with the same bearer secrets humans use, personal access tokens, API keys and package-registry tokens, stored where the model, its subprocesses and any co-resident malware can read them. Incidents in 2025 and 2026, including prompt-injection attacks on coding agents and self-propagating supply-chain worms, show that this arrangement fails in predictable ways. Passkeys solved the analogous problem for humans, but they require a human gesture on every use and cannot be operated by an autonomous process. We present the Agent Authenticator, a design that gives agents passkey-grade authentication properties: per-service hardware-bound keys, no shared secrets, origin binding enforced by the runtime rather than the model, normalized attestation of the execution environment, human-signed scoped mandates, and cross-device human approval for high-risk actions. The same protocol serves agents on developer laptops and in cloud enclaves, and it deploys as a profile over existing mechanisms, MCP authorization, OAuth token exchange including the Identity Assertion JWT Authorization Grant, git and SSH, rather than as a new identity system. The authenticator also produces a signed, hash-chained record of each action it authorizes. We analyze the design against a threat model centered on prompt injection and same-host compromise, map it onto five documented incidents, and discuss its limits.

Title: Random Cut Forests, Games and Diffusion over Approximately Nested Anisotropic Local Geometry

  • Authors: Sudipto Guha
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19548
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19548
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19548]] Random Cut Forests, Games and Diffusion over Approximately Nested Anisotropic Local Geometry(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19548)
  • Keywords: interpretability, diffusion
  • Abstract: Just as the data in a dynamic stream changes, questions asked of the stream also change in intent: what to hold constant, which counterfactuals to consider, and so forth. Energy-based learning has been celebrated for its uniform approach to losses and estimation, but interpretability remains challenging. Tree-based methods, on the other hand, offer high interpretability but often rely on bespoke, disjointed estimators. Neither paradigm readily supports changing questions on streaming data. In this paper we take a step towards combining energy, interpretability, and streaming. Random cut forests retain an evolving geometry of the data. We study this geometry as the basis for diffusion dynamics, prediction, explanation, and games. We show that several existing scores are escape probabilities of random walks on the trees. The induced affinity is realized exactly by a sparse electrical network on each tree, giving a single energy calculus, a Hamiltonian, maintained along one path per update. This connects an estimate, its supporting evidence, and the response to intervention through the same boxes, gaps, masses, and paths. Different questions correspond to different clamp decisions on the energy. Fixing observed values gives harmonic prediction with Gaussian uncertainty. Fixing a partition gives normalized cuts whose cost is attributed exactly to oriented faces. Releasing clamps gives coalition games, in which collusive displacement measures the field response. With noise, the same energy gives diffusion dynamics whose relaxation rates are read from the boxes. Axis-parallel cuts localize at a rate set by affine rather than ambient dimension, and this control passes to the averaged energy. Across a forest the boxes are only approximately nested; common reference observations align the trees. A rotating star with added noise illustrates the measurements.

  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) are designed to enable globally parallel generation. To address this, we propose SemMask, a novel fine-tuning paradigm that leverages a semantic-prioritized progressive masking strategy to encourage the model to decode high-information semantic tokens early and globally, thereby enhancing its global planning and parallel generation capabilities.   • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: We study the sample complexity of the empirical planning or plug-in approach for reinforcement learning (RL) with a generative model. Title: Risk-Averse Decision-Focused Learning: Minimizing CVaR of Regret

  • Authors: Jayanta Mandi, Senne Berden, Victor Bucarey, Concepcion Dominguez, Tias Guns
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19575
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19575

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Many combinatorial optimization (CO) problems involve parameters that are uncertain when the problem must be solved. Most existing DFL methods are risk-neutral and minimize the expected regret. When instantiated with SPO+, the resulting objective admits a generalization bound on the CVaR of the true regret. Experiments on knapsack, portfolio, shortest-path, and energy-aware scheduling problems show substantial reductions in tail regret when the uncertain parameters exhibit heterogeneous tail behavior.

Title: Explicit Regularization for Flow Matching

  • Authors: Lars Ruthotto, Eran Treister
  • Subjects: cs.LG, math.NA
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19587
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19587
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19587]] Explicit Regularization for Flow Matching(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19587)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Learning the underlying probability distribution from samples is a core problem in statistics and applied mathematics. Generative models based on diffusion and flow matching learn a velocity field whose flow transports a known distribution to the data distribution. They produce novel, realistic samples, although the empirical learning problem is ill posed near the data distribution. Its minimizer transports the samples onto the training data, and small perturbations of the data change the velocity field strongly near the end of the flow. The observed generalization is often attributed to implicit regularization by the network parameterization, early stopping of the training, and the time integration during sampling. Explicit regularization has advanced the theory and computation of many inverse problems and has recently been proposed to prevent memorization in generative models. With a regularizer on the magnitude of the velocity, however, the field approaches the memorizing velocity unless the regularization parameter grows to infinity fast enough, and it then stays bounded by a constant that is small only for large parameters. We show that penalizing the derivatives of order $k$ of the velocity renders the $D$-dimensional empirical problem well posed uniformly in time if $2k > D$ and the training points are in general position. Our experiments in one and two dimensions discretize the velocity with splines and use direct solvers for the regularized least-squares problems to exclude optimization error. Refining the discretization in space and time changes the error of the Hessian-regularized flows only marginally, whereas without the regularizer the error tends to that of memorization. These flows generalize, with errors comparable to or lower than those of well-tuned kernel density estimates.

  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.MA
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19598

  • Abstract: Current multi-agent authorization architectures prioritize system integrity over human outcomes. Intent has no formal definition, so no check against it can be definitive. Systems should instead report an openly probabilistic estimate and scale their caution with the risk of the action. Second, we argue that dynamic trust scores do not survive multi-agent handoffs; transmitting algorithmic judgment across a company boundary without underlying evidence risks violating Contextual Integrity. Third, treating a safely halted workflow as a successful refusal is a category error if it strands the user; compensating actions must be bound directly to the capability token, not only to the planning layer. Finally, we hypothesize that frictionless automated recovery introduces a moral hazard, disincentivizing developers from building robust initial intent checks. We conclude by proposing an architectural synthesis to bridge the gap between deterministic security and human outcomes, alongside the fundamental theoretical limits that remain unresolved.

  • Authors: Muhammad Qasim Elahi, Mahsa Ghasemi, Murat Kocaoglu

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19634
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19634

  • Keywords: fair
  • Abstract: Many decision-making problems involve multiple objectives, where actions affect several performance metrics, fairness criteria, or safety constraints. We study multi-objective causal bandits with multiple reward nodes, where actions correspond to interventions on subsets of variables in a causal graph and performance is evaluated through Pareto optimality. Existing approaches to multi-objective bandits often rely on pairwise Pareto dominance or optimize a fixed scalarization. However, pairwise comparisons alone can fail to identify actions dominated by randomized policies, whose expected reward vectors lie in the convex hull of action-level rewards. Thus, an intervention may be suboptimal even if no single intervention dominates it, because it can be dominated by a randomized policy over other interventions. We formalize this policy-level phenomenon and show that it has consequences for both causal action-space reduction and online learning. First, we provide a necessary-and-sufficient graphical characterization of possibly Pareto- optimal minimal intervention sets. Our characterization yields a minimal, sound, and complete candidate intervention family from the graph alone, correcting prior formulations that may include intervention sets that are never Pareto-optimal under any compatible structural causal model. Second, over this reduced intervention space, we design an online UCB-style algorithm that eliminates actions using dominance tests against convex combinations of other actions, and prove logarithmic gap-dependent Pareto regret. Finally, we study constrained multi-objective causal bandits, where feasibility and optimality may be realized only with randomized policies, and develop an online UCB-style constrained bandit algorithm with sublinear regret and constraint-violation guarantees.

  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19649]] LoopFill: Pipelined Multi-Token Speculative Decoding for Looped Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19649)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Looped Transformers improve parameter efficiency by repeatedly applying the same Transformer layers. During autoregressive decoding, however, generating each token requires several sequential passes through these layers, leading to high decoding latency. Pipelined self-speculative methods use shallow-loop predictions to process tokens at different loops in the same batch, but each token must reach the drafting depth before the next can enter the pipeline. We introduce LoopFill, a training-free speculative decoding algorithm for looped LLM that combines multi-token suffix drafts with shallow-loop predictions in a single draft tree. Both drafters contribute to one tree, so a suffix lookup can immediately extend a shallow prediction and several future tokens can enter the pipeline together. The shared block computes the highest-scoring draft candidates at different loop depths in one batch, and full-depth verification preserves the model's autoregressive output distribution. Across seven benchmarks, LoopFill's average speedup over greedy autoregressive decoding is $5.85\times$ on Ouro-2.6B and $5.13\times$ on Huginn-3.5B. The speedups exceed those of LoopAhead, the strongest baseline, by \(40\%\) on Ouro-2.6B and \(25\%\) on Huginn-3.5B.

Title: BadGCL: Towards Persistent Backdoor Attack on Graph Continual Learning Systems

  • Authors: Dong Li, Yufei Han, Xintao Wu, Xujiang Zhao, Wei Cheng, Chen Zhao
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19656
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19656
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19656]] BadGCL: Towards Persistent Backdoor Attack on Graph Continual Learning Systems(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19656)
  • Keywords: security, defense, attack, robust
  • Abstract: Backdoor attacks pose a persistent security threat to machine learning systems, yet their behavior under graph continual learning (GCL) remains underexplored. Conventional graph backdoor attacks often lose effectiveness as models are updated across successive tasks, creating the impression that continual updates naturally weaken malicious associations. This work reveals a contrasting vulnerability: knowledge-preservation mechanisms in GCL can also preserve early injected backdoors when the poisoned update is compatible with the clean-task signal being retained. For graph data, this risk is amplified by structural triggers, which are embedded into the message-passing paths of graph neural networks (GNNs) and can induce stable representation shifts across tasks. To expose this risk, we propose BadGCL, a model-agnostic framework for persistent backdoor attacks under a strict early-stage intervention setting. BadGCL learns topology-aware structural triggers using a gradient mutual-information objective that couples poisoned gradients with clean-task optimization signals, together with a propagation consistency objective that stabilizes trigger-induced effects through graph message passing. We provide theoretical analysis showing that gradient compatibility and propagation stability jointly support long-term backdoor persistence under benign continual updates. Extensive experiments across task-IL and class-IL protocols, four GCL benchmarks, and diverse victim systems show that BadGCL achieves persistent attack effectiveness with limited clean-utility degradation, substantially outperforming graph and continual-learning backdoor baselines and remaining robust against representative defenses.

The proposed framework enables stochastic closure modeling in realistic and challenging scenarios of complex dynamical systems in mechanics and engineering problems, where non-Gaussian statistics of model-form uncertainties are non-negligible (e.g., due to the nonlinearities of the system dynamics and/or the observations) and true closure data are not available, for both offline learning and online assimilation.

  • Abstract: How much must a predictor's scores change when a community's elicited fairness standard changes? For scores satisfying a source standard, we characterize the exact cost of retaining that standard during adaptation: runs of consecutive target constraints along shortest paths determine the optimal amplification factor. Its sharp worst case is $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$, and a full cost curve describes finite target-only repair budgets. The analysis separates the information needed for a substantial adaptation benefit from the precision needed for nearly optimal movement. Elicitation studies and training comparisons examine tolerance choice and the relation between numerical compliance and predictive utility.

Title: Multiaccuracy as an Error Benchmark for Learning with Distribution Shift

  • Authors: Konstantinos Stavropoulos
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.DS
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19701
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19701
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19701]] Multiaccuracy as an Error Benchmark for Learning with Distribution Shift(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19701)
  • Keywords: fair
  • Abstract: Agnostic learning has a well-developed theory of computational and statistical complexity. For learning with distribution shift, even the choice of an appropriate error benchmark remains challenging. Testable learning with distribution shift (TDS) was introduced to develop computational foundations for this setting: using labeled training data and unlabeled test data, the learner must either produce a predictor with low test error or detect distribution shift. However, standard agnostic TDS guarantees allow a constant-factor amplification of the optimum error even when the training and test distributions coincide. This yields a TDS formulation that becomes equivalent to multiaccuracy when there is no shift. Using a recent nontrivial reduction from agnostic learning to multiaccurate prediction from prior work, we obtain the first TDS framework that formally generalizes agnostic learning through an efficient reduction preserving the training marginal. The framework retains existing algorithmic upper bounds and inherits computational lower bounds from agnostic learning. Moreover, we establish a direct connection between our model and identity testing, yielding the first statistical characterization of agnostic TDS learning, in terms of Rademacher complexity. More broadly, we argue that shift multiaccuracy might be of independent interest in unsupervised domain adaptation, as it leads to sharper out-of-distribution generalization guarantees, and can be minimized directly, since its definition does not involve the unobserved test labels.

Title: Exploration of Reinforcement Learning in Generative Recommendation Models

  • Authors: Pengfei Zheng, Wuchao Li, Shiyao Wang, Honghui Bao, Hongyi Xie, Jiaxin Deng, Xingmei Wang, Jinghao Zhang, Yufei Ye, Ruiming Tang, Guorui Zhou, Han Li, Kun Gai, Defu Lian
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19742
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19742
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19742]] Exploration of Reinforcement Learning in Generative Recommendation Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19742)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Traditional cascaded recommender systems balance accuracy and efficiency over large item spaces, but misaligned stage-wise objectives hinder end-to-end optimization. Generative recommendation unifies these stages in an autoregressive framework, providing a natural interface for reinforcement learning (RL) to optimize the entire generation process. However, directly applying RL often leads to unstable optimization and degraded recommendation quality. We revisit Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) and other ratio-based objectives through a unified logit-gradient view. We identify three issues: (i) clipped surrogates create dead-gradient regions, under-utilizing samples; (ii) negative-advantage updates can become effectively unbounded as importance ratios grow, allowing a small subset of negatives to dominate optimization; and (iii) ratio-based control lacks sensitivity to absolute policy confidence, so updates on high-confidence cases may fail to vanish. Motivated by this diagnosis, we propose \emph{Gradient-Bounded Policy Optimization} (GBPO), a simple objective with stop-gradient normalization that is simultaneously (a) fully gradient-active, (b) sign-symmetrically bounded for both positive and negative updates, and (c) explicitly confidence-weighted under current policy probabilities. This ensures that updates gracefully vanish for $\pi\!\to\!1$ positives and $\pi\!\to\!0$ negatives. Across the evaluated settings, GBPO achieves competitive recommendation performance with more stable training than competing RL methods.

CompGuard achieves a 100% Defense Block Rate (0.0% to 0.8% pre-teardown Net ASR) at 61.4 microseconds interposition overhead, preserves a 98.0% solve rate on benign developer workflows, and triggers zero false alarms across 615 real cloud diagnostic calls.

Title: PISG: Constraint-Aligned Signal Amplification for Diffusion-Based Combinatorial Optimization

  • Authors: Yuming Zhang, Xianchen Zhou, Hongxia Wang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, math.OC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19768

  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Diffusion-based solvers have become a prominent approach to neural combinatorial optimization. At inference time, they assign each decision variable a prediction score. A greedy decoder ranks candidates by these scores and checks hard constraints to ensure feasibility. Top-ranked candidates should be both promising and jointly feasible, making prediction-constraint alignment critical. When this alignment is weak, greedy decoding skips constraint-violating candidates and falls back to lower-ranked alternatives, degrading solution quality. Existing test-time methods improve search, sampling, or decoding, but do not directly strengthen this alignment before decoding. We observe that trained denoisers already carry constraint-aligned structural signals. Building on this observation, we propose Perturbed Instance-Structure Guidance (PISG), a training-free method that amplifies these signals at test time. At each denoising step, PISG constructs a structure-degraded prediction and extrapolates the unperturbed prediction away from it. The resulting guided prediction improves candidate rankings before constrained decoding. Signal analysis shows that this guidance concentrates on constraint-critical variables rather than uniformly rescaling scores. Under greedy decoding and without downstream refinement, PISG improves all 16 TSP solver-scale pairs across DIFUSCO, T2T, FastT2T, and StruDiCO, and all four MIS-ER solvers, with MIS-ER solved-cost gains up to +4.8%. The gains persist on most large-scale settings after 2-opt, and a preliminary study extends PISG to a Transformer-based solver. These results identify prediction-constraint alignment as an effective test-time enhancement direction for diffusion-based combinatorial optimization. Code is available at this https URL.

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Embedding-based one-to-one association is common in vision and data integration: detections are associated with tracks and records are linked across databases. When target embeddings are costly, one can acquire them selectively and stop once a certificate proves that the current assignment is optimal. We give such a certificate for unit-norm embeddings. All 96 certificate simulations and 120 online selective timings were exact on their own realization (largest objective gap $6.82\times10^{-13}$). At group size 8 the shared certificate acquires on average 60% of candidate-active targets, against 74% (mean centre), 69% (MEB) and 60% (zero centre): it beats the mean and MEB centres but not the zero centre, and savings concentrate in one benchmark (7% acquired). The prespecified runtime claim is contradicted: selective execution is +0.510 s slower per run (dataset-cluster 95% interval [+0.248, +0.772]). Uniform-microbatch re-encodings preserved the optimum in only 5 of 8 encoder-dataset cells, and 45 of 120 online runs disagreed with an eager cache at near-ties.

Title: When Backdoors Can Be Switched Off: Suppression-Aware Backdoor Attacks on Vision-Language-Action Models

  • Authors: Nur Afsa Syeda, Mohamed Elmahallawy
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19774
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19774
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19774]] When Backdoors Can Be Switched Off: Suppression-Aware Backdoor Attacks on Vision-Language-Action Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19774)
  • Keywords: security, attack, steal
  • Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models map visual observations, robot states, and natural-language instructions to robotic actions, but their multimodal interfaces introduce new security risks. Existing VLA backdoor attacks primarily study trigger-based activation and stealthy action manipulation, leaving conditional suppression of malicious behavior underexplored. We investigate suppression-aware backdoors, which combine conjunctive trigger activation with instruction-conditioned suppression. We instantiate this design through three variants: Cross-Modal Trigger, combining robot-state and spatially separated visual triggers; Self-Suppressing Trigger, requiring a state trigger and a [DISRUPT] instruction token for activation, with [RESTORE] token suppressing the attack; and Multi-Trigger, additionally requiring a visual trigger while retaining suppression even when physical triggers persist. Experiments with OpenVLA on LIBERO further show that the attacks transfer to a 7B-parameter policy, evading STRIP and action-distribution-shift detection. Our findings establish conditional suppression as an important dimension for evaluating backdoor threats in VLA policies.

Title: Multi-Branch Transformer

  • Authors: Gaotang Li, Lingjie Chen, Chunyuan Deng, Zhiwei Xu, Hongzheng Chen, Christopher De Sa, Jingrui He, Yingheng Wang, Hanghang Tong
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19800
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19800
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19800]] Multi-Branch Transformer(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19800)
  • Keywords: robust, transformer
  • Abstract: Transformer language models are commonly scaled by making their blocks wider or stacking them deeper. In this work, we explore a complementary path: branching, the parallel composition of full Transformer blocks. We introduce the Multi-Branch Transformer (MBT), which processes the input through shared initial layers, executes independently parameterized branches of sequential blocks in parallel, and fuses their outputs before shared final layers. At comparable training compute, we study three MBT variants: Wide-Shallow preserves block width, Narrow-Deep preserves sequential depth, and Balanced interpolates between them. In controlled pretraining experiments at four mixture-of-experts scales (0.8B to 2.1B total parameters), 2-branch Wide-Shallow MBTs consistently outperform sequential Transformers under matched parameter and training-compute budgets. Balanced MBTs improve further and retain comparable quality as the number of branches increases. To improve inference efficiency at long contexts, we introduce Joint-KV, which constructs a shared KV cache from branch representations at each depth. Our final recipe combines Balanced MBTs with Joint-KV, supported by an inference engine that exploits branch grouping and shared-cache reuse. Fitted scaling curves indicate that standard Transformers require up to 1.26x as much training body compute to match our final models' validation loss. These models reduce full-model KV-cache storage by up to 34% and deliver up to 51.4% higher decoding throughput on a single H200 GPU. Ablation studies show that branching yields gains beyond changes in width and depth, enables an effective KV-sharing strategy (Joint-KV), remains robust across fusion functions, and performs best in the middle layers. These findings show that branching offers a practical path for scaling language models toward both better modeling quality and faster inference.

Title: Conditional Optimal Transport and Triangular Maps for Imitation Learning

  • Authors: Mohammad Al-Jarrah, Yuhang Mei, Karen Leung, Bamdad Hosseini, Amirhossein Taghvaei
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19815
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19815
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19815]] Conditional Optimal Transport and Triangular Maps for Imitation Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19815)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: We introduce a unified formulation of imitation learning from demonstrations as a conditional sampling problem solved by triangular transport: a policy is the second block of a triangular map that transports a reference distribution to the conditional distribution of the action chunk given the observation history. Unlike in Diffusion Policy, where it is a fixed Gaussian, the reference becomes a design variable; we take it to be the empirical distribution of the demonstrated action chunks and introduce three instances of the formulation: an optimal transport policy (OTP), a flow matching policy (FMP), and a stochastic interpolant policy (SIP). On the Push-T and robomimic Square tasks, the three policies are competitive in task performance with Diffusion Policy and Consistency Policy while using considerably smaller networks and shorter training, and the informative reference accounts for a substantial part of their performance when few network evaluations are used. The three instances offer different trade-offs rather than a single best method: OTP generates an action chunk with a single evaluation of the smallest network (0.23 million parameters on Push-T) and is suitable for settings in which inference time and model size matter, whereas SIP attains the highest scores among the proposed policies at a higher cost.

Title: Foundation Pareto Flow Policy for Multi-Objective Reinforcement Learning

  • Authors: Zhanjiang Yang, Lijun Sun, Yueming Li, Meng Li, Yijun Yang

  • Authors: Xu Yang, Gady Agam

  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Feature pyramids provide dense predictors with features at several spatial resolutions. Surprisingly, locations on the finest output grid allocate approximately 75\% of their attention to features two levels coarser, rather than to the current or adjacent levels. Even on the same output grid, the balance shifts toward coarser features as object size increases. Compared with FPN, AGPF improves all 19 object detection and all seven instance segmentation configurations evaluated on COCO.   • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: A language model is a complex system whose behavior is determined jointly by the Transformer and the corpus, yet how the representation geometry in the Transformer relates to the actual behavior of language models remains open. We analyze how the representation geometry changes along the forward propagation of actual language models and characterize the representation geometry of a document at a given layer by two collective coordinates, document distinguishability and document dimension. On the phase diagram spanned by these collective coordinates, inference is represented as a flow along depth. We find that, for untrained models, depending on their initialization scale, most of the 156 inference flows move toward the collapse and noise regions of the phase diagram.   • Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) are vulnerable to backdoor attacks introduced through data poisoning, making effective backdoor detection a critical security challenge. In this paper, we uncover a novel connection between VLM hallucinations and backdoor behaviors and exploit this connection for backdoor detection. Our key insight is that poisoned training data induces strong language priors toward the backdoor target token, while such priors are also a major source of hallucinations. This suggests that enhancing hallucinations can expose hidden backdoor-related priors. Specifically, backdoored VLMs tend to hallucinate backdoor target tokens more frequently across diverse inputs, while the hallucinated tokens of clean VLMs are largely random and exhibit no stable target-specific bias. Based on this observation, we propose HalluProbe, which enhances hallucinations during output decoding and measures the distributional consistency of hallucinated tokens to identify backdoored VLMs. We evaluate HalluProbe against six representative VLM backdoor attacks across three models. Experimental results demonstrate that HalluProbe can effectively detect backdoored VLMs with an accuracy exceeding 90%, outperforming existing backdoor detection methods.

Title: Recovery Gone Too Far: Understanding and Repairing Risky Fallback in LLM Agents

  • Authors: Liwen Wang, Jiangrong Wu, Zongjie Li, Zongyi Lyu, Shuai Wang, Shing-Chi Cheung
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19886
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19886

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents interact with their environments through tools, reading repositories, running builds, and pushing code. When a tool fails with an ordinary error, such as a rate limit or a timeout, the agent can fall back to a substitute tool and still complete the task. However, the same fallback that completes a task in one environment can cross an action boundary in another. For example, after a push is rejected, a forced push in one repository replaces only the agent's own commits, while in a different repository it also removes another author's commits. We call such a fallback a risky fallback. To understand this phenomenon, we conduct an empirical study on 304 scenarios that span eight domains and cover 55 real CVEs. All four mainstream models we evaluate exhibit risky fallback, with action-boundary violations in up to 96.8% of risky scenarios. We further evaluate three mainstream mitigations and find that none of them is effective against risky fallback. We then present nrepair. At each call to the substitute tool, nrepair scores the agent's context with a monitor whose threshold is calibrated on normal runs alone; when the monitor triggers, it withholds the call once and returns the decision to the agent. nrepair reduces action-boundary violations on all four models, by up to 76.0% (from 67.5% to 16.2%), at a negligible runtime cost. On most models, the normal success rate drops by less than 7%. We also evaluate nrepair on two production agents, Claude Code and Codex, and find that it is equally effective on both.

Title: The Vocabulary Tax: Safely Trimming Unused Tokens from LLMs for On-Device Use

  • Authors: Sattaya Singkul
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19913
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19913

  • Abstract: Open large language models (LLMs) and speech models ship vocabularies of up to 262k tokens, and every user downloads the tables those tokens index (the input embedding, an untied output head and, in recent on-device models, per-layer embeddings), whatever languages they need. We call the bytes in rows a deployment never uses the vocabulary tax and measure it, without downloading weights, for 1,244 generative and speech-recognition models on the Hugging Face Hub. For a Thai and English deployment it is a median 4.4% of model bytes below 0.5B parameters and 2.1% above 8B, and 1.93 GB in Gemma 4 E2B: it falls hardest on the small models that edge devices run. Keeping only the rows of the deployment's writing systems is exact. Keeping the tokens seen in the user's own text removes much more, a median 29.0% below 0.5B, but the usual way of doing it breaks BPE tokenizers: it drops the intermediate tokens that merges build on, so new text is split differently 1.9 times as often as the standard check reports. We repair this with merge closure, at a cost of 5.9% more rows, and add a trim certificate: a Good-Turing estimate, from the trimming text alone, of how much new text the trimmed tokenizer will split differently (correlation 0.996 over 300 settings in 22 languages) and of how much more text a target needs. Without test data our method never exceeds a 2% target, while prior keep rules checked on test data exceed it in 9-13 of 44 settings. On six models of 270M to 3B parameters in 11 languages, trimmed models keep their predictions: where the certificate passes, a median 89.7% of rows is removed and bits per character rise by at most 2.0%. The census, the trimming and the certificate are released as an open Python package, and the per-model tax for 41 languages as an open dataset.

Title: CHERI under Fault Injection: An Emulation-Based Security Evaluation of Capability Hardware Enhanced RISC Instructions

  • Authors: Eva Sophia Schreyer
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19915
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.19915
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.19915]] CHERI under Fault Injection: An Emulation-Based Security Evaluation of Capability Hardware Enhanced RISC Instructions(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19915)
  • Keywords: security, protect, attack
  • Abstract: Memory-safety vulnerabilities continue to dominate real-world exploit chains despite decades of research into software mitigations. CHERI is a hardware architecture that enforces spatial and temporal memory safety by augmenting pointers with hardware-checked metadata, thus preventing software-based exploitation, but its threat model explicitly excludes fault injection. This thesis investigates whether targeted fault injection can undermine CHERI's memory-safety guarantees in a pure-capability CHERI-RISC-V environment. Two fault classes are considered: bit flips corrupting capability metadata, and fault-induced check skipping on capability store instructions. We implement fault injection primitives in a QEMU-based emulation environment, which enables deterministic, repeatable experiments without specialised hardware. With them, we demonstrate four attack scenarios: buffer overflows, control-flow hijacking, data-only attacks, and use-after-free. Each succeeds with one or two targeted faults (lower bounds under the emulation-based setting). Check skipping offers an alternative route to control-flow hijacking and data-only attacks, with a minimum of one fault. These results show that CHERI's guarantees depend on the physical integrity of capability metadata, the uninterrupted execution of metadata checks, and the integrity of the revocation bitmap: a single targeted fault can circumvent spatial safety, temporal safety, or control-flow integrity. CHERI still raises the cost of exploitation, since fault injection is a prerequisite for every attack and the sentry mechanism forces an attacker to supply a valid tagged capability or spend a second fault restoring the tag. Based on these findings, we propose mitigations: ECC-protected capability memory, capability field authentication codes, redundant execution of security-critical checks, and revocation bitmap integrity verification.

  • Abstract: Knowledge-based visual question answering (KB-VQA) aims to combine visual information with external knowledge to answer user questions.   • Authors: Haoyang Hu, Masataka Seo, Liang Li, Yen-wei Chen
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20021

  • Abstract: Generative Refinement Networks (GRN) generate an image by repeatedly re-predicting and refining every token of a discrete latent. They reach high fidelity, but need dozens of Markov steps. We therefore train few-step GRNs on the distribution of their own samples. We use its own uniform-state corruption to perform distribution matching on discrete tokens, which lets a discrete generator learn by backpropagation, and we pair noise with samples in the manner of rectified flow. We find that the steps of a few-step GRN do not carry the same amount of information or the same repair load. We therefore exploit its discrete refinement during training and build few-step GRNs in three parts. Oversampling samples the student with more steps than it was trained for and refines its judge on these samples. Non-uniform sampling trains only the last step and hands it most of the information at sampling time. Refinement confidence keeping holds on to the tokens the student is already sure of. We train a fast class-conditional generator on ImageNet 256 and, with the same method, a 2B text-to-image model. On ImageNet 256, the 3-step FastGRN beats the FID of GRN. On a 2B binary-token text-to-image GRN, a 4-step student also nearly matches the GenEval of GRN-T2I 2B.

  • Keywords: robust, federate
  • Abstract: Federated learning trains a shared model from distributed client data. The deployment risk is typically specified through two approximations: each unknown local distribution is replaced by its empirical distribution, and the unknown deployment mixture across clients is replaced by empirical or prescribed training weights. Existing robust methods usually address one approximation at a time through local distributional ambiguity sets or worst-case client mixtures. However, joint treatment of both approximations remains underexplored. To address this gap, we construct a global ambiguity set formed by admissible mixtures of client-wise Wasserstein ambiguity sets, yielding a distributionally robust optimization formulation. The construction allows client-dependent ambiguity radii, preserves client-wise separability of the objective function, and yields a high-probability out-of-sample guarantee for the resulting robust problem. We then introduce a penalty-based approximation of this formulation and develop a communication-efficient algorithm called BiDRO-FedAvg, for which we establish a convergence guarantee. We validate the proposed framework through a market-demand prediction simulation.

In Study 1, on 48 sharded math tasks, retaining its replies lowered final success by 0.21 for GPT-4o-mini and 0.29 for Mistral-7B (Holm-adjusted p = 0.026 and 0.004); Llama-4 Scout showed the same direction, not significant. With the full history, accuracy fell from about 0.6 to 0.1 within twenty turns at matched difficulty, whereas a solver-verified state summary kept it 0.52-0.59 higher (Holm-adjusted p less than 0.001). Consistency and logical coherence fell far less than accuracy, and stability rose. None of these metrics, a paired readiness measure or calibrated single-stream drift detectors gave robust, model-general early warning, although in Study 2 the repeat-based metrics were complete only for GPT-4o-mini. In the monitors we tested, a single 100-turn stream had power only for very large drops. Systems built on LLMs should keep task state outside the transcript and monitor across sessions.

  • Abstract: Masked modeling is the dominant self-supervised paradigm for point-cloud videos. However, it leaks the information it is meant to predict, whether it reconstructs masked points as in MAE-style methods or predicts their latent features. Because point-cloud tokens are anchored at points sampled from each frame, querying a target at its own position reveals where its geometry has moved, in reconstruction and latent prediction alike. For latent predictors, random spatio-temporal masks introduce a further shortcut, as visible frames on both sides of each target allow interpolation rather than forecasting. Each target is queried only with its last-seen position and timestamp, while a latent action learned without annotations is meant to capture the part of the future that the past leaves undetermined. On HOI4D action segmentation with a P4Transformer backbone, PointWM improves F1@50 over training from scratch by 24.9 offline and 29.1 under strictly causal inference. These results show the promise of latent world models as a leakage-free pretraining paradigm for point-cloud this http URL code is publicly available at this https URL.

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20115

  • Abstract: Decision models are a new class of language model that, instead of generating text, choose one option per question from a fixed list and return a probability for every option. They should be validated in the configuration in which they will be used, restricted to the fields in which they are reliable, and combined with other methods for numeric values.

Title: Centered Ablation: Why Last-Layer Directional Ablation Under-Fires or Collapses, and a Closed-Form Fix

  • Authors: Quy-Anh Dang, Chris Ngo
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20148
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20148
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20148]] Centered Ablation: Why Last-Layer Directional Ablation Under-Fires or Collapses, and a Closed-Form Fix(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20148)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Directional ablation removes a hidden state's component along a "behaviour direction" while a language model runs. It is the standard way to switch a behaviour such as refusal off, but it is erratic: on some models it barely fires, on others the text collapses into repetition. We show why, and fix it. At the last residual stream, ablation is exactly a logit bias: a fixed vocabulary footprint scaled by a gate, the cosine between the hidden state and the direction. The gate is measured from the origin, but last-layer states share a large common mode far from it, so a direction that overlaps the common mode fires on benign text and under-fires on its targets. Centered Ablation (CA) projects the common mode out of the direction, in closed form. Across 8 models, CA raises attack success by 29 and 46 points where the standard recipe under-fires, cuts collapse on Gemma-2-9B from 50\% to 1\%, and preserves 12 to 40 points more benign answers on high-overlap models, 39 to 46 more than the affine offset of prior work. Two numbers computed before generation predict, on models the analysis had not seen, which gain faithfulness and which gain attack success, and a collapse index marks when text degenerates. We also show that two standard safety judges score collapsed text in opposite directions, which can fabricate a 46-point effect.

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Geometric deep learning has made available a large variety of structurally equivariant neural network architectures, meaning a particular transformation of their input causes a predictable transformation of their output. But structural equivariance is a very strong inductive bias that can limit performance if chosen poorly. In contrast, choosing an architecture with low geometric bias necessitates data augmentation to obtain correct behavior; extending the training process. We propose a novel neural network architecture that is soft equivariant; we select a set of symmetry groups and during training the network will become equivariant only to those symmetry groups that are appropriate given the training data. We show how our architecture and the fingerprinting process work in the case of an image classification task.

  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LG

  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: LLM-as-a-judge guardrails are often controlled primarily through system prompts, yet standard prompt optimization methods target average performance rather than explicit safety constraints. This paper studies system-prompt selection as a constrained optimization problem: maximize benign pass rate while keeping the missed-attack rate below a user-specified level {\alpha} with finite-sample statistical control. The framework combines LLM-based judging, automatic prompt generation, and distribution-free risk certification. This work instantiates it in two procedures: CPS and CRISP. CRISP is especially useful when fixed-pool search fails to produce certifiable high-utility candidates.

  • Authors: Ke An, Liang Chen, Yupei Wang
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20183
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20183

  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Recent methods try to inspect and revise masks to mitigate referential ambiguity and localization errors caused by complex spatial relations in language-guided remote sensing segmentation. However, these solutions often lead to Revision Degradation: revising missing regions may introduce errors into previously correct ones. Even a globally better mask can omit correct regions found in inferior masks. We term this Evidence Fragmentation: correct target evidence is scattered across imperfect masks. All masks generated by the model at this time are suboptimal. We propose GeoReVis, which unifies revision guided by execution feedback, Candidate Selection, and Cross-Mask Evidence Consolidation. It learns fine-grained revision from real actions that improve segmentation when executed and learns to select a better mask through quality comparisons between actual candidates, reducing the risk of degradation during revision. The selected whole mask can still discard useful regions. GeoReVis therefore retains all candidates across rounds, partitions their spatial disagreements into composable regions, and jointly reconstructs regions conditioned on the full instruction and context. This makes complementary and fragmentary evidence reusable regardless of its source candidate's overall quality, yielding improved segmentation. We further build a data pipeline to generate fine-grained multi-round segmentation trajectories and derive supervision for revision actions, candidate selection, and region composition from the model's own outputs, extending training beyond whole-mask IoU supervision. We also propose ReVis-Eval, a benchmark protocol evaluating multi-round revision and cross-round evidence consolidation accuracy. Experiments on ReVis-Eval and 5 benchmarks for remote sensing reasoning and referring segmentation demonstrate the effectiveness of GeoReVis.

Title: Understanding Users' Security, Privacy, and Trust Concerns in Healthcare AI Systems

  • Abstract: Modern multi-camera 3D detectors increasingly rely on large Vision Transformer (ViT) image encoders, making repeated full-depth encoding a dominant inference cost even though adjacent driving frames are highly redundant. Title: Scaling large language model-assisted line-of-therapy adjudication with human-in-the-loop evidence-calibrated selection

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have enabled the ability to scale clinical adjudication tasks traditionally dependent on exhaustive manual review. Title: GNN-Based Deep Reinforcement Learning for Coastal Emergency Response under Extreme Water Levels
