Title: A Bleichenbacher bit-by-bit oracle attack from partial message knowledge

  • Authors: Juan Carlos García Escartín
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20277
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20277
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20277]] A Bleichenbacher bit-by-bit oracle attack from partial message knowledge(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20277)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Oracle attacks show the importance of a correct implementation of cryptographic standards when translated into code. A notable example is the Bleichenbacher attack on RSA with PKCS#1 v1.5 padding and all the improvements that have been suggested over the decades. This paper shows how an attacker knowing a few least significant bytes of an RSA message can take advantage of a server with an oracle side-channel to decrypt the full message bit by bit. We will consider two oracles that can be tested with chosen ciphertexts: one revealing whether the decrypted message starts with the 00 02 bytes and a second one revealing if the decrypted message is fully PKCS compliant. For an attacker knowing the three least significant bytes of the message, the first oracle provides an attack where one oracle call gives the next least significant bit of the message and the second an attack which requires, with high probability, a small constant number of oracle calls to the oracle, with a constant which depends on the length of the message and the desired probability of success. The attack divides the message bits into two sections, one with the known part and one with the unknown part, and then chooses transformations that will keep the unknown part, except for the last unknown bit, orders of magnitude below the integer coming from the known part. The resulting string either gives a valid padding or not depending on the value of the unknown bit. The attacker can minimize the number of oracle calls at the cost of some local computation. Finally, the same method is adapted to show how it can also be used to extract the few least significant bytes of the message with an increased cost where the main complexity is finding a second valid message with a correct padding starting from the original captured ciphertext.

Title: Protecting Safety during Reasoning Training with Low-Rank Fisher Regularisation

  • Authors: Neel Rajani, Ivan Titov
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20288
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20288
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20288]] Protecting Safety during Reasoning Training with Low-Rank Fisher Regularisation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20288)
  • Keywords: protect, large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models are trained to have safety guardrails, but these can be compromised while fine-tuning for new capabilities, such as reasoning. One way to mitigate this degradation is to modify the fine-tuning objective to preserve safety behaviour, but doing so effectively and efficiently remains challenging. We revisit Elastic Weight Consolidation (EWC), which penalises parameter updates based on sensitivity estimates from the diagonal of the Fisher information matrix (FIM). While EWC provides some protection, the diagonal approximation ignores interactions between parameters. We address this limitation by exploiting recent findings that safety-relevant behaviour is located in a low-rank subspace within models. Our method, Subspace-Projected Elastic Weight Consolidation (SP-EWC), projects gradients onto this subspace to capture non-diagonal curvature efficiently. We investigate three different ways of identifying the safety subspace: through safety fine-tuning updates (LoRA), safety-related activations (ActSVD), and contrastive representations of safety and utility data (cPCA). We find that this choice substantially affects performance, with SP-EWC using cPCA comparing favourably against existing methods by better maintaining safety guardrails without introducing excessive over-refusal.

Title: Where the Savings Go: The Limits of Hiding Cross-Tenant Cache Hits in LLM Serving

  • Authors: Bowen Sun, Chaowei Xiao
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20291
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20291
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20291]] Where the Savings Go: The Limits of Hiding Cross-Tenant Cache Hits in LLM Serving(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20291)
  • Keywords: defense, attack
  • Abstract: LLM serving engines cache prompt prefixes; a cache hit skips computation and returns faster, so a shared cache reveals whether others have sent a given text. Per-account salting closes this channel but forgoes cross-tenant reuse and isolates only whole accounts, even where access is redistributed or trust domains are subdivided below them. Defenses that share globally but hide hits promise reuse without relying on account boundaries. Hiding a hit requires everything tenants observe afterwards to be distributed as if it had missed. The defenses we examine target the hit-miss gap; we instead follow where the saved time goes. Step-based schedulers that never idle while work waits pass it in full to every request queued behind the hit, shifting each earlier by the same amount. An attacker within ordinary rate limits brackets a probe with two groups of listeners that each return one token, sends them in one short burst, and counts in how many steps they finish. Replaying a production trace on one vLLM instance at up to half capacity, a single probe detects cached texts of 256 tokens or more with advantage at least 0.98. Hiding only the hit request falls to one probe on vLLM and SGLang; random delays must reach about ten times the shift or more to cut the advantage to 0.1; narrowing what is shared gives no guarantee against this inference. Since any observable tenant benefit is also a signal, we prove that a rate-limited tenant's gain from content written by k other accounts is at most of order k times the leakage allowed, whatever the provider does with identities; at zero leakage, only the provider keeps the savings. Exact hiding is attainable in principle on one instance, but mechanisms that only pause or hold work know skipped work's duration only approximately, so under saturated prefill they must pad every step that may hold a hit, at a price that grows with time rather than with hits.

Title: PoCoAttn: Position-Content Collaborative Sparse Attention for Fast and High-Fidelity Video Generation

  • Authors: Jianwen Wu, Meiling Wang, Junkang Zhou, Yipeng Shen, Haoran You
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20307
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20307
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20307]] PoCoAttn: Position-Content Collaborative Sparse Attention for Fast and High-Fidelity Video Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20307)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Video diffusion transformers rely on 3D full attention over tens of thousands of spatiotemporal tokens, and its quadratic cost dominates inference latency. Sparse attention reduces this cost, but existing methods face a dilemma: static methods apply predefined masks and cannot capture input-dependent attention, whereas dynamic methods reconstruct the sparse mask repeatedly, paying online to rediscover positional structure that is largely stable across inputs. To this end, we propose PoCoAttn, a training-free sparse attention framework that separates input-stable from input-dependent attention and resolves the former offline and the latter online. PoCoAttn features: (1) A relative-offset analysis of 3D query-key pairs, which shows that video attention decomposes into an input-stable positional prior that carries most of the attention mass and a small content-dependent residual. (2) A position-content collaborative sparse attention mechanism, which retrieves positional keys from offline-calibrated tables, selects content keys online with a head-adaptive ratio between the two sources, and compensates for the discarded keys to approximate full attention. (3) A step-adaptive budget allocation algorithm, which models how early-step sparse attention errors are amplified along the denoising trajectory and derives the optimal sparsity budget per step. Extensive experiments across four video diffusion models and three tasks show that PoCoAttn consistently achieves a better speed-fidelity Pareto frontier than state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods, improving PSNR by 1.9 to 5.0 dB over baselines and delivering up to 4.1$\times$ attention and 2.0$\times$ denoising speedups over dense FlashAttention-3 attention.

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Vision-based navigation systems for orbit must be trained almost entirely on synthetic imagery, because real labelled images of a given spacecraft in flight rarely exist. (3) Once the backbone adapts, augmentation helps only in the direction it models: our photometric stack improved the diffusely lit lightbox domain from 1.17 to 0.99 rad on every seed but moved sunlamp no more than seed-to-seed variation, while with the backbone frozen it helped sunlamp more. Building on previous work about LayerNorm, Softmax, and their alternatives, we introduce Learnable Normalization States (LENS), a framework that reformulates LayerNorm as tiny MLPs by abstracting a common structure from related methods and extends this method to Softmax, which moves more of the computation in Transformers to GEMM as a hardware-friendly architecture.   • Keywords: interpretability, transformer
  • Abstract: Linear probing is a common technique in mechanistic interpretability despite the fact that the decodability of a given feature does not necessarily imply the network uses that feature. Intervention faithfulness, not merely decodability, is the appropriate criterion for whether a feature serves a defined active role in the model. Meanwhile, probe regularization has been used for feature discovery, but the impact of regularization on feature causality remains unclear. We study probe regularization systematically, starting with unregularized ordinary least squares (OLS) regression and considering increasing amounts of regularization via ridge regression. We show theoretically that OLS probes amplify low-variance directions in activation space that have spurious correlations with the target feature, whereas regularization reduces influence from these directions. Empirically, we find in a toy transformer and Llama-2-7B that intervention faithfulness increases up to a point, and then decreases again, and that the optimal regularization strength leads to features bearing striking similarity (in both direction and variance) to those learned by optimizing for intervention outcomes directly. Together, these results provide mechanistic justification for regularizing regression probes.

Title: JointQ: Joint Scale-and-Integer Quantization for Large Language Models

  • Authors: Keiji Kimura, Yuma Ichikawa
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20384
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20384
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20384]] JointQ: Joint Scale-and-Integer Quantization for Large Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20384)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: GPTQ is the de facto standard solver for layer-wise post-training quantization (PTQ) of large language models, yet the solver itself has received less attention than weight preprocessing and cross-layer objectives. We target a bottleneck of GPTQ: its greedy procedure optimizes continuous group scales separately from the assignment of discrete integers. Evaluating each integer candidate with its closed-form optimal scale has been used for per-channel quantization; JointQ extends it to group-wise quantization, where a row's scales are coupled through the Hessian, and applies it to refine the GPTQ solution. Each local-search move changes one integer and recomputes its group's optimal scale in closed form, accounting for the other groups through the full Hessian; all scales are periodically re-solved jointly. The search starts from a row-wise selection among scale-refitted candidates including the GPTQ solution, so unregularized JointQ never has a larger layer-wise calibration error than GPTQ. JointQ preserves the GPTQ output format and is thus a drop-in replacement. Across four models and four settings, JointQ improves six-task average zero-shot accuracy over vanilla GPTQ in 14 of 16 comparisons and perplexity in 12 of 16. The mean accuracy gain increases from 0.0044 in W4G128 to 0.1559 in W3 per-channel, and JointQ also improves average accuracy over GPTQ with MSE-based scale search and activation ordering in all eight per-channel comparisons, though rarely in G128. An ablation shows that JointQ's diagonal Tikhonov regularization mitigates severe W3 per-channel perplexity degradation, so the largest gains are not due to the local search alone. Overall, JointQ's primary empirical benefit is downstream accuracy preservation in challenging low-bit and large-group regimes. Code is available in the OneComp library at this https URL.

  • Abstract: Execution traces help diagnose AI-agent failures, but sharing them can expose customer identities. Title: Attention Sinks as Spectral Spikes: A Mechanism Analysis of Gated Attention

  • Authors: Seojin Kim, Yehjin Shin, Noseong Park
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20403
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20403
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20403]] Attention Sinks as Spectral Spikes: A Mechanism Analysis of Gated Attention(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20403)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Transformer attention exhibits attention sinks, where a small subset of source tokens absorbs disproportionate attention mass across layers. Recent work shows that post-SDPA output gating strongly reduces attention-sink behavior, while separate studies link attention sinks to gradient sinks and massive activations through backward training dynamics. Yet how the same gate affects forward sink structure, backward gradient concentration, and training-time representation signatures remains unclear. We address this gap by formulating attention sinks as sink-induced low-rank modes in token-space covariance, with directions aligned to high-mass attention columns. Under this view, post-SDPA gating acts as a mode-dependent contraction: it attenuates sink-induced spectral energy in the forward pass and locally reduces value-path gradients routed through sink columns in the backward pass. Empirically, random-matrix diagnostics show that gating reduces outlier mass, weakens sink-subspace alignment, and increases effective rank, while backward analyses show active-gate attenuation of first-token value gradients. Training-time diagnostics further show that gating suppresses the co-emergence of attention concentration, value-gradient amplification, and representation dominance.

Title: Latent Feedback Is a Compute and Data Amplifier

  • Authors: Benhao Huang, Chaitanya Dwivedi, Xuezhe Ma, Alexander Moreno, Hector Liu
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20404
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20404
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20404]] Latent Feedback Is a Compute and Data Amplifier(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20404)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: When high-quality data runs out, a common practice is to train on it for several epochs, but each repeat is worth less than the last. Latent feedback, which feeds each token's top-layer state into the next token's input, is another way to spend that compute, yet it has not been tested against a vanilla Transformer that spends the same compute on more epochs. We run this test across a ladder of model sizes, unique data and epochs, tuning both models at every size and amount of unique data. Latent feedback makes every FLOP and every token count for more except when unique data is scarce relative to model size, a regime that today's models are far from. We find that latent feedback serves as an amplifier: with unique data fixed, it reaches a Transformer's loss with less compute; with epochs fixed, it reaches that loss with less unique data. Furthermore, both savings grow with unique data per parameter and with repetition, and downstream tasks agree. To go beyond our grid, we fit one scaling law to both models, in which latent feedback enters as a Transformer with more parameters; it predicts that both savings keep growing with unique data and repetition. Fitted on models from 60M to 271M parameters, the law predicts the losses at a held-out 778M size to within 0.3% and the gap between the two models to within 0.05%. At most, latent feedback saves 48% of the compute and 47% of the unique data. When data, not compute, is the limit, latent feedback is the better use of a FLOP for a model of a given size.

  • Abstract: Abstention, the withholding of a response by a large language model (LLM) under uncertainty or safety risks, is essential for trustworthy models.   • Abstract: Text-to-image (T2I) self-correction combines a vision-language model (VLM) critic that diagnoses errors and proposes instructions with an image editor that executes them. A small human evaluation confirms the ranking in estimated net corrections but suggests a smaller gain than detector-based evaluation. LEDGE thus distinguishes two limits of self-correction: knowing what to fix and being able to fix it.

  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20454
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20454

  • Abstract: Latent multi-agent communication directly transfers Key-Value (KV) cache tensors between transformer-based agents, bypassing text serialization entirely. To address this vulnerability, we propose KVMark, a training-free, zero-bit KV-cache watermarking framework to verify inter-agent handoff integrity. Empirical evaluation on a four-agent latent pipeline demonstrates $100\%$ detection across $1{,}500$ handoffs with a $0.0\%$ false positive rate ($\text{AUROC}=1.0$), $100\%$ layer-level tamper localization ($17/17$ attacked layers identified with $0/19$ false positives), and no statistically significant reasoning accuracy degradation across four benchmarks ($N{=}300$--$500$). Furthermore, the framework enables multi-hop cascade verification to isolate compromised agents (500/500 detection at three hops), withstands adaptive white-box adversaries and the Out-of-Sight DiffMean attack (up to $\alpha=8.0$), and generalizes seamlessly to Qwen3-8B without re-tuning (1,500/1,500 TPR).

Title: Designing Deceptive Model Organisms that Successfully Evade Auditing

  • Authors: Vasisht Duddu, Mark Wang, Lipeng He, Tim G. J. Rudner
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20459
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20459
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20459]] Designing Deceptive Model Organisms that Successfully Evade Auditing(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20459)
  • Keywords: security, robust
  • Abstract: AI models can exhibit deceptive behaviors such as strategically underperforming on evaluations (sandbagging), faking alignment, or covertly acting on behalf of a hidden principal. Safe deployment therefore requires auditing techniques that can reliably detect such behaviors. To develop and test these techniques, we can train model organisms (MOs): models that exhibit deceptive behaviors and serve as realistic evaluation testbeds. However, for such testbeds to be informative, MOs must actively anticipate and resist auditing---a gap in prior work that can lead to overestimation of auditing effectiveness and underestimation of safety risks. We address this gap by designing MOs that capture worst-case deceptive behavior and evade high-effort auditing. Focusing on sandbagging, we frame MO design as a security game between an auditor and a deceptive model and identify three design requirements for sandbagging MOs: (i) effectiveness (underperforming on targeted capabilities), (ii) utility preservation (maintaining performance on all other capabilities), and (iii) robustness (resisting auditing). We propose three MOs: two optimized against blackbox auditing techniques and one optimized against both blackbox and whitebox techniques. Through a comprehensive evaluation, we show that, unlike MOs from prior work, our MOs satisfy all three requirements, providing worst-case baselines for developing and evaluating future auditing techniques.

We then test every model, trained on present-day data only, on ED runs forced by SSP1-2.6 and SSP5-8.5 for 2021--2060 in six continental forest regions. With no training, TabPFN-3.5 has the lowest present-day height error of the three models and is within 4% of $DeepED_{LSTM}$ for soil carbon, but is less accurate for AGB, LAI and the carbon fluxes. In 2060, errors are several times larger than at the same sites under present-day forcing, and every model underestimates the biomass that ED gains. Under SSP5-8.5, TabPFN-3.5 is the most accurate model for height, AGB, soil carbon and LAI, with the lowest AGB error in all six regions, although, unlike ED, it predicts no larger biomass gain than under SSP1-2.6. Under SSP1-2.6, no model is best overall.

Title: Beyond Semantic Prompts: Unified Watermarking and Access Control for Text-to-Image Diffusion Models

  • Authors: Fangming Shi, Li Li, Guorui Feng, Xinpeng Zhang
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20470
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20470
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20470]] Beyond Semantic Prompts: Unified Watermarking and Access Control for Text-to-Image Diffusion Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20470)
  • Keywords: robust, watermark, diffusion
  • Abstract: Text-to-image diffusion services require both traceability of generated content and differentiated access to generation capabilities. However, existing watermarking methods commonly expose watermark control through mechanisms beyond the text-prompt interface, while credential-based access control does not inherently enforce distinct output quality levels. We propose a unified framework that enables customizable multi-bit watermark embedding and tiered access control through a single text prompt. A unified prompt represents watermark messages using designated words and associates protocol structures with different access states. The framework adopts a two-stage training strategy. First, watermark payload partitioning represents messages as designated watermark words, while vocabulary extension assigns each protocol word a dedicated token with a learnable input embedding. Guided by a frozen reference model and a pretrained watermark codec, joint adaptation of the U-Net and protocol-token embeddings transfers message-dependent watermark patterns into the generation process. Second, a prompt-structure recognition network controls feature modulation within the variational autoencoder decoder, which is trained to produce clear watermarked images, blurred previews, or black outputs according to the access state. Experiments demonstrate reliable watermark recovery, robustness to common image-processing operations and evaluated model modifications, and consistent performance across diverse generation settings. The controlled decoder also achieves distinct output behaviors under restricted access while closely preserving reference decoding fidelity and watermark recoverability under full access. Our open-source code and model weights will be public after acceptance.

  • Abstract: Harmful fine-tuning can rapidly compromise safety alignment in Large Language Models, underscoring the vulnerability of deployed models to post-release modification. We refine this paradigm by extending the theoretical framework to prioritize plateau regions alongside curvature. Extensive experiments confirm that our approach achieves robust defense against adversarial fine-tuning while largely maintaining general utility and downstream trainability.   • Abstract: Visual token pruning improves the efficiency of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) by removing redundant visual tokens, yet the reliability of existing selection signals has not been systematically examined. First, attention-based importance shifts non-uniformly under perturbations, so magnitude alone cannot distinguish stable evidence from unstable saliency (local instability). Title: VoxSAOR: Semantic-Aware Occupancy Robustification via Aleatoric Uncertainty Modeling

  • Authors: Mengfei Duan, Yuheng Zhang, Guoqiang Zhao, Yuanfan Zheng, Kailun Yang, Zhiyong Li
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.RO, eess.IV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20507
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20507
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20507]] VoxSAOR: Semantic-Aware Occupancy Robustification via Aleatoric Uncertainty Modeling(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20507)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Vision-based 3D occupancy prediction provides a dense semantic representation of scenes for autonomous driving and embodied perception, yet remains vulnerable to occlusion, depth ambiguity, and projection errors that induce spatially varying reliability in voxel features. Existing methods typically map voxel representations to semantic predictions without explicitly modeling such observation-induced feature variations. To address this limitation, we propose VoxSAOR, a semantic-aware occupancy robustification framework that models aleatoric uncertainty through feature-space proxies. It first quantifies Local Semantic Consistency (LSC) to derive a voxel-wise structural stability prior and uses cross-view semantic consensus to filter unreliable semantic correspondences. Within each semantic category, spatially matched stable and ambiguous voxels form stable-to-ambiguous feature transitions, which are modeled by a class-conditional diagonal Gaussian distribution, capturing both feature displacement and dispersion. Guided by these proxy distributions, Aleatoric Regime Ensemble Learning (AREL) trains independently parameterized prediction heads using feature perturbations at progressively increasing scales, with perturbation strength modulated by structural reliability. At inference, proxy modeling and sampling are disabled, and all heads process the same observed features; their output probabilities are then averaged to produce a deterministic consensus prediction without requiring sampling. Experiments across multiple datasets and occupancy backbones demonstrate the effectiveness of VoxSAOR in improving semantic occupancy prediction, long-tail recognition, and predictive calibration. The source code will be made publicly available at this https URL.

  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20537
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20537
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20537]] SmoothShift: Disrupting Visual Adversarial Attacks on LVLMs at Test Time(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20537)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, robust
  • Abstract: Visual adversarial perturbations can substantially impair large vision-language models (LVLMs) despite introducing only subtle input changes. To understand how these small changes cause substantial errors, we investigate their optimization and propagation through the model, revealing two key findings. First, stronger attacks rely on finer iterative optimization, while subtle input perturbations induce representation discrepancies that are amplified layer by layer through the vision encoder and persist in the language model. Second, disrupting the alignment between within-patch input patterns and the model's computation reduces clean-adversarial representation discrepancies and substantially weakens attacks. Together, these findings suggest that adversarial effectiveness depends on both fine-grained perturbation patterns and their coupling with the model's computation. Motivated by these findings, we propose SmoothShift, a lightweight, training-free defense that combines local smoothing and spatial transformations. Smoothing weakens fine-grained perturbation patterns, while spatial transformations disrupt their alignment with the encoder's patch grid. SmoothShift requires no model updates, auxiliary networks, or additional model inference. Experiments cover three LVLM families on four datasets and robustly fine-tuned Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training (CLIP) models on eight classification datasets. Evaluations across eight attack methods and multiple perturbation budgets demonstrate improved robustness while largely preserving clean performance. SmoothShift also provides robustness gains under defense-aware attacks using mixed-gradient BPDA with EOT.

Title: Experience: ENTRAP - An IaC Enterprise Cyber Range for Reproducible Security Assessment and Dataset Generation

  • Abstract: An enterprise breach causes most of its damage after the perimeter is crossed. Defenders, on the other hand, need to study this post-compromise stage, but they cannot rehearse it on a production enterprise network. We develop Enterprise Network Testbed for Reproducible Attack and Defense Practices (ENTRAP), an Infrastructure-as-Code (IaC) cyber range that defines a fully segmented enterprise as version-controlled automation. ENTRAP uses Ansible, KVM/Libvirt, and Cloud-init to provision the environment and reset it to a clean state whenever necessary. The enterprise network includes several entities such as a gateway, an internal Demilitarized Zone (DMZ), DNS, DHCP, a backup server, a web server, and clients. On this network, a user can learn to assemble the entities, launch attacks, and write rules or defensive measures that harden its security. We launch three attacks: a Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS)-backed ransomware campaign, a Layer-2 DHCP starvation and rogue DHCP attack, and perimeter web exploitation. Each attack runs from a clean state, and ENTRAP acts as a reproducible generator of labeled attack datasets. Using the collected dataset, we train a Random Forest classifier for ransomware detection and present its evaluation results. We publicly release the codebase, collected logs, and datasets to facilitate further research.

  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20595]] Invariant-Aware Harness Enforcement to Protect LLM Agents from Silent API Failures(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20595)
  • Keywords: protect
  • Abstract: LLM agents answer questions by calling APIs and trusting the results. An API can fail silently. It ignores part of a request, such as a misnamed filter, yet returns HTTP 200 and a plausible but wrong result. Current fixes, such as checklists, reviewer models and LLM judges, ask the model to catch it. But models often see the problem and answer anyway. We present SilentGuard, which moves the decision from the model to the harness running tool calls. It tests each result against invariants that any correct API must satisfy and withholds values that break one. We find silent failures in 11 of 15 public research APIs, with counts inflated up to 691 times. Current fixes leave over 70% of answers wrong, and agents report 77.4% of counts a judge flags. Across 7 open models, SilentGuard cuts wrong answers on its target faults from 74.0% to 1.7%, with no loss on fault-free tasks. On three frontier models they fall from 70.2% to 0.5%. A warning from the same check leaves 70.1% wrong, so enforcement is the key. On new fault types, the harness alone cuts wrong answers from 61.1% to 24.5%. It avoids wrong answers by abstaining rather than recovering.

Title: Quantum Computing-Based Wasserstein Generative Adversarial Networks for Airfoil Parameterization

  • Authors: Abdur Rahman Ovy, Xiaosong Du
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20602
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20602
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20602]] Quantum Computing-Based Wasserstein Generative Adversarial Networks for Airfoil Parameterization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20602)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Airfoil geometry parameterization is essential in aerodynamic design optimization. Optimization search in the large space suffers from low efficiency and modest feasible region, which could further incur failures to convergence. Generative adversarial networks (GAN), aiming to learn the statistical distribution of existing data, pave a new avenue for shape generation and airfoil parameterization. GAN manages to implicitly reduce design space by generating only smooth, realistic airfoils. Meanwhile, quantum computing has been emergingly exhibiting great potential for achieving superior performance in specific types of engineering problems but has not been introduced to airfoil parameterization. Q-WGAN achieves lower Jensen-Shannon divergence, Fréchet distance, and Wasserstein distance than the classical WGAN, indicating an improved agreement with the reference airfoil distribution. Title: Newton-PINet: A fast physics-informed neural network with Newton linearization for meta-learning nonlinear PDEs

  • Abstract: Scientific machine learning has opened new avenues for solving parameterized partial differential equations (PDEs), enabling models to learn a family of PDEs and generalize to unseen instances. Title: GPromptShield: Elevating Resilience in Graph Prompt Tuning against Adversarial Attacks

  • Authors: Shuhan Song, Ping Li, Ming Dun, Maolei Huang, Huawei Cao, Xiaochun Ye
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20621
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20621
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20621]] GPromptShield: Elevating Resilience in Graph Prompt Tuning against Adversarial Attacks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20621)
  • Keywords: security, defense, attack, robust
  • Abstract: The paradigm of ``pre-training and prompt-tuning", with its effectiveness and lightweight characteristics, has rapidly spread from the language field to the graph field. Several pioneering studies have designed specialized prompt functions for diverse downstream graph tasks based on various graph pre-training strategies. These prompts concentrate on the compatibility between the pre-training pretext and downstream graph tasks, aiming to bridge the gap between them. However, designing prompts blindly to adapt to downstream tasks based on this concept neglects crucial security issues. By conducting covert attacks on downstream graph data, we find that even when the downstream task data closely matches that of the pre-training tasks, it is still feasible to generate highly misleading prompts using simple deceptive techniques. In this paper, we shift the primary focus of graph prompts from compatibility to vulnerability issues in adversarial attack scenarios. We design a highly extensible shield defense system for the prompts, which enhances their robustness from two perspectives: Direct Handling and Indirect Amplification. When downstream graph data contains unreliable biases, the former directly combats invalid information by incorporating hybrid multi-defense prompts to the input graph's feature space, while the latter adopts a training strategy to bypass the invalid components and amplifies valid part. We provide a theoretical derivation that proves their feasibility, indicating that unbiased prompts exist under certain conditions on unreliable data. Extensive experiments across various scenarios of adversarial attacks indicate that the prompts within our defense system exhibit enhanced resilience and superiority. This paper explores a new perspective in graph prompt learning, offering a novel option for robust prompt tuning in downstream tasks.

  • Abstract: Identity unlearning in text-to-image diffusion models is increasingly important for privacy, safety, and ethical deployment, as it aims to remove the model's ability to generate specific individuals. Title: Conditional Diffusion Models for Amortized Bayesian System Identification

  • Authors: Mike Hänseroth, Niklas Wahlström, Thomas B. Schön, Thomas Seel, Jan-Hendrik Ewering
  • Subjects: cs.LG, eess.SY
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20666
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20666
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20666]] Conditional Diffusion Models for Amortized Bayesian System Identification(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20666)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer, generative
  • Abstract: Generative diffusion models rapidly solve Bayesian inverse problems, such as image restoration and inpainting, with a single trained model and without retraining for each new observation or task. Bayesian system identification, which typically involves inferring the hidden states and parameters of a dynamical system from measurements, poses a closely related inverse problem. Yet it is often addressed with computationally heavy Monte Carlo methods that must be rerun for every new measurement record. Moreover, these methods are restricted to a single inference task, and their cost grows with the state dimension and the record length. To alleviate these restrictions, we interpret Bayesian system identification in hiddem Markov models (HMMs) as a general Bayesian inverse problem, taking advantage of conditional diffusion models. Specifically, a transformer-based diffusion model is trained once on short segments of simulated trajectories to approximate the joint posterior of states and parameters. Through an attention mask induced by the Markov property and a relative time encoding, it can then be deployed on any new measurement record of custom length. At inference time, the same model can flexibly and rapidly be conditioned for joint state and parameter inference, state smoothing, parameter inference, or reconstructing missing measurements, without retraining. We demonstrate these capabilities on four benchmark systems and examine how the length of the training segments affects state and parameter inference. On records five times longer than the training segments, the model maintains its state accuracy, whereas comparison methods either show a more than tenfold error increase or require ten times as much sampling time.

  • Authors: Zirui Pang, Zhijie Deng, Yuancheng Wang, Qihan Yu, Xin Li, Jiaheng Wei, Zixin Zhong
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20674
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20674

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: LLM unlearning aims to erase targeted knowledge from large language models while preserving their utility. However, many existing methods suppress target knowledge without explicitly teaching the model how to respond to queries about it. We propose LEAF-OPD (LEArning to Forget from Privileged Contexts via On-Policy Distillation), in which a frozen teacher provides token-level supervision on student-generated responses. On forget data, the teacher uses privileged contexts (instructions not to reveal knowledge about forget targets) to guide forgetting. On retain data, the teacher provides supervision without privileged context to preserve model utility. The student operates without privileged context in both cases, learning how to respond when encountering knowledge that should be forgotten. A key challenge is that the teacher often provides effective supervision only at the first few tokens of student trajectories. We introduce Supervision Signal (SS) to measure corrective supervision over a specified token interval, evaluating the entire trajectory (SS1) and its second half (SS0.5) to assess overall strength and late-stage persistence. When supervision is weak, lightweight SFT strengthens the teacher's context-conditioned refusal behavior, substantially increasing both scores. We evaluate LEAF-OPD on three widely used benchmarks: TOFU, RWKU, and MUSE-News. On TOFU, LEAF-OPD achieves the best Forget Quality, Model Utility, and Forget ROUGE-L while maintaining competitive retain performance. On RWKU, it outperforms the second-best method by 34% in Forget Quality while maintaining competitive Retain Quality. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of privileged-context supervision with on-policy token-level distillation for LLM unlearning.

Title: SpeCal: Spectral Calibration for Unsupervised RLVR

  • Authors: Kai Tang, Xing Zheng, Haobo Wang, Guangcheng Zhu, Shenzhi Yang, Zhengqing Zang, Yingfan Ma, Cheng Peng, Bowen Song, Weiqiang Wang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20691
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20691
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20691]] SpeCal: Spectral Calibration for Unsupervised RLVR(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20691)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has substantially improved the reasoning abilities of large language models, but its dependence on ground-truth answers or external verifiers limits its ability to exploit abundant unlabeled reasoning data. Unsupervised RLVR alleviates this bottleneck by deriving scalar rewards from the model's own rollouts. However, the reliability of such self-generated supervision varies substantially across rollout groups and individual responses, while existing methods construct or refine their rewards mainly from decoded outcomes and reward statistics. These endpoint signals reveal what answers the model produces, but provide limited information about whether the underlying rollout representations form a reliable structure for learning. Our key observation is that hidden-state spectra offer a complementary view of supervision reliability by characterizing both the organization of a rollout group and the support received by each rollout within that group. Based on this observation, we propose SpeCal, a bi-level spectral calibration framework for unsupervised RLVR. At the group level, SpeCal combines spectral mass with a Gaussian band-pass gate over normalized entropy-based effective rank, favoring rollout groups with substantial representation variation whose effective dimensionality lies in a preferred regime. At the rollout level, the same group effective rank determines how many dominant directions are retained in a leave-one-out spectral subspace formed by the remaining rollouts, within which the conditional support of each held-out rollout is measured. The resulting signals calibrate the original reward-induced advantages at the group and rollout levels without redefining the underlying unlabeled reward.

Title: Fast at the Frontier, Wrong at the Fallback: A Commit-Level Analysis of Threshold Decoding in Diffusion Language Models

  • Authors: Roman Makarov, Zhe Xu, Jian Kang
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20693
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20693
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20693]] Fast at the Frontier, Wrong at the Fallback: A Commit-Level Analysis of Threshold Decoding in Diffusion Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20693)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion language models (DLMs) can write several tokens per forward pass. Open-weight DLMs commit, in any order, every masked position whose confidence passes a threshold. If none does, a fallback commits the most confident one, and the commits are never revised. Wide windows let the decoder commit far ahead of the text, which should speed up decoding but often costs accuracy. It is unclear where the model is confident and which commits cause errors. We trace every commit of four off-the-shelf DLMs on math, code, translation and summarization, and rerun failing answers with one step changed. Most tokens become confident only near the frontier, the leftmost masked position, so committing only there, still several tokens per step, is about as fast as committing anywhere. The accuracy loss comes from committing tokens before the text to their left, and committing one token per step does not reduce it. The worst case is an early end-of-sequence token that cuts the answer short. Fallback commits are 11-35% of commits but start 61-72% of the failures that left-to-right decoding avoids. Changing only the step where such a failure starts repairs it more often than changing a similar earlier step. Committing only at the frontier recovers most of the lost accuracy on all four models. Our code is available at this https URL.

We introduce IndustrialKnowEval (IndKnow), a benchmark for industrial knowledge and engineering question answering. It contains 43,282 samples covering 21 task types across seven industrial sectors, including aerospace, electric power, and metallurgy, organized into four levels of cognitive demand: memory, comprehension, reasoning, and engineering application. Enterprise-based domain specialists and experienced researchers participate in all stages of dataset construction and quality control. We conduct a series of experiments on 18 representative LLMs, with domain experts deeply involved in the evaluation. Building upon IndKnow, we propose the Industrial Cognitive Capability Gap (ICCG) metric to characterize the performance differential between foundational industrial knowledge and higher-order reasoning and application, and we analyze it alongside absolute performance to provide a more complete view of model capabilities. We anticipate that IndustrialKnowEval will support the benchmarking of LLMs in industrial scenarios and promote the development of robust industrial intelligence. Code is available at: this https URL

Title: A First Look at System One Decision Models for Malware Detection

  • Authors: Pier Mattia Basciano, Giovanni Farina, Salvatore Monteleone
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20699
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20699
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20699]] A First Look at System One Decision Models for Malware Detection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20699)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Malware detection needs scores calibrated at a fixed false positive rate (FPR) and decisions an analyst can read: feature-based detectors give the first, large language models (LLMs) on decompiled code the second, through generated text of uncalibrated confidence. System One decision models answer a typed question about a text with a probability over its declared answers in one forward pass. We present the first evaluation of these models for Windows Portable Executable malware detection, analyze how they decide, and propose S1Mal, an ensemble on the typed answers and the embedding of CLM-8B. In an isolated virtual machine, Ghidra and capa decompose each binary, and the host renders the records into a canonical textual state, a digest of headers, imports, MITRE ATT&CK capabilities and strings, optionally with decompiled functions, without class shortcuts. S1Mal feeds gradient-boosted trees with the atomic answers and the A2 score of CLM-8B. We evaluate CLM-8B, ten open decision models and two LLMs read through logits on BODMAS temporal splits, SOREL-20M out-of-distribution malware and 14,534 matched benign files. On BODMAS, A0 wastes the static evidence: its area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) spans 0.4025-0.8590 across 13 models. A1 recovers it, raising every model's AUROC by +54.61% on average; A2 lifts CLM-8B by +0.2749 to 0.9943, above a combiner on digest facts (0.9901), and reaches 0.9853-0.9948 on nine open models. S1Mal reaches 0.9946 and detects 93.47% of the malware at a realized FPR of 1.45%, and 59.15% at 0.1% FPR on the test ROC curve, against 46.59% with A2. S1Mal gives analysts calibrated decisions, each with the atomic answers behind it.

Title: Efficient Multi-View Generation with Iterative Global-Local Proxy Attention

  • Authors: Wei Tan, Kunming Luo, Hongyu Yan, Yu Zhang, Zihao Zhang, Xiatian Zhu, Lei Xie
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20703
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20703
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20703]] Efficient Multi-View Generation with Iterative Global-Local Proxy Attention(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20703)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Generating consistent multi-view RGB images and normal maps from a single input image provides complementary appearance and geometric cues for 3D reconstruction. However, maintaining cross-view consistency requires information exchange across viewpoints, and dense cross-view attention becomes computationally expensive as the number of views and spatial resolution increase. In this paper, we propose Global-Local Proxy Attention (GLPA) to reduce cross-view communication costs while preserving fine-grained within-view processing. Specifically, GLPA retains full-resolution interactions within each view and uses compact global proxies for cross-view communication. To further leverage the exchanged information, GLPA incorporates iterative proxy refinement, which rebuilds the proxies from updated features and reuses the same attention parameters for subsequent interactions within a single denoising step. We evaluate GLPA on two structurally different diffusion backbones, FLUX and Stable Diffusion. Experiments on FLUX show that GLPA achieves a $2.65\times$ speedup in attention computation per denoising step over dense attention, while improving reconstruction IoU from 0.6849 to 0.7019 and reducing NVS LPIPS from 0.086 to 0.076. Results on Stable Diffusion further demonstrate the effectiveness of GLPA, with a $1.80\times$ attention speedup and improvements across all five quality metrics.

Title: QASK-Edit: Adaptive Sparse Attention Across Queries and Key Sources for Efficient Video Editing

  • Authors: Hengrui Liu, Wenbo Xu, Ziqi Sheng, Wei lu
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20704
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20704
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20704]] QASK-Edit: Adaptive Sparse Attention Across Queries and Key Sources for Efficient Video Editing(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20704)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) have achieved strong performance in generation based video editing, but suffer from high inference latency due to the long token sequences formed by conditioning inputs and generated videos. Although training-free sparse attention reduces this cost, existing methods typically apply a fixed retention ratio or a single threshold shared across all tokens. In this work, we view video editing as conditional generation and identify a key insight: attention demand varies significantly both across different key sources (e.g., source videos, reference images, audio, text, and generated videos) and across queries within each source. Motivated by this, we propose QASK-Edit (Query-Adaptive Sparsity over Key Sources for Video Editing), a training-free sparse attention framework that allocates computation independently for each query and key source. Specifically, for each query, QASK-Edit considers both the attention mass and approximation risk of each block. It then evaluates candidate routing thresholds against each key source's risk budget, choosing exact computation or approximations to dynamically adjust sparsity for each query. Extensive experiments on three video editing models demonstrate that QASK-Edit achieves up to $1.46\times$ end-to-end speedup over dense attention pipeline while preserving both editing quality and unedited region fidelity. Furthermore, QASK-Edit generalizes directly to Reference-to-Video generation, delivering up to $1.36\times$ speedup while maintaining generation quality.

Title: MSAR: Next-Scale Autoregressive Forecasting for Time Series via Modular Multi-Scale Decoupling

  • Authors: Fanda Fan, Kuiye Ding, Liming Mao, Bingsong Wang, Yao Wang, Xiaorui Wang, Ruijie Jian, Zhipeng Liu, Luqi Gong, Zhenghua Lu, Chunjie Luo, Jianfeng Zhan

  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Time series forecasting underpins critical applications in finance, energy, healthcare, and transportation. We introduce the Modular Scale-wise Autoregressive Framework (MSAR), a model-agnostic design that forecasts progressively across multiple temporal resolutions. MSAR offers three advantages: (1) scale-wise aligned modeling, which disentangles heterogeneous temporal patterns by aligning inputs and outputs at each scale; (2) scale-wise autoregression, where coarse-scale predictions guide finer-scale forecasting through hierarchical information flow; and (3) a modular architecture, enabling seamless integration with diverse backbones such as CNNs, MLPs, and Transformers. Extensive experiments across a broad set of datasets and forecasting models demonstrate that MSAR achieves consistent improvements in both accuracy and inference efficiency, validating the effectiveness of scale-aligned autoregression for multiscale time series forecasting. Title: Calibrate Once, Grow the Vocabulary: Candidate-Independent Scores for Conformal Risk Control in Open-Vocabulary Segmentation

  • Authors: Md Tanveer Hossain Munim, Bijoy Ahmed Saiem, Muhammad Abdullah Adnan
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20715
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20715
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20715]] Calibrate Once, Grow the Vocabulary: Candidate-Independent Scores for Conformal Risk Control in Open-Vocabulary Segmentation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20715)
  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Open-vocabulary segmenters accept class names at inference. Conformal risk control can bound how often their prediction sets miss the true label, but we show that this bound breaks when names are added after calibration. On ADE20K, a threshold calibrated for 10% risk at 150 names reaches up to 15.4% risk at 845 names in released CAT-Seg models. The risk exceeds the target on every calibration split, yet the prediction sets grow far less than the target requires. In SED they instead grow larger than needed. The cause is that pruning, cross-label attention and vocabulary normalisation make each label's score depend on the other requested labels. Scores can move either way, so a threshold recalibrated at a larger vocabulary need not hold at smaller ones. We remove this dependence without training by scoring each label against a fixed reference bank of names. A label's score then stays the same when names are added, so one calibration keeps its guarantee as the vocabulary grows. The guarantee holds whatever names are added, even ones chosen per image after seeing it. On three benchmarks and five segmenters, the converted models meet the target at every vocabulary size we evaluate. For CAT-Seg and SED at alpha=10%, they use at most one more label per pixel than the released models recalibrated at the grown vocabulary. Treating names instead of images as calibration samples also bounds the miss rate of added names. If the annotated names are a random sample of the names in the calibration images, an added name's expected miss rate on those images is at most the target. For harder added names, the excess is at most their gap in mean miss rate. Applied to Grounding DINO, which splits large vocabularies into chunks, the conversion also makes inference about six times faster.

Title: Instant Weight-Space Consolidation for Long-Term Memory in LLMs

  • Authors: Manan Suri, Dinesh Manocha
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20759
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20759

  • Keywords: extraction, generative
  • Abstract: Agentic memory systems give LLMs persistent memory by extracting experience into text and retrieving it at query time, but the model itself does not evolve: however many interactions it has had, it starts each query the same. Extraction errors compound, implicit cross-session patterns that no single fragment states cannot be surfaced, and the construction cost grows with every conversation. Fine-tuning on past interactions does change the weights, but requires backpropagation at serving time and degrades on long histories. We introduce CLAM (Carried Latent Adapter Memory), which combines the strengths of both: a recurrent hypernetwork performs parametric consolidation, compiling a stream of session activations into a low-rank adapter in a single forward pass with no gradient steps and no language-model generation calls, while a complementary episodic buffer supplies verbatim detail at inference. A gated latent state carries knowledge across sessions in fixed size, so the parametric memory accumulates without growing. On LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S across three open models (2-7B parameters), CLAM leads all agentic baselines by 1-45 points and all gradient-based parametric methods by 10-45 points, while requiring zero generative LLM calls, zero gradient steps, and 3-151x less wall-clock time than extraction systems to build its memory. The two channels are complementary: the consolidated adapter adds 12-15 accuracy points when the episodic buffer misses the gold evidence, and still adds 4-5 points even with an oracle buffer.

  • Abstract: Multimodal reinforcement learning (MRL) offers a promising paradigm for autonomous driving by jointly leveraging complementary sensor observations to learn effective decision-making policies. Title: Baryonic Keys and the Inheritance of Crypto-Assets: Revisiting the Tales From the Crypt Protocol

  • Authors: Frédéric Prost
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20782
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20782
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20782]] Baryonic Keys and the Inheritance of Crypto-Assets: Revisiting the Tales From the Crypt Protocol(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20782)
  • Keywords: security, privacy, attack
  • Abstract: The inheritance of crypto-assets faces a structural obstacle: ownership is the knowledge of a key, so the key either disappears with its owner or has to be shared beforehand. The Tales From the Crypt Protocol (TFCP) addressed one part of the problem, the distributed and privacy-preserving acknowledgment of a death by a blockchain. We revisit it with a new primitive. A \emph{baryonic key} is a physical object from which bits are obtained only through a measurement, are never stored digitally, and which exists in exactly $n$ copies, where $n$ is neither zero nor unbounded. We define the primitive, outline a candidate instantiation based on a replicated micro-fractured relief, and state its limits: digitising a copy is slow and tamper-evident, not impossible. We also outline other uses of the primitive, from post-quantum pre-shared keys to common coins for Byzantine agreement. We then give a threat model for TFCP, review the attack models of the literature that apply to it, and show where it resists and where it does not. We propose TFCP-B, in which decrypting the will requires both a secret shared among registrars and the response of a baryonic key, and in which heirs claim their share through hash commitments, without any prior key or wallet. We detail each change from TFCP to TFCP-B, with its rationale and its price. TFCP-B removes the anonymity loss caused by colluding registrars and blocks several false-alarm attacks. It does not remove the shallow-account attack, remains exposed to transaction censorship during the deliberation period, and introduces new assumptions on custodians and reading devices. The security arguments are informal and the physical figures are design estimates.

Title: PhaseCLIP: Phase-Aware Frequency-Domain Adaptation of Vision-Language Models for Few-Shot Remote-Sensing Classification

  • Authors: Mehmet Sergen Catal, Abdurrahman Gumus, Mehmet Zübeyir Ünlü
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20787
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20787
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20787]] PhaseCLIP: Phase-Aware Frequency-Domain Adaptation of Vision-Language Models for Few-Shot Remote-Sensing Classification(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20787)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Remote-sensing (RS) image scene classification aims to assign a semantic category to an overhead image, and in practice must often do so from only a handful of labelled examples, since annotation requires expert interpretation and the resulting labels transfer poorly across sensors and regions. Recent progress has come from adapting vision-language models (VLMs) such as CLIP, through learned prompts or feature adapters. Although effective, these methods construct their representation entirely in the spatial domain. RS scenes are dominated by periodic texture and regular geometry, which the Fourier spectrum describes compactly through an amplitude component, carrying texture and contrast statistics, and a phase component, carrying spatial structure. Existing frequency-domain adaptation of VLMs either exploits amplitude alone or does not separate amplitude from phase, leaving the structural component unused. In this article, we propose PhaseCLIP, which inserts phase-capable spectral modules and a feature-gating module into a frozen CLIP visual encoder and trains only 1.30% of its parameters. Because the amplitude and phase pathways share an identical parameter layout, a single weight set is learned on phase and transferred exactly to amplitude, giving a two-stage scheme that adds no parameters. Experiments on five public RS datasets, EuroSAT, PatternNet, UC Merced, LSRS and RSSCN7, against eight baselines under a matched protocol, show that PhaseCLIP attains 84.28% mean accuracy and is the best method in 23 of 30 dataset-by-shot cells, with phase contributing 1.93 points overall and 4.36 points on the most difficult dataset. Because the modules act on the spectrum of image patches and transformer tokens and leave the text encoder untouched, the approach can be extended to other RS sensors and tasks, and to other domains in which class evidence lies in spatial structure.

  • Abstract: Diffusion-based image restoration achieves strong perceptual quality but typically relies on iterative sampling, leading to slow inference. Existing single-step methods often use diffusion distillation or adversarial training, complicating optimization. To simplify training, we propose FDIR, a framework for training single-pass generative restoration models through regression-style supervised optimization using directly computable objectives. To realize this paradigm, FDIR employs the Fréchet distance to match the feature distributions of restored and real images in frozen representation spaces, providing explicit distribution-level supervision. To better accommodate image restoration, we incorporate local patch features to capture fine-grained restoration discrepancies. Experiments demonstrate strong perceptual restoration quality and efficient training, suggesting a simpler paradigm for generative image restoration.

Title: The Cybersecurity Dataset Observatory: Which Data for AI in Cybersecurity?

  • Authors: Francesco Cerasuolo, Gabriele Mangiacapre, Antonio Pescapè
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20812
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20812
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20812]] The Cybersecurity Dataset Observatory: Which Data for AI in Cybersecurity?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20812)
  • Keywords: security
  • Abstract: AI is increasingly used for cybersecurity tasks such as intrusion and malware detection, in enterprise, industrial, vehicular and mobile networks. Its effectiveness depends on data: models need representative network traffic, both to be trained and to be evaluated realistically. Public datasets of cybersecurity network traffic, however, are scattered across laboratory pages, repositories and aggregators, and are continuously released, corrected and withdrawn: 92 of the 234 datasets we catalog were released in 2023 or later. At this pace, surveys that list them are outdated soon after publication, since they describe the field only as it was when they were written. We present the Cybersecurity dataset observatory, a public and continuously updated catalog of cybersecurity datasets based on network traffic. New datasets are added as they are found or submitted, every record is periodically re-checked against its original source, to which it links rather than hosting the data, and every change is recorded in a public change log. This paper documents the first release: we define a traffic-centered scope and a seven-axis taxonomy, and review 234 datasets (204 original captures and 30 derivatives) across 14 domains. The review shows a field that grows quickly but unevenly, where 91 datasets distribute only processed records, a few laboratories produce a large share of the data, and UAV, wireless and V2X networks have little public evidence, while 5G has few real captures. We then relate the catalog to common uses in AI research, outline a procedure for selecting datasets, and discuss benchmark pitfalls and open gaps.

Title: Tight PAC-Bayes Generalisation Guarantees for Large Language Model Judges

  • Authors: Tom A. Lamb, Philip H. S. Torr, Tim G. J. Rudner
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20818
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20818
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.20818]] Tight PAC-Bayes Generalisation Guarantees for Large Language Model Judges(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20818)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language model (LLM) judges are increasingly used to monitor the outputs of LLM-based AI agents, yet formal guarantees on the reliability of these judges remain largely absent, limiting confidence in their use for safety-critical oversight. We consider certifying such a judge and define its risk over safety classifications of prompt--response pairs drawn from a deployed generator. We apply a PAC-Bayes framework to this setting, refining existing compression-based methodology with the use of tighter bounds and more efficient coding. We show that standard parameter-efficient fine-tuning can yield non-vacuous and even tight certificates for safety judges, for both classification and Brier risk, without holding out data for validation. We introduce LoRA-GT, which shares a single quantisation scale across layers and achieves the tightest certificates with minimal degradation in predictive performance. Across five guardrail models and four benchmarks, we find that certificate tightness is governed more by adaptation complexity than by base model scale. Finally, we show how far quantisation can be pushed before the resulting increase in risk outweighs the bound tightening gained from shorter descriptions. Together, these results provide a practical route toward certifiable scalable oversight and offer insight into how the complexity of safety adaptations shapes generalisation.

All hypotheses, thresholds and decision rules were registered before any model was run, and all deviations are logged.

To address that, we propose ProSE, a training-free, semantic-guided expert skipping framework that probes layer-wise reconstruction error to promote representation consistency compared with default activation. Title: Certified, Not Explained: Exact Token Erasure in Gated Vision State Space Models

  • Authors: Sadia Parveen, Tehseen Zia
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20897
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.20897

  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: Vision models built on Mamba are often explained through their selective step size Delta, on the assumption that a token the scan does not admit cannot influence the prediction. We test that in a concept bottleneck model for lung-nodule malignancy built on a single gated, bidirectional selective state-space block. The answer becomes an interpretability framework: a guarantee, a procedure that checks it, and a pre-registered audit of what it explains. For a fixed gate pattern, masking the input of the causal convolution of the block and giving closed tokens zero weight at readout makes the concept and task logits exactly invariant to the closed tokens. Gating Delta is neither necessary nor sufficient. On LIDC-IDRI, a path-by-path knockout over 15 cross-validated models agrees with this in every run: the largest logit change is exactly 0 whenever the conditions hold and between 4.1 and 6.7 when they do not. The gated model reaches a pooled out-of-fold AUROC of 0.8425 +/- 0.0085, level with an ungated Mamba bottleneck (0.8428). The guarantee belongs to the masked model, not to the gate as an explanation. Post-hoc rankers, which inherit the guarantee on this model, order tokens better than the gate. Gate maps from a model trained on permuted labels are as close to the real maps as two real-label seeds are to each other. Under retraining, the tokens of the gate carry more task information than random tokens and, on shifted crops, more than central ones, but no more than the tokens chosen by hidden attention. We therefore read the gate as a certified support set produced by mask supervision. Splitting the Oracle Impurity Score into own-concept and cross-concept parts reveals differences the aggregate score misses. Partitioning the readout reduced leakage slightly under an earlier recipe, and the final model sits at, rather than below, the level implied by correlations among the true concepts.

Title: MedJev: A System One Vision-Language Decision Model for Fast and Effective Medical Image Understanding

  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Medical image understanding with vision-language models (VLMs) typically relies on autoregressively generating reasoning and answer tokens. Title: MemDoctor: Stage-wise Error Attribution and Repair for MLLM Agent Memory

  • Authors: Ruiyang Jiang, Xinyang Liao, Weikai Huang, Yuqi Niu, Junjie Zhou, Yubin Zheng
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21019
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21019
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21019]] MemDoctor: Stage-wise Error Attribution and Repair for MLLM Agent Memory(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21019)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) now assist users across sessions of images and text, carried by an external memory of what was said and shown. When such an assistant answers wrongly, the failure usually began earlier, in how a record was written, updated or retrieved, and the answer alone does not say where. We formalize multimodal memory as four stages, Write, Update, Retrieve and Answer, and define nine error types across them. A stage-resolved analysis on Mem-Gallery traces most failures to a source stage, and most of those lie before the answer. Injecting known errors against fact-preserving control edits, we then follow each tainted record downstream. Early errors survive to the answer as apparent evidence, and hurt most when no other record holds the fact. A diagnosis that trusts the quoted evidence therefore blames the stage where the error surfaces, not the one where it began. We propose MemDoctor, in which the MLLM that built the memory diagnoses its own failures. It traces each needed fact backward through the four stages, accepts a diagnosis only when the cited evidence is found in memory, and repairs that stage before answering again. On a 4B backbone, MemDoctor improves accuracy on both Mem-Gallery and MemLens, outperforming all evaluated 8B baselines, and the same loop also improves MuRAG and A-Mem.

To address these challenges, we propose ScoutCache, a training-free framework that jointly optimizes denoising, historical KV reading, and cache construction.   • Authors: Hang Xing, Yan Xia, Yongjiang Liu, Siqi Zhang, Xueming Ding
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21085
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21085
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21085]] State-Message Interaction Adapter for Cross-Domain Few-Shot Segmentation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21085)
  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Cross-domain few-shot segmentation adapts natural-image pretrained encoders to medical or industrial domains from a few pixel-annotated support masks. Within frozen DINOv3, intermediate per-head attention messages already carry target-background distinctions that baseline final-layer similarities and predictions do not consistently reflect. We propose the State-Message Interaction Adapter (SMIA) to learn how per-head messages should update each receiving token representation while keeping pretrained attention and FFN parameters frozen. Inserted right after per-head aggregation, SMIA conditions the transformation of each head's aggregated output (message) on each patch's pre-attention feature (state), allowing similar contextual messages to produce distinct or opposing updates depending on the receiving position. Because scarce support masks cover only limited state-message combinations, a shared low-rank bilinear map confines the learned interactions to low-dimensional state, message, and output subspaces. Added before each adapted block's frozen FFN, these updates yield adapted features that are refined by frozen FFNs and deeper blocks for linear readout. With only 0.51M trainable parameters and single-pass, support-free inference, SMIA achieves six-dataset mean mIoUs of 77.40% at 5 shots and 80.99% at 10 shots across 20 medical and industrial tasks, surpassing the strongest prior methods by +3.61 and +3.93 percentage points. In controlled 10-shot comparisons across three benchmarks on the same DINOv3 backbone, SMIA also outperforms parameter-matched AdaptFormer, LoRA, and ParaX with a better precision-recall balance.

  • Abstract: Significant privacy and copyright concerns are raised by the use of publicly available audio recordings to train audio large language models (LLMs), yet effective auditing methods remain limited. In practice, however, audio recordings may be paired with privately generated transcripts, captions, or question-answer pairs that are unavailable to owners of the recordings. This motivates audio-file-level membership inference, which determines whether a recording was used for training without assuming knowledge of its training tasks and annotations. This paper provides the first systematic investigation of this challenge and introduces AudioTraceBench, a controlled benchmark comprising 1,285 audio recordings with explicit fine-tuning membership labels. Consistent improvements over existing baselines are observed, with AUROC reaching 0.60 and increasing further under increased fine-tuning exposure or adaptation capacity. Further experiments were conducted to examine the factors influencing membership signals for audio-file-level detection. These findings establish audio-file-level membership inference as a distinct and practically important auditing challenge, with AudioTrace providing an effective initial approach to this challenge.

Title: Beyond Family Labels: A Taxonomic Ornstein-Uhlenbeck Prior for Avian 3D Shape Recovery

  • Authors: Yunchan Jeon, SooGon Kim, Seungwook Kim, CheolWon Lee, Jongmin Lee
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21102
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21102
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21102]] Beyond Family Labels: A Taxonomic Ornstein-Uhlenbeck Prior for Avian 3D Shape Recovery(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21102)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Recovering 3D pose and shape of birds from single images is bottlenecked by the long tail of avian diversity: out of ~10,000 species, only a few dozen have 3D-labeled training data, and in-the-wild test sets routinely contain species never seen at training. Generalizing across clades is therefore a central problem. The current state of the art, AniMer+, mitigates it with a family-level contrastive loss that conveys only a binary same-family indicator per pair and discards the continuous structure of the species tree. We propose a taxonomic Ornstein-Uhlenbeck prior on the shape code of the AVES parametric bird mesh model: a multivariate Gaussian whose covariance decays exponentially with Linnaean rank distance, with a learnable selection strength and noise scale. The prior generalizes family contrastive into a continuous, generative form, exposing the graded cross- and within-family similarity that a binary indicator cannot, and brings continuous comparative phylogenetics in as a biology-grounded inductive bias for the long tail. Our method improves over AniMer+ on both metrics of the unseen-species CowBird benchmark and on CtrlAVES3D, while remaining comparable on seen-species CUB. A permutation ablation isolates taxonomic structure from generic Gaussian smoothing, and a rank-granularity ablation identifies cross-order distinctions (the structure beyond a family-vs-not indicator) as the dominant source of the unseen-species gain.

Title: How to Explain your Unexplainable Video Anomaly Detector

  • Authors: Giacomom D'Amicantonio, Egor Bondarev
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21131
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21131
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21131]] How to Explain your Unexplainable Video Anomaly Detector(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21131)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Weakly-supervised Video Anomaly Detection (VAD) models assign anomaly scores from high-dimensional features, and their predictions remain unexplained. Saliency maps show where a model looks but not what it relies on, and the descriptions produced by Vision-Language Models (VLMs) are generated by branches that the anomaly score never reads. To address this faithfulness gap, we propose \textbf{Explain The Unexplainable (ETU)}, a classifier-agnostic framework that audits frozen VAD models through counterfactual interventions in their feature space. ETU maps video features to a discrete dependency graph of agents, actions and outcomes ($A_r \to A_a \to A_o$), regenerates the features by graph-conditioned diffusion with selected nodes of the graph masked, and measures the change in the score of a frozen detector; one set of interventions is applied unchanged to every detector. The generator is trained only to reconstruct the input features, so it cannot learn the contrast it is asked to measure, and we validate the audit with experiments that test every alternative explanation of its results, from the cost of regeneration to random deletions of matched size. On UCF-Crime features obtained from a CLIP encoder, we show that substituting the scene semantics results in the largest drop for most detectors, and that removing the outcome chain of the graph ($\Delta_{rel}$) changes the anomaly score of all six audited detectors far more than removing the same number of random nodes, even though the random deletion perturbs the features about as much. We show that this targeted behavior comes from the divergence objective of the graph embedding. Without it, the two interventions become indistinguishable, and with a divergence objective in the generator as well, the score change of the detector follows the displacement of the reconstruction rather than the semantics that were removed.

Title: Beyond Token Stacking: Multi-scale Visual Modulation and Adaptive Fused-feature Calibration for MLLMs

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Current Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have advanced rapidly and follow two main paradigms: one involves concatenating visual and textual tokens for input into Large Language Model (LLM), while the other injects visual knowledge into Feed-Forward Networks (FFNs) of the LLM. However, the former introduces a large number of redundant visual tokens, substantially increasing computational overhead. Moreover, naively injecting visual knowledge into the FFNs could induce activation distribution shifts due to cross-modal semantic discrepancies.
