Experiments show that our approach achieves state-of-the-art performance.

Title: RAGE: Residual-Adaptive Gating for Flow-Based Image Editing

  • Authors: Ruofeng Mei, Xiangyu Xu
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21177
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21177
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21177]] RAGE: Residual-Adaptive Gating for Flow-Based Image Editing(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21177)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Recent inversion-free, flow-based image editing methods evolve the source image toward the target via a differential velocity field. We observe that this velocity field does not explicitly account for which regions should be preserved, leading to unintended drift in non-editing regions. To address this, we formulate source fidelity control as a stochastic optimal control problem in which a selective fidelity term on the editing residual is enforced along the editing trajectory. Solving this problem and approximating its solution yields the Residual Gating Principle: each latent component is pulled back toward the source with a gain that decays exponentially with the local residual magnitude, so that small deviations are suppressed while large, semantically meaningful edits pass through unimpeded. The proposed method, RAGE, requires only one lightweight gated interpolation per sampling step, involves no mask estimation, attention maps, retraining, or architecture-specific modifications, and is applied on top of FlowEdit-based editors. On PIE-Bench, RAGE achieves the best overall rank among 17 methods and substantially improves background preservation on both Stable Diffusion 3 and FLUX backbones while maintaining competitive editing quality.

To safeguard against these threats, migration to new quantum-safe algorithms is underway and expected to be expedited. Though resistant to attacks, the quantum safe algorithms may impact the real-world performance due to their large key lengths. In this work, we analyze the end-to-end performance of the Transport Layer Security (TLS) protocol with the post-quantum (PQ) and hybrid cryptographic algorithms standardized by NIST. We develop a test setup to evaluate the performance of TLS connections between a client and a server under various network conditions. We focus on the impact of the new algorithms on the speed (throughput, connection time) and sustainability (energy, CPU cycles) of TLS 1.3. Our analysis demonstrates that the standardized post-quantum algorithms do not introduce any measurable performance degradations in TLS 1.3, despite their larger key lengths. However, hybrid algorithms introduce noticeable performance degradation, which becomes increasingly pronounced under adverse network conditions. Our analysis suggests that migration to hybrid algorithms should be ``network condition aware'' as poor network conditions can severely degrade performance in TLS 1.3. We provide anonymised link to the code and a web interface to analyse performance of various combination of algorithms.

  • Authors: Hasnain Naeem, Ryan Henry
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.OS, cs.SE

  • Keywords: security, attack
  • Abstract: Time-of-check-to-time-of-use (TOCTOU) races are hard to reproduce and lost races can make vulnerable programs look fixed. Race Specifications compose reusable hold engines, actions, observers and outcome rules. When the declared hold is reached and the attack completes while held, Chronos orders it before the use under blocking and interference assumptions.   • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Modern large language models (LLMs) increasingly support longer contexts and serve more concurrent requests, increasing both sequence lengths and batch sizes. Both trends increase KV-cache size and memory traffic during inference. KV-cache quantization reduces these costs, with recent approaches using codebook-based vector quantization to achieve extremely low bit widths. However, hardware constraints limit codebook size, and most methods reconstruct the cache in higher-precision floating-point formats for attention, limiting the computational benefits of quantization. To address these limitations, we introduce MiSTiQ, a drop-in replacement for codebook-based vector quantizers that combines trellis-coded storage with microscaling (MX) formats for low-precision computation. We combine trellis quantization with beam search to encode the KV cache over large reconstruction spaces without large codebooks. To support MX computation, we derive MX-Hash functions that generate reconstruction values directly in the target MX element format. These functions use the scale-conditioned marginal distribution of block-normalized Gaussian inputs. On Llama3 and Qwen3, MiSTiQ achieves lower WikiText perplexity and higher GSM8K accuracy than the evaluated baselines across several block-scale formats at approximately 1.25 effective bits per element. With NVFP4, it also achieves the highest LongBench average on both models.

Title: Verifiable Random Sampling of Private Data Without a Trusted Single Party

  • Authors: Muhammed Karamuk, Mikołaj Glinka, Ruben Wolff
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21222
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21222
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21222]] Verifiable Random Sampling of Private Data Without a Trusted Single Party(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21222)
  • Keywords: security, fair
  • Abstract: Problem: When a party supports claims about a private dataset by disclosing a sample, auditors cannot tell whether it was drawn at random or "cherry-picked" to hide flaws in the rest of the data. Solution: The data holder commits to the hashes of every item in its dataset and registers the commitment with independent servers. Selection then waits on a decentralized randomness beacon: a network that jointly emits a fresh random value every few seconds, outside the control of the data holder and the auditors. The value emitted two beacon rounds after the commitment picks the sample. Each server returns a signed receipt, so anyone can later check that the revealed sample is exactly the one that value prescribes. Selection from the committed set stays unbiased as long as the beacon's threshold holds and one required server is honest, and a pre-agreed name, checked with the servers, stops repeated or abandoned draws. We call this protocol NoCherry. Applications: Any audit that spot-checks a private corpus: private AI benchmarks and held-out test sets, dataset quality claims, code and security audits, test-fairness review, vulnerability handling, and compliance sampling. Auditors need not trust the data holder to hand over a representative sample of the committed data, since the protocol proves it, and data holders need not expose their entire dataset.

Title: In-Context Estimation and Prediction for Parametric ODEs

  • Authors: Abdullah Ahmed, Yun Yang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, math.NA, math.ST, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21268
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21268
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21268]] In-Context Estimation and Prediction for Parametric ODEs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21268)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Transformers have demonstrated the ability to perform in-context learning, that is, to adapt to new tasks from a few examples without updating their parameters. Existing theory has largely focused on emulating gradient-based algorithms for convex statistical problems or establishing general function approximation guarantees without exploiting the underlying statistical structure. We study in-context parameter estimation and trajectory prediction for parametric ordinary differential equations (ODEs), where estimation is generally nonconvex. Under suitable identifiability and regularity conditions, we construct a transformer that estimates unknown system parameters from noisy trajectory observations through a two-stage approximate maximum likelihood procedure: a coarse search provides an initialization within a locally strongly convex region, followed by gradient descent with local linear contraction. For prediction, we compose this estimator with a neural approximation of the ODE solution map. We further show that, through parameter sharing, a single transformer can perform both parameter estimation and trajectory prediction simultaneously. For these constructions, we establish nonasymptotic guarantees and finite-pretraining oracle inequalities that separate errors due to finite pretraining data, finite context length, and finite-sized transformer approximation. Title: PLECTA: Overlap-aware instance segmentation of dense strand networks from binary masks

  • Authors: Oday Allan, Igor Ostanin
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cond-mat.soft
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21283
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21283

  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Many materials and tissues, including carbon-nanotube sheets, collagen and paper, comprise overlapping strands whose arrangement influences their effective properties. Measuring this geometry from micrographs requires individual strands to be traced through crossings and signal gaps, which merge distinct strands or split one strand into disconnected fragments. We present PLECTA (from Latin plecta, a braid), a deterministic method that segments individual strands using only a binary image whose foreground pixels mark strand centrelines. At each junction, PLECTA selects which strand segments continue into one another while allowing segments to terminate. It formulates this decision as an exact maximum-weight partial-matching problem, with an explicit penalty for unpaired segments and no restriction on the number of segments entering a junction. The output permits crossing pixels to belong to multiple strands. On synthetic scenes, PLECTA resolved strand identities more accurately than three released alternatives at every density tested. The same ranking held on an independently published generator run at its own settings. A separate stage uses the corresponding greyscale micrograph to estimate which strand lies on top at each crossing. The complete workflow is demonstrated on scanning electron micrographs of carbon nanotube sheets, from raw images to individual strand instances with estimated crossing order, without manual annotations for training or as pipeline inputs.

Title: The Price of Not Planning: Parallel Sampling Depth Peaks at the Reconstruction Threshold

  • Authors: Chuling Wen, Weijie Liang, Jian Lu
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21285
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21285
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21285]] The Price of Not Planning: Parallel Sampling Depth Peaks at the Reconstruction Threshold(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21285)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Masked diffusion models sample the tokens of one round independently. Planned decoders first generate a coarse plan, then fill in the tokens in parallel. We ask when a plan saves rounds, how many it saves, and how fine it must be. We study this on broadcast trees: the leaves are the tokens, the internal nodes are the plan, and the edge correlation $\theta$ sets how strongly tokens depend on the plan. Errors are measured by the forward Kullback-Leibler divergence $\varepsilon$, and the lower bounds hold for every deterministic value-adaptive rule, also with learned conditionals. We show that a decoder without the plan needs of order $\min\{n,(n/\varepsilon)^{\alpha}\}$ rounds below the reconstruction threshold, where $\alpha=\log b/(4\log(1/\theta))$ rises to $1/2$ at the threshold, and that the exponent stays below $1/2$ above it (binary channel: $0.02\le\theta\le0.98$). With the plan, $\log_b n+1$ rounds suffice. In the binary model below the threshold, a plan with units of $m$ tokens needs of order $\log_b(n/m)+\min\{m,(n/\varepsilon)^{\alpha}\}$ rounds, so it gives logarithmic depth exactly when $m$ is logarithmic. A plan therefore helps most near the threshold, and only if its units are small. Exact divergence computations in the Gaussian model with up to $2^{20}$ leaves confirm these exponents below the threshold.

Title: Wavelet-Energy-Driven Dynamic Patching for Time Series Forecasting

  • Authors: Syed Hafiz Bin Salim Alsree, Stephen Roberts, Stefan Zohren
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21319
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21319
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21319]] Wavelet-Energy-Driven Dynamic Patching for Time Series Forecasting(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21319)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Patch-based transformers tokenise a time series into fixed-size, uniform windows, which commits the model to a single temporal resolution even though real signals are multi-scale. The discrete wavelet transform (DWT) is a natural alternative because it decomposes a signal across scales. A growing body of work makes the wavelet filter learnable, on the assumption that a more flexible wavelet forecasts better. That assumption has rarely been tested in isolation, because a single reported improvement usually credits three things at once. These are the use of any multi-resolution decomposition, the choice of wavelet and training the filter. We introduce a forecaster that turns a learnable-DWT per-scale energy map into variable-length patches by merging adjacent windows that share a dominant scale. Three findings follow. First, most of the improvement is there before any filter is learned. A frozen wavelet beats a fixed-patch baseline on eight of the nine datasets by 1.5% to 7.8%, and on four of them it recovers all of the gain. Traffic is the one exception, where it is 1.2% worse. Second, learning the filter gives a further improvement on only some datasets. It helps on three of the nine datasets and makes Traffic 2.7% worse. Third, having variable-length patches helps, but exactly where their boundaries fall does not. A fixed-size uniform grid, even with its patch size tuned per task, is worse than our variable-length patches on 27 of 36 settings. Recomputing the boundaries from the learned filter at every step, rather than caching them once, never helps. The improvement in wavelet-based forecasting comes from the multi-resolution structure and the variable-length patches it induces, not from making the wavelet learnable. In practice, start from any valid wavelet, cache the patch boundaries once and treat training the filter as a per-dataset decision rather than a default.

Title: TRIAGE-CPG: A Cross-Model Study of Budgeted Static Malware Triage with Large Language Models

  • Authors: Paul-Andrei Sava, Alexander Küchler, Daniel Kowatsch, Bernhard Grill, Jennifer Pullman, Yanick Fratantonio, Luca Invernizzi
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21331
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21331
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21331]] TRIAGE-CPG: A Cross-Model Study of Budgeted Static Malware Triage with Large Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21331)
  • Keywords: security, large language model
  • Abstract: When triaging unknown binaries, security analysts must decide which samples to clear as benign, flag as malicious, or escalate for deeper analysis. We study how current LLMs behave when static triage is formulated as budgeted evidence acquisition: which functions they inspect and how they trade off benign clearance, malware detection, and escalation. To enable a controlled comparison, we implement TRIAGE-CPG, a fixed pipeline over a code property graph (CPG) of a decompiled binary. An LLM-free heuristic selects seed functions, an agent expands the resulting evidence dossier under a fixed budget, and a reporting stage produces a benign, suspicious, or malicious verdict together with a behavioral report. We evaluate eight closed-source and open-weight models on 1,000 Windows PE binaries, using a 600-sample test split and the same zero-shot protocol for every model. We assess the ternary verdicts under both a strict binary reading and triage balanced accuracy (tBA), which credits malware that is flagged or escalated and benign samples only when cleared. After analyzing about 40 of a mean 1,000 functions per binary, the highest-scoring model reaches a strict balanced accuracy of 0.805 and a tBA of 0.855, flagging 65% of malware, escalating another 12%, and clearing 94% of benign samples. The models implement different triage policies: high-specificity models produce small, malware-rich escalation queues, whereas recall-oriented models escalate or flag much more broadly. Both closed-source and open-weight models appear across this spectrum. Our results identify no universal deployment configuration: model choice itself determines the clear/flag/escalate trade-off under limited static evidence.

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Streaming video generation must sustain quality beyond the training horizon while handling self-generated history and growing cache costs. To address these challenges, we present MovieRoll, a base-to-distilled framework that connects streaming fine-tuning, long-rollout distillation, and long-horizon inference. To retain historical context efficiently, hierarchical KV-cache compression (HieraKV) exploits layer-wise attention patterns, retaining full history in shallow layers and sink--recent history in deeper layers. This reduces cache memory by up to 78% while improving generation quality. Building on this base, cumulative long tuning with distribution matching distillation (CuLT-DMD) holds parameters fixed throughout each long self-rollout and averages local gradients before updating. Each update therefore combines supervision across temporal positions while retaining only one local computation graph, enabling efficient long-rollout training of a few-step generator. To extend generation beyond the training horizon, KV recomputation bounds context for both models, while guidance annealing (AnnealCFG) reduces guidance toward the clean endpoint to stabilize MovieRoll-Base. Complementing these modeling advances, FBCA attention and PS-VAE decoding support efficient training and streaming output. On HeliosBench, MovieRoll-Base remains competitive at 30s after training on videos of up to 15s, and MovieRoll-Distilled achieves the highest total scores among the compared methods across single- and multi-event evaluations up to 60s, combining sustained naturalness with active motion.

Title: Representational Change Under Self-Consuming Training: A Sparse Autoencoder Analysis

  • Authors: Suchit Gupte, Zhongteng Cai, Xueru Zhang, Mohammad Mahdi Khalili
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21348
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21348
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21348]] Representational Change Under Self-Consuming Training: A Sparse Autoencoder Analysis(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21348)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models are increasingly exposed to model-generated text, raising the possibility that future models will be trained recursively on the outputs of their predecessors. Prior work on such self-consuming training loops has largely focused on output quality and downstream performance, leaving their effects on internal representations poorly understood. We study how representations evolve across five generations of self-consumption and whether sparse autoencoders (SAEs) remain reliable under this shift. At each generation, we compare a lineage trained on synthetic data with a matched control trained on real data under the same training settings. We evaluate how well SAEs trained before self-consumption transfer to later generations, measuring reconstruction and sparsity across SAE widths, architectures, and model layers. We then fine-tune the original SAE on each generation and use sparse probing to test whether task-relevant information remains concentrated in a small number of latents or becomes more distributed across the representation. We find that zero-shot SAE performance degrades progressively under self-consumption, with growing reconstruction and sparsity gaps that vary substantially across layers. Fine-tuning largely restores reconstruction, but not feature-level usefulness: task-relevant information remains accessible in the full latent space while becoming less concentrated in individual features. These results show that self-consumption induces representational drift and that reconstruction fidelity alone is insufficient to establish that an adapted SAE remains equally useful for interpretation.

Title: Specificity-Guided Knowledge Diversion for Decomposable Generative Models

  • Authors: Yucheng Xie, Fu Feng, Jing Wang, Xin Geng, Yong Rui
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21352
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21352
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21352]] Specificity-Guided Knowledge Diversion for Decomposable Generative Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21352)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Generative model pre-training typically optimizes a model as a whole without explicitly organizing its parameters for selective reuse. Decomposable pre-training instead learns reusable components for downstream adaptation while retaining strong generative performance. However, existing knowledge diversion methods incur substantial parameter overhead through weight factorization and rely on fixed component assignments to induce specialization, potentially compromising in-distribution generation quality. We therefore propose Specificity-Guided Knowledge Diversion (SGKDiv), which learns how generative knowledge is shared and specialized for effective generation and selective downstream reuse. Together, these designs replace predefined component assignments with adaptively learned and explicitly regulated knowledge specialization. Experiments across diverse model and training settings demonstrate better in-distribution generation than dense and MoE baselines, alongside stronger downstream adaptation than representative PEFT methods across eight datasets.

Title: Federated TD Learning in Heterogeneous Environments with Average Rewards: A Two-timescale Approach with Polyak-Ruppert Averaging

  • Subjects: cs.LG

  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21370]] Federated TD Learning in Heterogeneous Environments with Average Rewards: A Two-timescale Approach with Polyak-Ruppert Averaging(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21370)
  • Keywords: federate
  • Abstract: Federated Reinforcement Learning (FRL) provides a promising way to speedup training in reinforcement learning when one has access to multiple edge devices that can operate in parallel. Recently, it has been shown that even when these edge devices have access to different dynamic models---such as cars or robots with differing system configurations---an optimal convergence rate that has a linear speedup proportional to the number of devices is achievable. However, this result requires that the algorithm's stepsize be chosen in a manner that depends on the unknown model parameters. To achieve our result, we modify the existing one-timescale FRL method to a novel two-timescale variant that additionally incorporates iterate averaging. We remark that this idea also extends to other settings already studied in the literature, such as the homogeneous FRL or the heterogeneous case with a discounted cost function.

Title: Stability-Compensated Threshold Calibration for Selection-Conditioned Risk Control in Large Language Models

  • Authors: Zhiyuan Wang, Yuanchang Ye, Jiahao Yuan, Jinhao Duan, Xiaoshuang Shi, Weijun Xie, Ye Wei, Kaidi Xu
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21384
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21384
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21384]] Stability-Compensated Threshold Calibration for Selection-Conditioned Risk Control in Large Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21384)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models often produce hallucinated answers, motivating selective prediction policies that abstain on uncertain queries. However, heuristic uncertainty thresholds alone do not provide statistical control of errors among accepted predictions. Linear Expectation Constraints (LEC) reduce selection-conditioned error rate (SCER) calibration to a nonpositive expected linear-loss constraint, but the associated loss is non-monotonic in the acceptance threshold. We construct an exact counterexample under i.i.d. sampling demonstrating that LEC's empirical threshold-selection rule can violate the population SCER target. To address this risk-transfer gap, we introduce Stability-Compensated Linear Expectation Control (SC-LEC), which uses the negative linear risk of a theoretical full-data reference to compensate for threshold-selection instability. For local expansion beyond a safe anchor, we derive two finite-sample certificates: a stability-based certificate exploiting rank crossings and a direct certificate using the structure of threshold expansion to reduce conservatism. With a valid population anchor margin, our certificate conditions ensure finite-sample marginal SCER control, and independent margin certification further provides a $1-\delta$ high-probability guarantee for this calibration-averaged control. Experiments on CommonsenseQA, ARC-Challenge, and TriviaQA show useful retention gains beyond the same certified anchor, while revealing conservative behavior in small-margin regimes.

  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: Camera-based heart rate (HR) and respiratory rate (RR) estimates vary in reliability from window to window, and a common remedy is to fuse several extraction methods with weights from reference-free signal quality indicators. Title: Replicating and Extending Weight-Space Programming: Frontier LLMs Compile Programs into Weights and Read Input Layers

  • Authors: Roksana Goworek, Alexander Hickey, Federico Nanni, Martin O'Reilly
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21449
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21449
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21449]] Replicating and Extending Weight-Space Programming: Frontier LLMs Compile Programs into Weights and Read Input Layers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21449)
  • Keywords: interpretability, large language model
  • Abstract: Researchers at MIT recently reported that GPT-6 Astra, a frontier large language model (LLM), can write the weights of small neural networks directly in a single reply, and can read the raw weights of small networks to say what they were trained to do. In a three-day sprint, we replicated and extended these results. Our four findings: (1) Writing weights reproduces. Astra's networks for the game Breakout scored 514, 550 and 307 (versus MIT's 517), and scored just as well on two modified versions of Breakout. Claude Opus 5.5 can also write working networks, but often runs out of output tokens. (2) What is new is translating programs into weights. Earlier models could write a good policy as code a generation before they could write network weights, and the networks the models write behave like compiled programs: two models independently decompiled Astra's Breakout network into Python matching its actions in all tested states. On classifying handwritten digits, Astra's networks scored 50% on average, below a simple classifier (82%). (3) Reading weights is mostly reading the input layer. Astra and Opus 5.5 identify which game a network plays almost perfectly, but on three new games we designed and trained ourselves, Astra recovered where things are on the board, while mostly missing how the game works. (4) Auditing from weights alone is possible but shallow. Without executing code, Astra picked out a small language model poisoned during training in 10 of 10 runs, one poisoned by extra training afterwards in 7 of 10, and a malicious adapter in 23 of 32 cases, but never identified the trigger or malicious behaviour. Open-weight models lag behind. From this early study, we conclude that LLMs can read and write small networks, but that this currently amounts to compiling hand-designed programs and reading the layout of the input layer. It is a promising but early tool for interpretability.

  • Abstract: AI assistants are becoming part of how knowledge workers think and perform, accumulating the prompts, exemplars, memory and interaction histories that encode a worker's professional judgment. Title: Differentiable Exact Learning

  • Authors: Hristo Papazov, Francesco D'Angelo, Nicolas Flammarion

We further show that threshold calibration on finite samples produces substantial false-positive-rate drift, motivating calibration variance reporting as standard practice for auditable provenance.

Title: Embedding Cosine Measures Text Length Before It Measures Homogenization

  • Authors: Fengming Liu
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.CY, cs.SI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21519
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21519
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21519]] Embedding Cosine Measures Text Length Before It Measures Homogenization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21519)
  • Keywords: transformer, generative
  • Abstract: Mean pairwise cosine similarity between transformer embeddings is a standard measure of whether online discourse is converging under generative AI. When the embeddings are mean-pooled outputs of an encoder never trained for sentence similarity, this measure mostly tracks text length. In 6.06 million Reddit comments, an uncentered DeBERTa-v3 measure had suggested that only conservative discourse broke toward homogeneity after late 2022. Truncating the same comments from 10 to 150 words raises their mean cosine by eight times the reported break. A placebo that swaps every comment for a random one of equal length reproduces 81% of the asymmetry, and a fixed-content simulation reproduces 78%. A census of 31 studies locates the risk where anisotropic encoders meet time-series or length-unequal designs. A reverse control on a well-known experiment shows that centering with renormalization can reverse a genuine effect. I propose a low-cost diagnostic protocol. Corrected measures show that the two political camps converged between 2019 and 2022 and stopped converging before ChatGPT. Cross-camp replies engage their targets more closely than same-camp replies.

Title: Post-Quantum Migration of Wi-Fi Key Establishment: A Hybrid ML-KEM Design for OWE and FILS, Validated on OWE with hostapd

  • Authors: Jesús R. Abascal, Ana I. Gómez, Domingo Gómez-Pérez
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.NI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21528
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21528
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21528]] Post-Quantum Migration of Wi-Fi Key Establishment: A Hybrid ML-KEM Design for OWE and FILS, Validated on OWE with hostapd(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21528)
  • Keywords: security, protect
  • Abstract: The key agreements that protect a Wi-Fi connection rely on Diffie-Hellman, which a sufficiently capable quantum computer would break; an adversary can therefore record an association today and decrypt its traffic years later. Key establishment is thus the part of IEEE 802.11 that has to be migrated first. This work targets the two protocols that perform that agreement inside the frames used to set up the link: Opportunistic Wireless Encryption (OWE), which encrypts otherwise open networks, and Fast Initial Link Setup (FILS), designed for fast connection in dense and mobile deployments. We propose replacing their Diffie-Hellman exchange with a hybrid one that runs the classical elliptic-curve agreement alongside ML-KEM (FIPS 203) and derives the master key from both secrets, so the connection stays protected as long as either component remains unbroken. The exchange reuses the frames each protocol already sends and adds no extra round trip; an access point can advertise the hybrid mode alongside classical OWE, and a station configured to require it refuses to associate without a completed post-quantum exchange. Binding the advertised capabilities to the derived key is specified as a design extension and is not part of the prototype. We validate the design with a working implementation on hostapd and wpa_supplicant: both endpoints derive the same key in every measured connection, with the two smaller parameter sets each enlarged association frame fits in a single frame even under a conservative 1500-byte fragmentation threshold, and the added connection time and computation stay below half a millisecond. The measurements are obtained on OWE; the design carries over to FILS, whose frame budget is left for specific measurement. Of the three ML-KEM parameter sets, the intermediate one offers the best balance between security level and size.

  • Abstract: An embodied speaker's expressiveness depends not only on what is said, but also on how the voice sounds and how the body moves. Title: Optimistic Verifiers Get Hacked: Reward Hacking Under an Exact Process Oracle

  • Authors: Arunkumar V, Manoranjan Gandhudi, Gangadharan G. R., Arun Prakash, S. Senthilkumar
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21538
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21538
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21538]] Optimistic Verifiers Get Hacked: Reward Hacking Under an Exact Process Oracle(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21538)
  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards inherits whatever its verifier gets wrong. Outside mathematics and code the verifier is a proxy, a rubric, a learned judge or a hand-written checker, and its distance from the true objective is never measured, so a rising reward cannot be told apart from a hack. We build a setting where the true reward is exactly computable at the process level: executable authorization policies over 124,416 contexts admit an oracle that lists every minimal sufficient reason for every decision. We compile these policies into exact process rewards, degrade the verifier along seven realistic axes (outcome-only, sufficiency, recall-only, extraction noise, coarse taxonomy, stale policy, LLM judge), and train against each while the exact verifier scores every rollout. Four phenotypes appear. Optimistic verifiers are hacked by padding: the sufficiency proxy ends at 0.940 while true faithfulness falls from 0.731 to 0.667 on 96 held-out episodes. Outcome-only verifiers saturate and stop teaching, noise dilutes, and ranking-preserving gaps do no harm. A model-free audit of the verifier, its selection error and gap sign, together with a short saturation pilot, anticipates which phenotype a run will produce. Across nine configurations spanning two frameworks, two model vendors, two adapter sizes, three precisions, two initialisations and two seeds, the phenotypes recur, sufficiency damage is positive in all 10 replication cells and beyond two standard errors in 7, and optimistic gaps remain the steeper branch. The damage magnitude does not replicate, and five pre-registered attempts to explain it are reported as they fell. We release the testbed, the training instrumentation, and 14 real public policies (Cedar, OPA, Gatekeeper) ported to executable oracles.

  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI

  • Abstract: Intrusion detectors must recognize evolving malicious behavior even when confirmed attack labels are scarce or incomplete. TRACE learns from benign system-activity graphs by combining message-passing and topological representations, then assigns anomaly scores in the same projected embedding space optimized by the contrastive objective. Title: Reference Forcing: Long Video Generation with Region-Guided Drift Correction

  • Authors: Sheng Zhao, Haitian Zheng, Weikai Lin, Junjie Zhao, Yuhao Zhu
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21559

  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Long video generation should keep the subject and background consistent while supporting rich, natural motion. However, autoregressive models are often distilled with distribution matching distillation (DMD) over short windows, which does not train them to keep this consistency over long ranges. As a result, the subject and background can drift over time. Methods that manage the key-value (KV) cache retain or retrieve history to reduce drift. Yet this history may itself have drifted. We propose Reference Forcing, which uses the initial frames as an appearance reference. We first build a teacher that uses attention masks to force each region of a new frame to attend to the same region in the initial frames. The teacher finds these regions with off-the-shelf segmentation models. We then distill this behavior into a separate attention path of the student, which needs no external models at inference. We further introduce value correction, which corrects drifted values in the KV cache toward the matching values of drift-free initial frames. Experiments show that Reference Forcing achieves state-of-the-art subject and background consistency among long-video methods on different base models, and its advantage grows with video length. Ablations show both components are needed to gain consistency without losing motion. More visualizations and code are available at this https URL

Title: When Tool Names Are Not Identities: Measuring Provider Misbinding in MCP-Based AI Agents

  • Authors: Zeynab Anbiaee, Sajjad Dadkhah, Ali A. Ghorbani
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21577

  • Keywords: attack, large language model
  • Abstract: Large language model (LLM) applications increasingly act as agents that select and invoke external tools. The Model Context Protocol (MCP) standardizes this interaction by allowing clients to compose tools advertised by multiple servers. However, tool metadata used for discovery and selection is not, by itself, necessarily bound to a verified provider identity. This paper studies this gap as tool-provider misbinding: a failure in which a request intended for a legitimate provider is instead resolved to an attacker-controlled tool. The attacker does not compromise the host, LLM, legitimate server, or network channel; it only operates another MCP server that advertises tools with chosen metadata. We formulate tool provider misbinding as an attacker-controlled tool-selection optimization problem and evaluate it in a controlled MCP environment across five LLM-based clients. Different models expose the same provider-binding weakness differently, including attacker-controlled provider selection, clarification requests, and unstable or inconsistent provider resolution.

Title: The Provenance-Privacy Paradox: Can Private Cloud Compute (PCC) Prove That Apple Reference Images Are Not Used for Learning?

  • Authors: Mohammad I. Husain, Viet Nguyen
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21590
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21590
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21590]] The Provenance-Privacy Paradox: Can Private Cloud Compute (PCC) Prove That Apple Reference Images Are Not Used for Learning?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21590)
  • Keywords: secure, privacy
  • Abstract: Apple Reference Image (ARI) combines camera-sensor signatures, Secure Enclave attestation (SEP), cryptographic timestamp bounds, Private Cloud Compute (PCC), and a final post-quantum signature to establish photographic provenance. PCC is central to this process because it validates the secure digital negative and develops authenticated raw sensor measurements into a reference JPEG. Although the secure digital negative is encrypted during transmission, the authorized PCC workload must process the raw pixels in plaintext. This requirement creates a provenance-privacy paradox: cloud processing strengthens the provenance guarantee while giving an Apple-authorized, machine-learning-capable workload access to valuable, hardware-authenticated raw imagery. We argue that workload attestation and transparency-log inclusion verify software identity and authorization but do not, by themselves, prove that an authorized workload cannot learn from or retain information derived from the photograph. We implement this counterexample in a Python- and Docker-based experimental testbed that models relevant ARI and PCC functions. Across 27 public-domain photographs, a baseline workload and a learning-enabled workload both passed the same verification checks and returned byte-for-byte identical JPEGs. However, the learning-enabled workload retained a first-layer gradient of approximately 48 KB from which its 64 x 64 input was reconstructed with a maximum absolute per-pixel error of 6 x 10^-8. The additional processing overhead was small relative to image decoding and processing. These results do not demonstrate that Apple currently trains on ARI photographs or reproduce production PCC, but identify non-learning and non-retention as independently verifiable assurance requirements.

Title: FaceJev: Direct Visual Decisions for Face Forgery Detection

Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) can recognize facial parts and assess their visual plausibility. Existing MLLM-based methods typically express these judgments through textual explanations, but seldom treat individual part decisions as explicit learning targets. We present FaceJev, an MLLM-based method for face forgery detection through direct visual decisions. Following the Jev formulation, FaceJev answers eight independent questions about facial parts using a shared image encoding and reads anomaly probabilities from the native answer logits. Title: Latent Lattice Language Models

  • Authors: Shivam Duggal, Zongze Wu, William T. Freeman, Antonio Torralba, Richard Zhang, Eli Shechtman
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21608
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.21608
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.21608]] Latent Lattice Language Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21608)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Language models typically increase per-token computation through deeper or looped layers and latent reasoning steps, while richer residual connections improve information flow across layers. We instead increase computational capacity: the number of active computational states a model maintains. We introduce the Lattice of Latents, a set of per-layer horizontal latent streams that run recurrently along time, alongside the vertical residual stream that flows through depth. Like hyper-connections, mHC, and attention or value residuals, the Lattice enhances the residual stream, but rather than adding or rerouting residual pathways through depth, it provides companion computational states that persist across tokens. Unlike hybrid Transformer-SSM architectures, whose recurrent states primarily serve as internal memory for sequence mixing, these latents directly condition the vertical stream's attention, KV-cache writes, MLP computation, and decoding. On Transformers trained on 20B FineWeb-Edu tokens, a 406M-parameter Lattice model lowers validation perplexity from 15.41 to 14.40 over a parameter-matched Transformer. These gains extend across architectural variants. The Lattice composes with prior residual-stream enhancements: with hyper-connections and input residuals, it lowers validation perplexity from 15.41 (base Transformer) and 14.22 (Transformer with the same enhancements) to 13.61. On Gated DeltaNet, Mamba-2, Triadic GDN, and GDN-Mamba-2 hybrids, it improves perplexity at every tested native state size, with Lattice-GDN and Lattice-Triadic-GDN variants outperforming pure baselines with 4x larger native states. These results identify persistent per-layer state as a complementary axis for scaling language-model computation.
